  // Restore the saved color scheme preference, or
  // enact the browser preference if "automatic", 
  // without expecting DOM load to have completed.
  //
  // Also restore any saved readingmode and ToC display preferences.
  function initializeReadingPreferences() {
    let saved_theme = localStorage.getItem("ar5iv_theme") || "automatic";
    if (saved_theme === "automatic") {
      if (window.matchMedia("(prefers-color-scheme: dark)").matches) {
        saved_theme = "dark";
      }
    }
    if (saved_theme == "dark") {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-theme", "dark");
    } else {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-theme", "light");
    }

    const tocDisplay = localStorage.getItem('arxiv_html_paper_toc_display');
    if (tocDisplay) {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-toc-display", tocDisplay);
    }
    const readingMode = localStorage.getItem('arxiv_html_paper_reading_mode');
    if (readingMode) {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-reading-mode", readingMode);
    }
  }
  // Run as soon as JS starts, to minimize repainting
  initializeReadingPreferences();
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
  
  /* Reading mode hides the chrome and the empty .arxiv-html-header marker: the pinned JS reads
     the marker's display to know which way to toggle. The marker draws no box. */
  .arxiv-html-header { display: contents; }
  html[data-reading-mode="enabled"] :is(.arxiv-html-site-chrome, .arxiv-html-header, .ds-site-footer) { display: none; }
  /* The paper controls' icons are blocks, so they sit centred (the pinned JS shows the theme
     icon by making its span a block). */
  .arxiv-html-site-chrome .ds-site-header-tools svg { display: block; width: 18px; height: 18px; flex-shrink: 0; fill: currentColor; }
  /* ar5iv dims every image in dark mode and puts it on a white wash, for paper figures;
     arXiv's own render as they are (the logo on the bar, the funder logos on their white
     chips). Important: ar5iv's dark rules are unlayered, as specific as
     :root:not([data-theme]) img:not(.ltx_no_dark_filter). */
  :is(.ds-site-header, .ds-site-footer) img { filter: none !important; }
  .ds-site-header img { background-color: transparent !important; }

  /* Wide (the theme's 1280px): the theme lays the body out as a grid whose full-width top rows
     were the band's and its sticky reader bar's. The band takes the first and scrolls away; the
     header, the paper's bar (no site navigation, the paper's controls), takes the second and
     stays in view, the one sticky bar, with the anchor clearance that requires (DESIGN-POLICIES).
     The controls show their names. */
  @media (min-width: 1280px) {
    html { scroll-padding-top: 80px; }
    .arxiv-html-site-chrome { display: contents; }

    .arxiv-html-site-chrome > .ds-site-header { grid-area: header; position: sticky; top: 0; z-index: 3; }
    .arxiv-html-site-chrome .tool-icon { display: none; }
  }
  /* Narrow: the header scrolls away, and the controls show icons (names kept for screen
     readers; Why HTML? goes). The theme's contents panel is fixed below where its old sticky
     bar was, and hidden only once the reader has closed it, so until then it is an invisible box
     over the header and the paper: it shows only while open, under the chrome, in its place in
     the page. The paper's footer (build logs, reporting instructions) keeps the text's side
     inset; the theme gives it none, so it met the screen edges. */
  @media (max-width: 1279px) {
    .arxiv-html-site-chrome .tool-wide { display: none; }
    .arxiv-html-site-chrome .tool-label { position: absolute; width: 1px; height: 1px; overflow: hidden; clip: rect(0 0 0 0); white-space: nowrap; }
    html:not([data-toc-display="block"]) .ltx_page_navbar { display: none; }
    .ltx_page_navbar { position: absolute; top: auto; }
    .arxiv-html-footer { padding-inline: 1rem; }
  }

 Bachelor’s Thesis
    Principal Bundles and Gauge Theories 
 
 
 Matthijs Vákár
   
  Affiliation:  Student number 3367428
 
  Affiliation:  Universiteit Utrecht
     
 Utrecht, June 21, 2011 

 
  Supervisor: Erik van den Ban  
  
 
 
 Preface 

 
 Principal bundles are of great mathematical importance. It will be argued that, in some sense, they are the best fibre bundles for a given structure group, from which all others can be constructed. For instance, one can use one principal bundle to understand all tensor bundles of a vector bundle or one principal bundle to understand all (Dirac and non-Dirac) spinor bundles. Moreover, they encode the structure of certain nice (i.e. smooth, free, and proper) group actions on a manifold. It is clear that these objects could be studied in their own right, without any physical applications. 
 
 
 On the other hand, they are arguably  the  most important spaces in modern physics. They arise when we want to resolve the issue that we are trying to give an intrinsic description of nature, while all measurements made with respect to the reference frame of some observer are non-intrinsic – one might even argue that they say as much about the observer as about the observed. They are the spaces on which certain modern field theories describing elementary particle interactions, called  gauge theories , are formulated, even if the physicists themselves are not very explicit about it. 
 
 
 Mathematicians often equip principal bundles with some extra geometrical structure called a principal connection. These are the natural generalisations of the affine connections we know from Riemannian geometry. They have been an object of study in pure mathematics ever since Cartan. Following the famous paper by Yang and Mills in 1954, a theory of elementary particle physics was built using precisely this kind of mathematics (without being aware of it). However, as is often the case in physics and mathematics, communication was rather sporadic and it was realised only later that the physicists had been studying precisely what the mathematicians had already worked out years before.  [ 21 ]  
 
 
 It should be clear that the subject of principal bundles with connections, sometimes called gauge theory, is of interest from a mathematical perspective as well as from a physical one. This is also clear from the amount of material on the subject published in both disciplines. However, little has been written that would satisfy both mathematicians and physicists - as far as I am concerned, only  [ 6 ]  comes close to doing this. Although I have no illusions of filling this gap, I will try to give an introduction to the subject from both points of view. Although most results in this thesis are not original - probably with the exception of the category-theoretic ones, especially those in section   1.1   - I presume that the presentation and the derivations are less conventional.
 
 I assume that the reader has some familiarity with manifold geometry and the classical field theories of general relativity and electromagnetism. Naturally, I first discuss the mathematics of the subject and only then proceed to the physical side of the story, of which I unfortunately cover only the tip of the iceberg, restricting myself to a brief introduction to non-second-quantised gauge theories of the Yang-Mills kind. I have tried to keep both accounts as separate as possible, although the chapter on physics rests heavily on the mathematical preliminaries. Almost all results that are proven in the chapter on mathematics have some application in physics, even if this application is not entirely clear from this thesis.

 Key Insights 

 
 While writing this thesis, I have had a few insights that now form the guiding thread of the text. The reader might want to bear these in mind. 
 
  1.  
 
 Mathematical insight: Principal bundles are bundles of frames of their associated bundles. We can formulate this principle as an equivalence of categories between principal    G  G   -bundles and     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -fibre bundles for an effective action    λ  \lambda   . 
  
  2.  
 
 Mathematical insight: It should be possible to construct categories of     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -fibre bundles with appropriate connections. (In the case of     G  ​  L  ​   (   𝕜  k   )    GL(\Bbbk^{k})    these would be affine connections.) Then the equivalence of item 1 should extend to an equivalence of categories between principal bundles with principal connections and these     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -bundles with     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -connections. In the case of     G  ​  L  ​   (   𝕜  k   )    GL(\Bbbk^{k})    it is clear from this thesis that this is true and we should easily be able to prove the general claim in an analogous way. 
  
  3.  
 
 Didactic insight: Using this equivalence, we can view principal bundles with principal connections (which arise in particle physics) as a generalisation of vector bundles with affine connections (which arise in general relativity), the generalising step being the replacement of     G  ​  L  ​   (   𝕜  k   )    GL(\Bbbk^{k})    by an arbitrary Lie group    G  G   . 
  
  4.  
 
 Physical insight: Because of item 1, principal bundles are the natural context for many physical gauge theories. Principal connections naturally arise when we try to give a geometric description of gauge-invariant interactions. Principal bundles with connections may serve as a unifying language in physics. Not only do they provide a mathematically rigorous framework for first-quantised gauge field theories, but they also give geometric interpretations for the physicists’ calculations, helping to create an overview of this vast subject. 
  
 
  
 
 
  Notation and Conventions    
 
 
 Throughout the text, maps and actions will be assumed to be smooth, i.e.     C  ∞   C^{\infty}   , unless stated otherwise.
 
 At times, we use the notation     m  ∈  M   m\in M   ,     g  ∈  G   g\in G   ,     s  ∈  S   s\in S   ,     f  ∈  F   f\in F   ,     p  ∈  P   p\in P   ,     v  ∈  V   v\in V   , without further comment. Moreover, principal connection    1  1   -forms are denoted by    ω  \omega    and equivariant maps in      C  ∞   ​   (  P  ,  W  )    C^{\infty}(P,W)    from a principal    G  G   -bundle to a vector space with a    G  G   -action are denoted by    ψ  \psi   . 
 
 
 If     F   ⟶  π   M   F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    is a fibre bundle and     m  ∈  M   m\in M   ,     F  m   F_{m}    denotes the fibre of    π  \pi    over    m  m   :      π   −  1    ​   (  m  )    \pi^{-1}(m)   . Moreover, we use the conventional abuse of notation, sometimes writing    F  F    for a fibre bundle instead of     F   ⟶  π   M   F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}M   . A second abuse of notation is the dot notation for a group action if there is no ambiguity in which action we mean.
 
 A glossary of notation can be found at the end of this thesis. 
 
 
 
  Chapter 1  Fibre Bundles and Connections 

 
  1.1  Categories of Fibre Bundles 

 
 I know from personal experience that the notion of a fibre bundle can be a very confusing one, if it is not introduced properly. This is because there are many different but related variants, and their definitions include many subtleties. Moreover, it is very hard to find a good account of these in the literature. I will therefore begin this thesis with some very strict categorical definitions in this domain, most of which I have had to formulate myself, since I could not find them in the literature  1    1 
               1 
              
              
              
            The book that comes closest is the classic reference  [ 28 ]  by Steenrod.   . 
 
 
 These definitions allow us to interpret the associated bundle construction as defining an equivalence of categories (for an effective action). This result sets the tone for this entire thesis. If one reads between the lines in many books, it seems that some form of this result must be known, although it has not been (as far as I know) stated explicitly. Starting from this abstract point of view, I will gradually specialise to more specific variants of fibre bundles. 
 
 
   Definition     (Fibre bundle) .  
 
 A  fibre bundle with standard fibre    S  S     is the combination of 
 
  1.  
 
 a smooth  2    2 
                       2 
                      
                      
                      
                    Most of this theory can be developed in equal generality for the case of topological spaces, instead of smooth manifolds. However, from now on, we shall assume that we are working with the latter, since the smooth structure is essential to formulate the equations of many physical theories.    manifold    S  S   , called the  fibre , 
  
  2.  
 
 a map of manifolds     F   ⟶  π   M   F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}M   , called the  bundle projection  from the  total space     F  F    onto the  base space     M  M   , 
  
 
 such that for each     m  ∈  M   m\in M   , there exists a  local trivialisation , i.e. a neighbourhood    U  U    of    m  m    in    M  M   , such that       π   −  1    ​   (  U  )    ≅   U  ×  S    \pi^{-1}(U)\cong U\times S    via a fibre-respecting diffeomorphism:                π   −  1    ​   (  U  )    {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{-1}(U)}               U  ×  S   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\times S}                U  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}               U  .   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U.}                        ψ  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}                        π  |     π   −  1    ​   (  U  )     \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi|_{\pi^{-1}(U)}}                    ↺  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circlearrowleft}                       π  1   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}                   
 We define a  map of fibre bundles      π   ⟶  k    π  ′    \pi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle k}}{{\longrightarrow}}\pi^{\prime}    to be a tuple of maps     F   ⟶  k    F  ′    F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle k}}{{\longrightarrow}}F^{\prime}    and     M   ⟶   k  M     M  ′    M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle k_{M}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M^{\prime}    such that 
 
              F  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}               F  ′   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}}                M  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}                M  ′   .   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime}.}                        k  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}                      π  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}                    ↺  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circlearrowleft}                       π  ′   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}}                       k  M   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{M}}           
 
 
 By a  map of fibre bundles over    M  M    , we mean a map     π   ⟶  k    π  ′    \pi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle k}}{{\longrightarrow}}\pi^{\prime}    between fibre bundles for which     M  =   M  ′    M=M^{\prime}   . Note that for some authors     k  M   k_{M}    might still be nontrivial; here I mean the more restrictive case in which      k  M   =   id  M    k_{M}=\mathrm{id}_{M}   . 
 
 
 This gives a category     F  ​  B   FB    of fibre bundles and a category     F  ​   B  M    FB_{M}    of fibre bundles over    M  M   , where composition is defined as one would expect. 
 
 
 
 One often has preferred local trivialisations     (   U  α   ,   ψ  α   )   (U_{\alpha},\psi_{\alpha})    on a fibre bundle. A set of such local trivialisations such that     (   U  α   )   (U_{\alpha})    covers    M  M    is called a  fibre bundle atlas . Then, for each     α  ,  β   \alpha,\beta    there exists a unique function       (    U  α   ∩   U  β    )   ×  S    ⟶   ϕ   α  ​  β     S   (U_{\alpha}\cap U_{\beta})\times S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi_{\alpha\beta}}}{{\longrightarrow}}S   , such that        ψ  α   ∘   ψ  β   −  1     ​   (  m  ,  s  )    =   (  m  ,    ϕ   α  ​  β    ​   (  m  ,  s  )    )    \psi_{\alpha}\circ\psi_{\beta}^{-1}(m,s)=(m,\phi_{\alpha\beta}(m,s))   . Equivalently, we find  transition functions        U  α   ∩   U  β     ⟶   ϕ   α  ​  β      Diff  ​  S    U_{\alpha}\cap U_{\beta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi_{\alpha\beta}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{Diff}\;S   , with       ϕ   α  ​  β    ​   (  m  )   ​   (  s  )    =    ϕ   α  ​  β    ​   (  m  ,  s  )     \phi_{\alpha\beta}(m)(s)=\phi_{\alpha\beta}(m,s)   . Obviously, these maps     (   ϕ   α  ​  β    )   (\phi_{\alpha\beta})    satisfy the  Čech cocycle condition          ϕ   α  ​  β    ​   (  m  )    ⋅   ϕ   β  ​  γ     ​   (  m  )    =    ϕ   α  ​  γ    ​   (  m  )     \phi_{\alpha\beta}(m)\cdot\phi_{\beta\gamma}(m)=\phi_{\alpha\gamma}(m)   . A fibre bundle together with such a fibre bundle atlas is called a fibre bundle with coordinates or, briefly, a  coordinate fibre bundle . 
 
 
  1.1.1  Coordinate     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -Bundles 

 
 Unfortunately, the group     Diff  ​  S   \mathrm{Diff}\;S    is difficult to handle. For instance, we run into trouble when we try to endow it with a smooth structure. It turns out to have a natural structure of an infinite-dimensional manifold (a Fréchet manifold if    S  S    is compact), in which, indeed, the group operations are smooth maps.  [ 22 ]  This would lead to many technicalities. Fortunately, for many important examples of fibre bundles these transition functions only take values in a small part of     Diff  ​  S   \mathrm{Diff}\;S   . To be precise, often, we can find a Lie group    G  G   , together with an action      G  ×  S    ⟶  λ   S   G\times S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda}}{{\longrightarrow}}S   , such that the     ϕ   α  ​  β    \phi_{\alpha\beta}    factor through    λ  \lambda   . That is, we can find maps       U  α   ∩   U  β     ⟶   g   α  ​  β     G   U_{\alpha}\cap U_{\beta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g_{\alpha\beta}}}{{\longrightarrow}}G   , such that      λ  ∘   g   α  ​  β     =   ϕ   α  ​  β     \lambda\circ g_{\alpha\beta}=\phi_{\alpha\beta}   :                U  α   ∩   U  β    {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\alpha}\cap U_{\beta}}               Diff  ​  S   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Diff}\;S}                ↺  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circlearrowleft}                G  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}            .                       ϕ   α  ​  β    \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\alpha\beta}}                       g   α  ​  β    \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{\alpha\beta}}                      λ  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}           
 The special case of an  effective  action    λ  \lambda   , i.e. where we can interpret    λ  \lambda    as an (algebraic) embedding     G   ⟶  λ    Diff  ​  S    G\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{Diff}\;S   , will turn out to be of special importance since the fibre bundles corresponding to these actions can be characterised in a coordinate-free way (by a maximal atlas). This leads to the following definitions. 
 
 
   Definition     (   G  G   -cocycle) .  
 
 Let    M  M    be a manifold and    G  G    a Lie group. We define a    G  G   - cocycle of transition functions  on it to be the combination of 
 
  1.  
 
 an open cover     (   U  α   )   (U_{\alpha})   , 
  
  2.  
 
 a family of smooth mappings       U  α   ∩   U  β     ⟶   g   α  ​  β     G   U_{\alpha}\cap U_{\beta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g_{\alpha\beta}}}{{\longrightarrow}}G   , which satisfies the cocycle condition         g   α  ​  β    ​   (  m  )    ⋅   g   β  ​  γ     ​   (  m  )    =    g   α  ​  γ    ​   (  m  )     g_{\alpha\beta}(m)\cdot g_{\beta\gamma}(m)=g_{\alpha\gamma}(m)   , for all     m  ∈    U  α   ∩   U  β   ∩   U  γ     m\in U_{\alpha}\cap U_{\beta}\cap U_{\gamma}    and       g   α  ​  α    ​   (  m  )    =  e   g_{\alpha\alpha}(m)=e   . 
  
 
 We understand a  map of cocycles  to consist of 
 
  1.  
 
 a map     M   ⟶  h    M  ′    M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h}}{{\longrightarrow}}M^{\prime}   , 
  
  2.  
 
 a tuple of cocycles     (   U  α   ,   g   α  ​  β    )   (U_{\alpha},g_{\alpha\beta})    on    M  M    and     (   U  α  ′   ,   g   α  ​  β   ′   )   (U^{\prime}_{\alpha},g^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta})    on     M  ′   M^{\prime}   , 
  
  3.  
 
 maps       U  β   ∩    h   −  1    ​   (   U  α  ′   )      ⟶   h   α  ​  β     G   U_{\beta}\cap h^{-1}(U^{\prime}_{\alpha})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h_{\alpha\beta}}}{{\longrightarrow}}G   , 
  
 
 such that         h   α  ​  β    ​   (  m  )    ⋅   g   β  ​  γ     ​   (  m  )    =    h   α  ​  γ    ​   (  m  )     h_{\alpha\beta}(m)\cdot g_{\beta\gamma}(m)=h_{\alpha\gamma}(m)   , for     m  ∈    U  γ   ∩   U  β   ∩    h   −  1    ​   (   U  α  ′   )      m\in U_{\gamma}\cap U_{\beta}\cap h^{-1}(U^{\prime}_{\alpha})    and         g   α  ​  β   ′   ​   (   h  ⁡   (  m  )    )    ⋅   h   β  ​  γ     ​   (  m  )    =    h   α  ​  γ    ​   (  m  )     g^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta}(h(m))\cdot h_{\beta\gamma}(m)=h_{\alpha\gamma}(m)   , for     m  ∈    U  γ   ∩    h   −  1    ​   (    U  β  ′   ∩   U  α  ′    )      m\in U_{\gamma}\cap h^{-1}(U^{\prime}_{\beta}\cap U^{\prime}_{\alpha})   .
 
 It is easily seen that the    G  G   -cocycles on smooth manifolds form a category     C  ​   C  G    CC^{G}   , where the composition of     (  h  ,   (   U  α   ,   g   α  ​  β    )   ,   (   U  α  ′   ,   g   α  ​  β   ′   )   ,   h   α  ​  β    )   (h,(U_{\alpha},g_{\alpha\beta}),(U^{\prime}_{\alpha},g^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta}),h_{\alpha\beta})    and     (  i  ,   (   U  α  ′   ,   g   α  ​  β   ′   )   ,   (   U  α  ′′   ,   g   α  ​  β   ′′   )   ,   i   α  ​  β    )   (i,(U^{\prime}_{\alpha},g^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta}),(U^{\prime\prime}_{\alpha},g^{\prime\prime}_{\alpha\beta}),i_{\alpha\beta})    is defined to be
    (   i  ∘  h   ,   (   U  α   ,   g   α  ​  β    )   ,   (   U  α  ′′   ,   g   α  ​  β   ′′   )   ,    (   i  ∘  h   )    α  ​  β    )   (i\circ h,(U_{\alpha},g_{\alpha\beta}),(U^{\prime\prime}_{\alpha},g^{\prime\prime}_{\alpha\beta}),(i\circ h)_{\alpha\beta})   , with        (   i  ∘  h   )    α  ​  γ    ​   (  m  )    :=      i   α  ​  β    ​   (   h  ⁡   (  m  )    )    ⋅   h   β  ​  γ     ​   (  m  )     (i\circ h)_{\alpha\gamma}(m):=i_{\alpha\beta}(h(m))\cdot h_{\beta\gamma}(m)     3    3 
                   3 
                  
                  
                  
                This is immediately seen to be independent of the choice of    β  \beta   .   . 
 
 
 
   Definition     (Coordinate     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -bundle structure) .  
 
 Suppose     F   ⟶  π   M   F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    is a fibre bundle, with standard fibre    S  S   ,    G  G    a Lie group and      G  ×  S    ⟶  λ   S   G\times S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda}}{{\longrightarrow}}S    is a left action of    G  G    on    S  S   . A     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -fibre bundle with coordinates or, briefly, a  coordinate     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -bundle structure  on    π  \pi    for the action    λ  \lambda    consists of 
 
  1.  
 
 a    G  G   -cocycle     (   U  α   ,   g   α  ​  β    )   (U_{\alpha},g_{\alpha\beta})    on    M  M   , 
  
  2.  
 
 a fibre bundle atlas     (   U  α   ,   ψ  α   )   (U_{\alpha},\psi_{\alpha})   , 
  
 
 such that        ψ  α   ∘   ψ  β   −  1     ​   (  m  ,  s  )    =   (  m  ,   λ  ⁡   (    g   α  ​  β    ​   (  m  )    ,  s  )    )    \psi_{\alpha}\circ\psi_{\beta}^{-1}(m,s)=(m,\lambda(g_{\alpha\beta}(m),s))   . A coordinate     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -bundle is also called a coordinate    G  G   -bundle if there is no ambiguity about the action    λ  \lambda   .
 
 We understand a  map of coordinate    G  G   -bundles  to consist of 
 
  1.  
 
 a map of cocycles     (  h  ,   (   U  α   ,   g   α  ​  β    )   ,   (   U  α  ′   ,   g   α  ​  β   ′   )   ,   h   α  ​  β    )   (h,(U_{\alpha},g_{\alpha\beta}),(U^{\prime}_{\alpha},g^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta}),h_{\alpha\beta})   , 
  
  2.  
 
 a map of fibre bundles:              F  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}               F  ′   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}}                M  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}                M  ′   ,   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime},}                        k  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}                      π  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}                       π  ′   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}}                       k  M   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{M}}           
  
 
 such that      k  M   =  h   k_{M}=h    and       ψ  α  ′   ∘  k  ∘    ψ  β   −  1    ​   (  m  ,  s  )     =   (    k  M   ​   (  m  )    ,   λ  ⁡   (    h   α  ​  β    ​   (  m  )    ,  s  )    )    \psi^{\prime}_{\alpha}\circ k\circ\psi_{\beta}^{-1}(m,s)=(k_{M}(m),\lambda(h_{\alpha\beta}(m),s))   .
 
 Again, we can form a category, which we shall call     C  ​  F  ​   B  λ    CFB^{\lambda}   , where the composition is understood to be the simultaneous composition of maps of cocycles and of fibre bundles. 
 
 
 
 Note that, in both categories     C  ​   C  G    CC^{G}    and     C  ​  F  ​   B  λ    CFB^{\lambda}   , the isomorphisms are precisely those maps that are diffeomorphisms on the base space. 
 
 
 One can wonder if it is possible to construct a fibre bundle from an arbitrary     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -cocycle. The (affirmative) answer is given by the well-known fibre bundle construction theorem. Here, I formulate a categorical version of this theorem. 
 
 
   Theorem 1.1.1     (Fibre bundle construction theorem) .  
 
  Let      G  ×  S    ⟶  λ   S   G\times S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda}}{{\longrightarrow}}S    be a smooth group action. We then have an equivalence of categories  
 

  
  
     C  C     G   ⇆      C  ​   U  λ         C  ​   B  λ     C  F  B     λ   .   CC^{G}\mathop{\leftrightarrows}^{CU^{\lambda}}_{CB^{\lambda}}CFB^{\lambda}.    
    
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
  We define the functor     C  ​   U  λ    CU^{\lambda}    as the obvious forgetful functor that sends a coordinate     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -bundle     (  π  ,   U  α   ,   ψ  α   ,   g   α  ​  β    )   (\pi,U_{\alpha},\psi_{\alpha},g_{\alpha\beta})    to the cocycle     (   U  α   ,   g   α  ​  β    )   (U_{\alpha},g_{\alpha\beta})    and a map of coordinate bundles to its map of cocycles. Functoriality holds by definition  4    4 
                     4 
                    
                    
                    
                  Moreover, I defined the composition of maps of cocycles in this way, so that functoriality would hold.    of the composition in     C  ​  F  ​   B  λ    CFB^{\lambda}   .
 
 The definition of the functor     C  ​   B  λ    CB^{\lambda}    is the non-trivial part of the equivalence. We proceed by first defining it on objects. Suppose we are given a cocycle      (   U  α   ,   g   α  ​  β    )   ∈   C  ​   C  G     (U_{\alpha},g_{\alpha\beta})\in CC^{G}    on    M  M   . Write    Σ  \Sigma    for the topological coproduct       ⨆  α    U  α    ×  S   \bigsqcup_{\alpha}U_{\alpha}\times S   . To be explicit, we understand the underlying set to be      ⋃  α     {  α  }   ×   U  α   ×  S    \bigcup_{\alpha}\{\alpha\}\times U_{\alpha}\times S   . On this topological space, we define an equivalence relation:  
 

  
  
         {  α  }   ×   U  α   ×  S   ∋   (  α  ,  m  ,  s  )   ∼   (  β  ,   m  ′   ,   s  ′   )   ∈    {  β  }   ×   U  β   ×  S      iff      (  m  ,  s  )   =   (   m  ′   ,     g   α  ​  β    ​   (  m  )    ⋅   s  ′    )     .   \{\alpha\}\times U_{\alpha}\times S\ni(\alpha,m,s)\sim(\beta,m^{\prime},s^{\prime})\in\{\beta\}\times U_{\beta}\times S\quad\textnormal{iff}\quad(m,s)=(m^{\prime},g_{\alpha\beta}(m)\cdot s^{\prime}).    
    
 
  Note that the fact that this is an equivalence relation follows directly from the cocycle condition. We can divide out this relation and give     F  :=  Σ  /  ∼   F:=\Sigma/\sim    the quotient topology. Note that there exists a natural projection     Σ   ⟶   π  ′    M   \Sigma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi^{\prime}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M   , which we obtain by the fact that we have a projection       U  α   ×  S    ⟶   π  1     U  α    ⟶   i  α    M   U_{\alpha}\times S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}U_{\alpha}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i_{\alpha}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M   , where     π  1   \pi_{1}    is the projection onto     U  α   U_{\alpha}    and     i  α   i_{\alpha}    is the obvious inclusion, for each term in the coproduct. Since points that are related by    ∼  \sim    project along     π  ′   \pi^{\prime}    to the same element, this projection descends to the quotient:     F   ⟶  π   M   F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}M   .  
 
 
  The trivialisations     (   ψ  α   )   (\psi_{\alpha})    of the bundle are then defined as one would expect:       ψ  α   ​   (   [   α  ,  m  ,  s   ]   )    :=   (  m  ,  s  )    \psi_{\alpha}([\alpha,m,s]):=(m,s)   , if we write      [   α  ,  m  ,  s   ]   ∈  F   [\alpha,m,s]\in F    for the equivalence class of      (  α  ,  m  ,  s  )   ∈  Σ   (\alpha,m,s)\in\Sigma   . This map is well-defined since      (  α  ,  m  ,  s  )   ∼   (  α  ,   m  ′   ,   s  ′   )    (\alpha,m,s)\sim(\alpha,m^{\prime},s^{\prime})    iff      (  m  ,  s  )   =   (   m  ′   ,   s  ′   )    (m,s)=(m^{\prime},s^{\prime})   . It is a homeomorphism since it is an inverse to the restriction of the quotient map.  
 
 
  To see that    F  F    is a Hausdorff space, suppose that      f  ,   f  ′    ∈  F   f,f^{\prime}\in F    are two distinct points. If      π  ⁡   (  f  )    ≠   π  ⁡   (   f  ′   )     \pi(f)\neq\pi(f^{\prime})   , we can separate them in    M  M   , which is Hausdorff, and take the inverse image under    π  \pi    to obtain distinct neighbourhoods in    F  F   . Otherwise, we can separate    f  f    and     f  ′   f^{\prime}    in one chart     ψ  α   \psi_{\alpha}   , since we know    S  S    to be Hausdorff. To see that    F  F    is second countable, note that    M  M    is so. Therefore, we can find a countable refinement     (   U  i  ′   )   (U^{\prime}_{i})    of     (   U  α   )   (U_{\alpha})   . Then, each     U  i  ′   U^{\prime}_{i}    lies in the domain of a local trivialisation     ψ   α  i    \psi_{\alpha_{i}}   . Since    S  S    is second countable, we find a countable basis     (   V  j   )   (V_{j})    for its topology. We conclude that      ∪   i  ,  j     (   ψ   α  i    −  1     (   U  i  ′   ×   V  j   )   )    \cup_{i,j}(\psi_{\alpha_{i}}^{-1}(U^{\prime}_{i}\times V_{j}))    is a basis for the topology of    F  F   . It is clearly countable.  
 
 
  We conclude that we have constructed a topological fibre bundle     F   ⟶  π   M   F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}M   . We want to show that    π  \pi    has structure group     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   . For this, suppose that       U  α   ∩   U  β    ≠  ∅   U_{\alpha}\cap U_{\beta}\neq\emptyset   . Then,     m  ∈    U  α   ∩   U  β     m\in U_{\alpha}\cap U_{\beta}    implies that       ψ  β   −  1    ​   (  m  ,  s  )    ∈    π   −  1    ​   (    U  α   ∩   U  β    )    =     π   −  1    ​   (   U  α   )    ∩    π   −  1    ​   (   U  β   )      \psi_{\beta}^{-1}(m,s)\in\pi^{-1}(U_{\alpha}\cap U_{\beta})=\pi^{-1}(U_{\alpha})\cap\pi^{-1}(U_{\beta})   . Since       ψ  β   −  1    ​   (  m  ,  s  )    ∈    π   −  1    ​   (   U  α   )     \psi_{\beta}^{-1}(m,s)\in\pi^{-1}(U_{\alpha})   , we can write it as     [   α  ,   m  ′   ,   s  ′    ]   [\alpha,m^{\prime},s^{\prime}]    for some       m  ′   ∈   U  α    ,    s  ′   ∈  S    m^{\prime}\in U_{\alpha},\;s^{\prime}\in S   . Then, however,     m  =   m  ′    m=m^{\prime}    and      s  ′   =     g   α  ​  β    ​   (  m  )    ⋅  s    s^{\prime}=g_{\alpha\beta}(m)\cdot s   , by definition of    ∼  \sim   . We conclude that       ψ  β   −  1    ​   (  m  ,  s  )    =   [   α  ,  m  ,     g   α  ​  β    ​   (  m  )    ⋅  s    ]    \psi_{\beta}^{-1}(m,s)=[\alpha,m,g_{\alpha\beta}(m)\cdot s]    and therefore        ψ  α   ∘   ψ  β   −  1     ​   (  m  ,  s  )    =   (  m  ,     g   α  ​  β    ​   (  m  )    ⋅  s   )    \psi_{\alpha}\circ\psi_{\beta}^{-1}(m,s)=(m,g_{\alpha\beta}(m)\cdot s)   . Now, the smooth structure on    F  F    is obtained from that on    S  S    by the maps     (   ψ  α   )   (\psi_{\alpha})   . This gives a consistent notion of smoothness precisely because the transition functions act by the (smooth) action of a Lie group. Therefore     (  π  ,   U  α   ,   ψ  α   ,   g   α  ​  β    )   (\pi,U_{\alpha},\psi_{\alpha},g_{\alpha\beta})    is a     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -coordinate bundle, which we denote by     C  ​   B  λ   ​   (   U  α   ,   g   α  ​  β    )    CB^{\lambda}(U_{\alpha},g_{\alpha\beta})   .  
 
 
  The next thing to consider is the definition of     C  ​   B  λ    CB^{\lambda}    on arrows. Suppose we are given a map of cocycles     (  h  ,   (   U  α   ,   g   α  ​  β    )   ,   (    U  ~   α   ,    g  ~    α  ​  β    )   ,   h   α  ​  β    )   (h,(U_{\alpha},g_{\alpha\beta}),(\tilde{U}_{\alpha},\tilde{g}_{\alpha\beta}),h_{\alpha\beta})   
in     C  ​   C  G    CC^{G}   . Write     F   ⟶  π   M   F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    and      F  ~    ⟶   π  ~     M  ~    \tilde{F}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{\pi}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tilde{M}    respectively for the fibre bundles corresponding to these cocycles by     C  ​   B  λ    CB^{\lambda}   , as described above, and adopt similar notations     (   ψ  α   )   (\psi_{\alpha})    and     (    ψ  ~   α   )   (\tilde{\psi}_{\alpha})    for their trivialisations. Suppose     f  ∈    π   −  1    ​   (    U  β   ∩    h   −  1    ​   (    U  ~   α   )     )     f\in\pi^{-1}(U_{\beta}\cap h^{-1}(\tilde{U}_{\alpha}))    and     f  =    ψ  β   −  1    ​   (  m  ,  s  )     f=\psi_{\beta}^{-1}(m,s)   . Then we can define the smooth function       π   −  1    ​   (    U  β   ∩    h   −  1    ​   (    U  ~   α   )     )     ⟶   k   α  ​  β      F  ~    \pi^{-1}(U_{\beta}\cap h^{-1}(\tilde{U}_{\alpha}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle k_{\alpha\beta}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tilde{F}    by         ψ  ~   α   ∘   k   α  ​  β     ​   (  f  )    =     ψ  ~   α   ∘   k   α  ​  β    ∘    ψ  β   −  1    ​   (  m  ,  s  )     :=   (   h  ⁡   (  m  )    ,     h   α  ​  β    ​   (  m  )    ⋅  s   )    \tilde{\psi}_{\alpha}\circ k_{\alpha\beta}(f)=\tilde{\psi}_{\alpha}\circ k_{\alpha\beta}\circ\psi_{\beta}^{-1}(m,s):=(h(m),h_{\alpha\beta}(m)\cdot s)   . Note that this defines a smooth function since      ψ  ~   α   \tilde{\psi}_{\alpha}    and     ψ  β   \psi_{\beta}    are diffeomorphisms. (Explicitly,        k   α  ​  β    ​   (  f  )    =     ψ  ~   α   −  1    ​   (   h  ⁡   (  m  )    ,      h   α  ​  β    ​   (  m  )    ⋅   π  S    ​   (    ψ  β   ​   (  f  )    )    )     ,   k_{\alpha\beta}(f)=\tilde{\psi}_{\alpha}^{-1}(h(m),h_{\alpha\beta}(m)\cdot\pi_{S}(\psi_{\beta}(f))),    if     m  =   π  ⁡   (  f  )     m=\pi(f)   .)  
 
 
  Now, the sets      (    π   −  1    ​   (    U  β   ∩    h   −  1    ​   (    U  ~   α   )     )    )    α  ,  β    (\pi^{-1}(U_{\beta}\cap h^{-1}(\tilde{U}_{\alpha})))_{\alpha,\beta}    form an open cover of    F  F   . If we verify that the functions     k   α  ​  β    k_{\alpha\beta}    agree on overlaps of their domains, the sheaf property of smooth functions will give us a unique smooth function     F   ⟶  k    F  ~    F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle k}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tilde{F}   , such that       k  |     π   −  1    ​   (    U  β   ∩    h   −  1    ​   (    U  ~   α   )     )     =   k   α  ​  β     k|_{\pi^{-1}(U_{\beta}\cap h^{-1}(\tilde{U}_{\alpha}))}=k_{\alpha\beta}   . Then  
 

  
  
      k   α  ​  β    ​   (  f  )    \displaystyle k_{\alpha\beta}(f)    
       =     ψ  ~   α   −  1    ​   (   h  ⁡   (  m  )    ,        h   α  ​  β    ​   (  m  )    ⋅   g   β  ​  γ     ​   (  m  )    ⋅   π  S    ​   (    ψ  γ   ​   (  f  )    )    )     \displaystyle=\tilde{\psi}_{\alpha}^{-1}(h(m),h_{\alpha\beta}(m)\cdot g_{\beta\gamma}(m)\cdot\pi_{S}(\psi_{\gamma}(f)))    
    
  
  
  
       =     ψ  ~   α   −  1    ​   (   h  ⁡   (  m  )    ,      h   α  ​  γ    ​   (  m  )    ⋅   π  S    ​   (    ψ  γ   ​   (  f  )    )    )     \displaystyle=\tilde{\psi}_{\alpha}^{-1}(h(m),h_{\alpha\gamma}(m)\cdot\pi_{S}(\psi_{\gamma}(f)))    
    
  
  
  
       =    k   α  ​  γ    ​   (  f  )     \displaystyle=k_{\alpha\gamma}(f)    
    
  
  
  
       =     ψ  ~   δ   −  1    ​   (   h  ⁡   (  m  )    ,         g  ~    δ  ​  α    ​   (   h  ⁡   (  m  )    )    ⋅   h   α  ​  γ     ​   (  m  )    ⋅   π  S    ​   (    ψ  γ   ​   (  f  )    )    )     \displaystyle=\tilde{\psi}_{\delta}^{-1}(h(m),\tilde{g}_{\delta\alpha}(h(m))\cdot h_{\alpha\gamma}(m)\cdot\pi_{S}(\psi_{\gamma}(f)))    
    
  
  
  
       =     ψ  ~   δ   −  1    ​   (   h  ⁡   (  m  )    ,      h   δ  ​  γ    ​   (  m  )    ⋅   π  S    ​   (    ψ  γ   ​   (  f  )    )    )     \displaystyle=\tilde{\psi}_{\delta}^{-1}(h(m),h_{\delta\gamma}(m)\cdot\pi_{S}(\psi_{\gamma}(f)))    
    
  
  
  
        =    k   δ  ​  γ    ​   (  f  )     ,   \displaystyle=k_{\delta\gamma}(f),    
    
 
  if     f  ∈    π   −  1    ​   (    U  γ   ∩   U  β   ∩    h   −  1    ​   (     U  ~   α   ∩    U  ~   δ    )     )    =     π   −  1    ​   (    U  γ   ∩    h   −  1    ​   (    U  ~   δ   )     )    ∩    π   −  1    ​   (    U  β   ∩    h   −  1    ​   (    U  ~   α   )     )      f\in\pi^{-1}(U_{\gamma}\cap U_{\beta}\cap h^{-1}(\tilde{U}_{\alpha}\cap\tilde{U}_{\delta}))=\pi^{-1}(U_{\gamma}\cap h^{-1}(\tilde{U}_{\delta}))\cap\pi^{-1}(U_{\beta}\cap h^{-1}(\tilde{U}_{\alpha}))   ,     m  =   π  ⁡   (  f  )     m=\pi(f)    and     π  S   \pi_{S}    denotes the projection onto    S  S   . (Here we have used the compatibility of      h   α  ​  β    ,   g   α  ​  β     h_{\alpha\beta},\;g_{\alpha\beta}    and      g  ~    α  ​  β    \tilde{g}_{\alpha\beta}   , which holds because we started with a map of cocycles.)
This gives us our smooth map     F   ⟶  k    F  ~    F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle k}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tilde{F}   . Obviously,       π  ~   ∘  k   =   h  ∘  π   =    k  M   ∘  π    \tilde{\pi}\circ k=h\circ\pi=k_{M}\circ\pi    and     h  =   k  M    h=k_{M}   . We conclude that    k  k    gives us a map of coordinate     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -bundles. This will be our definition for     C  ​   B  λ    CB^{\lambda}    on arrows:  
 

  
  
  
       C  ​   B  λ   ​   (  h  ,   (   U  α   ,   g   α  ​  β    )   ,   (    U  ~   α   ,    g  ~    α  ​  β    )   ,   h   α  ​  β    )    :=   (  h  ,   (   U  α   ,   ψ  α   ,   g   α  ​  β    )   ,   (    U  ~   α   ,    ψ  ~   α   ,    g  ~    α  ​  β    )   ,   h   α  ​  β    ,  k  )    .   \displaystyle CB^{\lambda}(h,(U_{\alpha},g_{\alpha\beta}),(\tilde{U}_{\alpha},\tilde{g}_{\alpha\beta}),h_{\alpha\beta}):=(h,(U_{\alpha},\psi_{\alpha},g_{\alpha\beta}),(\tilde{U}_{\alpha},\tilde{\psi}_{\alpha},\tilde{g}_{\alpha\beta}),h_{\alpha\beta},k).    
    
 
  Again, functoriality is a simple consequence of the definition of composition. 
 
 Finally, we check that these two functors indeed define an equivalence of categories. Obviously,        C  ​   U  λ    ∘  C   ​   B  λ    =   id   C  ​   C  G      CU^{\lambda}\circ CB^{\lambda}=\mathrm{id}_{CC^{G}}   . Thus, what remains to verify is that we can find a natural isomorphism        C  ​   B  λ    ∘  C   ​   U  λ     ⟹  ϵ    id   C  ​  F  ​   B  λ      CB^{\lambda}\circ CU^{\lambda}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\epsilon}}{{\Longrightarrow}}\mathrm{id}_{CFB^{\lambda}}   .  
 
 
  Let     ℱ  =   (   U  α   ,   ψ  α   ,   g   α  ​  β    ,   F   ⟶  π   M   )   ∈   C  ​  F  ​   B  λ     \mathcal{F}=(U_{\alpha},\psi_{\alpha},g_{\alpha\beta},F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}M)\in CFB^{\lambda}   . Write
     ℱ  ′   =   (   U  α   ,   ψ  α  ′   ,   g   α  ​  β    ,    F  ′    ⟶   π  ′    M   )    \mathcal{F}^{\prime}=(U_{\alpha},\psi_{\alpha}^{\prime},g_{\alpha\beta},F^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi^{\prime}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M)    and     κ  ′   \kappa^{\prime}    for the images of objects    ℱ  \mathcal{F}    and arrows    κ  \kappa   , respectively, of     C  ​  F  ​   B  λ    CFB^{\lambda}    under the functor       C  ​   B  λ    ∘  C   ​   U  λ    CB^{\lambda}\circ CU^{\lambda}   . We define the components of    ϵ  \epsilon    as follows. Let them be the identity map of cocycles together with the following maps      F  ′    ⟶   δ  ℱ    F   F^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\delta_{\mathcal{F}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}F   , for each    ℱ  \mathcal{F}   . We first define the diffeomorphism      δ   ℱ  α    :=    ψ  α   −  1    ∘   ψ  α  ′    :     π   ′     −  1     ​   (   U  α   )     ⟶       π   −  1    ​   (   U  α   )      \delta_{\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}}:=\psi_{\alpha}^{-1}\circ\psi_{\alpha}^{\prime}:\pi^{\prime-1}(U_{\alpha})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}\pi^{-1}(U_{\alpha})   . Note that for      f  ′   ∈    π   ′     −  1     ​   (    U  α   ∩   U  β    )     f^{\prime}\in\pi^{\prime-1}(U_{\alpha}\cap U_{\beta})   , if      m  ′   =    π  ′   ​   (   f  ′   )     m^{\prime}=\pi^{\prime}(f^{\prime})    and     s  =    π  S   ​   (    ψ  β  ′   ​   (   f  ′   )    )     s=\pi_{S}(\psi_{\beta}^{\prime}(f^{\prime}))   , then  
 

  
  
      δ   ℱ  α    ​   (   f  ′   )    \displaystyle\delta_{\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}}(f^{\prime})    
       =    ψ  α   −  1    ​   (    ψ  α  ′   ​   (   f  ′   )    )     \displaystyle=\psi_{\alpha}^{-1}(\psi_{\alpha}^{\prime}(f^{\prime}))    
    
  
  
  
        =    ψ  α   −  1    ​   (   m  ′   ,     g   α  ​  β    ​   (   m  ′   )    ⋅  s   )     ,   \displaystyle=\psi_{\alpha}^{-1}(m^{\prime},g_{\alpha\beta}(m^{\prime})\cdot s),    
    
 
  while  
 

  
  
      ψ  α   ​   (    δ   ℱ  β    ​   (   f  ′   )    )    \displaystyle\psi_{\alpha}(\delta_{\mathcal{F}_{\beta}}(f^{\prime}))    
       =     ψ  α   ∘   ψ  β   −  1     ​   (   m  ′   ,  s  )     \displaystyle=\psi_{\alpha}\circ\psi_{\beta}^{-1}(m^{\prime},s)    
    
  
  
  
        =   (   m  ′   ,     g   α  ​  β    ​   (   m  ′   )    ⋅  s   )    .   \displaystyle=(m^{\prime},g_{\alpha\beta}(m^{\prime})\cdot s).    
    
 
  Since     ψ  α   \psi_{\alpha}    is injective,       δ   ℱ  α    ​   (   f  ′   )    =    δ   ℱ  β    ​   (   f  ′   )     \delta_{\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}}(f^{\prime})=\delta_{\mathcal{F}_{\beta}}(f^{\prime})    on overlaps.
Note that the open sets     (    π   ′     −  1     ​   (   U  α   )    )   (\pi^{\prime-1}(U_{\alpha}))    cover     F  ′   F^{\prime}   . If we consider     δ   ℱ  α    \delta_{\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}}    as a map       π   ′     −  1     ​   (   U  α   )     ⟶     F   \pi^{\prime-1}(U_{\alpha})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}F   , the sheaf property of smooth maps gives us a unique map      F  ′    ⟶   δ  ℱ    F   F^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\delta_{\mathcal{F}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}F   , such that        δ  ℱ   |     π   ′     −  1     ​   (   U  α   )     =   δ   ℱ  α     \delta_{\mathcal{F}}|_{\pi^{\prime-1}(U_{\alpha})}=\delta_{\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}}   . This map is obviously a bijection and since each     δ   ℱ  α    \delta_{\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}}    is a diffeomorphism, so is     δ  ℱ   \delta_{\mathcal{F}}   . Moreover, we easily see that      π  ∘   δ  ℱ    =   π  ′    \pi\circ\delta_{\mathcal{F}}=\pi^{\prime}   . So     δ  ℱ   \delta_{\mathcal{F}}    is an isomorphism of fibre bundles. We conclude that the combination     ϵ  ℱ   \epsilon_{\mathcal{F}}    of the identity map of cocycles and     δ  ℱ   \delta_{\mathcal{F}}    is an isomorphism of coordinate     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -bundles.  
 
 
  Naturality of    ϵ  \epsilon    is also obvious. Since    ϵ  \epsilon    is the identity on cocycles, we only verify that     δ  ℱ   \delta_{\mathcal{F}}    is natural in    ℱ  \mathcal{F}   . Let     κ  :=   (  h  ,   ℱ  :=   (   U  α   ,   ψ  α   ,   g   α  ​  β    ,   F   ⟶  π   M   )    ,    ℱ  ~   :=   (    U  ~   α   ,    ψ  ~   α   ,    g  ~    α  ​  β    ,    F  ~    ⟶   π  ~     M  ~    )    ,   h   α  ​  β    ,   F   ⟶  k    F  ~    )    \kappa:=(h,\mathcal{F}:=(U_{\alpha},\psi_{\alpha},g_{\alpha\beta},F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}M),\tilde{\mathcal{F}}:=(\tilde{U}_{\alpha},\tilde{\psi}_{\alpha},\tilde{g}_{\alpha\beta},\tilde{F}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{\pi}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tilde{M}),h_{\alpha\beta},F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle k}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tilde{F})    be an arrow in     C  ​  F  ​   B  λ    CFB^{\lambda}   . We check that       δ   ℱ  ~    ∘   k  ′    =   k  ∘   δ  ℱ     \delta_{\tilde{\mathcal{F}}}\circ k^{\prime}=k\circ\delta_{\mathcal{F}}   :                F  ′   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}}                F  ~   ′   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{F}^{\prime}}                F  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}                F  ~   .   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{F}.}                         k  ′   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}}                       δ  ℱ   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\mathcal{F}}}                    ↺  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circlearrowleft}                       δ   ℱ  ~    \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\tilde{\mathcal{F}}}}                      k  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}           
  Let      f  ′   ∈   F  ′    f^{\prime}\in F^{\prime}   , such that      m  ′   =    π  ′   ​   (   f  ′   )    ∈   U  β    m^{\prime}=\pi^{\prime}(f^{\prime})\in U_{\beta}   . Then  
 

  
  
       ψ  ~   α   ∘   δ   ℱ  ~    ∘    k  ′   ​   (   f  ′   )     \displaystyle\tilde{\psi}_{\alpha}\circ\delta_{\tilde{\mathcal{F}}}\circ k^{\prime}(f^{\prime})    
       =     ψ  ~   α   ∘    ψ  ~   γ   −  1    ∘    ψ  ~   γ  ′   ∘   k  ′   ∘    ψ  β   ′     −  1     ​   (   m  ′   ,    π  S   ​   (    ψ  β  ′   ​   (   f  ′   )    )    )      \displaystyle=\tilde{\psi}_{\alpha}\circ\tilde{\psi}_{\gamma}^{-1}\circ\tilde{\psi}_{\gamma}^{\prime}\circ k^{\prime}\circ\psi_{\beta}^{\prime-1}(m^{\prime},\pi_{S}(\psi_{\beta}^{\prime}(f^{\prime})))    
    
  
  
  
       =      ψ  ~   α   ∘    ψ  ~   γ   −  1     ​   (   h  ⁡   (   m  ′   )    ,      h   γ  ​  β    ​   (   m  ′   )    ⋅   π  S    ​   (    ψ  β  ′   ​   (   f  ′   )    )    )     \displaystyle=\tilde{\psi}_{\alpha}\circ\tilde{\psi}_{\gamma}^{-1}(h(m^{\prime}),h_{\gamma\beta}(m^{\prime})\cdot\pi_{S}(\psi_{\beta}^{\prime}(f^{\prime})))    
    
  
  
  
       =   (   h  ⁡   (   m  ′   )    ,         g  ~    α  ​  γ    ​   (   h  ⁡   (   m  ′   )    )    ⋅   h   γ  ​  β     ​   (   m  ′   )    ⋅   π  S    ​   (    ψ  β  ′   ​   (   f  ′   )    )    )    \displaystyle=(h(m^{\prime}),\tilde{g}_{\alpha\gamma}(h(m^{\prime}))\cdot h_{\gamma\beta}(m^{\prime})\cdot\pi_{S}(\psi_{\beta}^{\prime}(f^{\prime})))    
    
  
  
  
       =   (   h  ⁡   (   m  ′   )    ,      h   α  ​  β    ​   (   m  ′   )    ⋅   π  S    ​   (    ψ  β  ′   ​   (   f  ′   )    )    )    \displaystyle=(h(m^{\prime}),h_{\alpha\beta}(m^{\prime})\cdot\pi_{S}(\psi_{\beta}^{\prime}(f^{\prime})))    
    
  
  
  
       =     ψ  ~   α   ∘  k  ∘    ψ  β   −  1    ​   (   m  ′   ,    π  S   ​   (    ψ  β  ′   ​   (   f  ′   )    )    )      \displaystyle=\tilde{\psi}_{\alpha}\circ k\circ\psi_{\beta}^{-1}(m^{\prime},\pi_{S}(\psi_{\beta}^{\prime}(f^{\prime})))    
    
  
  
  
       =     ψ  ~   α   ∘  k  ∘   ψ  β   −  1    ∘    ψ  β  ′   ​   (   f  ′   )      \displaystyle=\tilde{\psi}_{\alpha}\circ k\circ\psi_{\beta}^{-1}\circ\psi_{\beta}^{\prime}(f^{\prime})    
    
  
  
  
        =     ψ  ~   α   ∘  k  ∘    δ  ℱ   ​   (   f  ′   )      .   \displaystyle=\tilde{\psi}_{\alpha}\circ k\circ\delta_{\mathcal{F}}(f^{\prime}).    
    
 
  Since      ψ  ~   α   \tilde{\psi}_{\alpha}    is a diffeomorphism, naturality follows.∎  
 
 
 
 This is a remarkable result. It means that any two categories     C  ​  F  ​   B  λ    CFB^{\lambda}    and     C  ​  F  ​   B  μ    CFB^{\mu}    are equivalent for any two    G  G   -actions    μ  \mu    and    λ  \lambda    on a manifold. We may even be dealing with a situation in which    μ  \mu    and    λ  \lambda    are actions on two different manifolds. In this sense, the cocycle structure actually encodes precisely all relevant information about the coordinate    G  G   -bundle. 
 
 
 For each Lie group    G  G   , there is one particularly easy canonical choice for a smooth    G  G   -action, namely the action    L  L    of    G  G    on itself by left multiplication. We can take the category     C  ​  F  ​   B  L    CFB^{L}    as a representative of the equivalence class of categories equivalent to     C  ​   C  G    CC^{G}   . The objects of this category are known as  coordinate principal    G  G   -bundles . (This may explain the name.) Therefore we also write     C  ​  P  ​   B  G    CPB^{G}    for     C  ​  F  ​   B  L    CFB^{L}   . 
 
 
 
  1.1.2      (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -Bundles 

 
 Since, in modern differential geometry, we would like our theory to be coordinate-independent, we say that some coordinate     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -bundle structures are equivalent and describe the same     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -fibre bundle. Compare this with manifold theory, where a differentiable manifold is defined as a topological space, together with an equivalence class of coordinate atlases. Similarly, we can also define an equivalence relation on the coordinate     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -bundle structures for a given      G  ×  S    ⟶  λ   S   G\times S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda}}{{\longrightarrow}}S   . We say that two coordinate     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -bundle structures     (   U  α   ,   ψ  α   ,   g   α  ​  β    )   (U_{\alpha},\psi_{\alpha},g_{\alpha\beta})    and     (    U  ~   α   ,    ψ  ~   α   ,    g  ~    α  ​  β    )   (\tilde{U}_{\alpha},\tilde{\psi}_{\alpha},\tilde{g}_{\alpha\beta})    for the fibre bundle     F   ⟶  π   M   F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    are  strictly equivalent  (in the terminology of  [ 28 ] ) if there exist smooth maps       U  β   ∩    U  ~   α     ⟶   t   α  ​  β     G   U_{\beta}\cap\tilde{U}_{\alpha}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{\alpha\beta}}}{{\longrightarrow}}G   , such that         ψ  ~   α   ∘   ψ  β   −  1     ​   (  m  ,  s  )    =   (  m  ,     t   α  ​  β    ​   (  m  )    ⋅  s   )    \tilde{\psi}_{\alpha}\circ\psi_{\beta}^{-1}(m,s)=(m,t_{\alpha\beta}(m)\cdot s)   , for all     s  ∈  S   s\in S   . Such an equivalence class is called a     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -fibre bundle. One can compare these with the equivalence classes of atlases that correspond to manifold structures on a topological space. Again, one more often speaks simply of    G  G   -bundles and considers the action    λ  \lambda    to be understood. 
 
 
 These objects are spaces that are ubiquitous in theoretical physics. They are of special importance in studying classical field theories. Therefore, it is useful to define a category     F  ​   B  λ    FB^{\lambda}    which has these     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -fibre bundles as objects. A natural definition for the arrows of this category is that they are equivalence classes of arrows in     C  ​  F  ​   B  λ    CFB^{\lambda}    corresponding to the same map     F   ⟶  k    F  ′    F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle k}}{{\longrightarrow}}F^{\prime}    of fibre bundles. Of course, again, the isomorphisms in     F  ​   B  λ    FB^{\lambda}    are precisely those maps that are diffeomorphisms on the base space. Note that this gives us an obvious quotient functor      C  ​  F  ​   B  λ     ⟶   Q  λ     F  ​   B  λ     CFB^{\lambda}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle Q^{\lambda}}}{{\longrightarrow}}FB^{\lambda}    which ‘forgets’ the particular cocycle structure, i.e. it sends each object and arrow to its equivalence class in the sense described above. Note also that we have the obvious functorial embedding      F  ​   B  λ     ⟶      F  ​  B    FB^{\lambda}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}FB   . Of course, this also gives us a category      P  ​   B  G    :=   F  ​   B  L     PB^{G}:=FB^{L}    of  principal    G  G   -bundles . 
 
 
 
  1.1.3  Principal Bundles 

 
 Note that, since we also have a right    G  G   -action    R  R    on    G  G    that commutes with    L  L   , we can define a global right    G  G   -action    ρ  \rho    on    P  P   , if     P   ⟶   π  ~    M   P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{\pi}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    is a principal    G  G   -bundle. This is the action that looks like right multiplication in a local trivialisation. Obviously, this action, called the  principal right action  restricts to a transitive, free action on each fibre. Therefore,    G  G    acts freely on    P  P   . Moreover, this action on    P  P    is easily seen to be proper. These properties turn out precisely to characterise the fact that a fibre bundle     P   ⟶   π  ~    M   P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{\pi}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    is a principal    G  G   -bundle.  [ 4 ]  
 
 
   Theorem 1.1.2     (Quotient manifold theorem) .  
 
  Let    ρ  \rho    be a right action of a Lie group    G  G    on a manifold    P  P   . Then the following conditions are equivalent:  
 
  1.  
 
  The action is proper and free.  
  
  2.  
 
  The quotient map      P   ⟶   π  ~     P  /  G    P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{\pi}}}{{\longrightarrow}}P/G     is a principal fibre bundle   5    5 
                         5 
                        
                        
                        
                      In particular, since the action is proper and free, the quotient space     P  /  G   P/G    has a unique smooth structure such that the quotient map     P   ⟶   π  ~     P  /  G    P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{\pi}}}{{\longrightarrow}}P/G    is a smooth submersion.     with     ρ  \rho     as a principal right action.  
  
 
 
 
 
 It is easier to see what maps of principal bundles are. Of course, they are a special kind of maps of fibre bundles between principal bundles. The property that precisely captures the fact that they are maps of principal bundles is that they intertwine both principal right actions.  6    6 
                 6 
                
                
                
              It should be noted that, although this is a common definition of a map of principal bundles and it is used in, for example, the standard reference on fibre bundles,  [ 15 ] , the standard reference on principal bundles,  [ 18 ] , uses a broader class of maps. They define a map of principal bundles to be the combination of a map      π  ~    ⟶  k     π  ~   ′    \tilde{\pi}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle k}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tilde{\pi}^{\prime}    of fibre bundles from the principal    G  G   -bundle     π  ~   \tilde{\pi}    to the principal     G  ′   G^{\prime}   -bundle      π  ~   ′   \tilde{\pi}^{\prime}    and a homomorphism     G   ⟶  f    G  ′    G\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\longrightarrow}}G^{\prime}    of groups, such that      k  ∘   ρ  g    =    ρ   f  ⁡   (  g  )    ′   ∘  k    k\circ\rho_{g}=\rho^{\prime}_{f(g)}\circ k   , for all     g  ∈  G   g\in G   . (Here    ρ  \rho    and     ρ  ′   \rho^{\prime}    denote both principal right actions.) Let us denote the category of all principal fibre bundles with this larger collection of maps by     P  ​  B   PB   .
This category has some advantages as it can be used to describe more phenomena, like maps between principal bundles with different structure groups. As a consequence, the restriction of     P  ​  B   PB    to some fixed base space    M  M    on which the arrows are demanded to be the identity,     P  ​   B  M    PB_{M}    has finite products. Indeed, these products arise in Yang-Mills theories.
At this point, however, I see no possibility to adjust the definition of a map of     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -bundles to fit this definition. For instance, I cannot see how the associated bundle functor, which can be defined for a     G  ′   G^{\prime}   -action    λ  \lambda    and a map      (  P  ,  G  )    ⟶   (  k  ,  f  )     (   P  ′   ,   G  ′   )    (P,G)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(k,f)}}{{\longrightarrow}}(P^{\prime},G^{\prime})    to be the obvious map      P  ⁡   [   λ  ∘  f   ]     ⟶   k  ⁡   [  λ  ]       P  ′   ​   [  λ  ]     P[\lambda\circ f]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle k[\lambda]}}{{\longrightarrow}}P^{\prime}[\lambda]   , would extend to an equivalence from this category.    To see this, note that for a map of principal    G  G   -bundles              P  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}               P  ′   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}}                M  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}                M  ′   ,   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime},}                        k  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}                       π  ~   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\pi}}                        π  ~   ′   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\pi}^{\prime}}                       k  M   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{M}}           
 the following holds if we choose corresponding principal    G  G   -bundle atlases     (   U  α   ,   ψ  α   ,   g   α  ​  β    )   (U_{\alpha},\psi_{\alpha},g_{\alpha\beta})    and     (   U  α  ′   ,   ψ  α  ′   ,   g   α  ​  β   ′   )   (U^{\prime}_{\alpha},\psi^{\prime}_{\alpha},g^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta})   : 
 

  
  
      ψ  α  ′   ​   (   k  ⁡   (   ρ  ⁡   (    ψ  β   −  1    ​   (  m  ,   g  1   )    ,   g  2   )    )    )    \displaystyle\psi_{\alpha}^{\prime}(k(\rho(\psi_{\beta}^{-1}(m,g_{1}),g_{2})))    
       =    ψ  α  ′   ∘  k  ∘    ψ  β   −  1    ​   (  m  ,    g  1   ⋅   g  2    )      \displaystyle=\psi_{\alpha}^{\prime}\circ k\circ\psi_{\beta}^{-1}(m,g_{1}\cdot g_{2})    
    
  
  
  
       =   (    k  M   ​   (  m  )    ,     h   α  ​  β    ​   (  m  )    ⋅   (    g  1   ⋅   g  2    )    )    \displaystyle=(k_{M}(m),h_{\alpha\beta}(m)\cdot(g_{1}\cdot g_{2}))    
    
  
  
  
       =   (    k  M   ​   (  m  )    ,    (     h   α  ​  β    ​   (  m  )    ⋅   g  1    )   ⋅   g  2    )    \displaystyle=(k_{M}(m),(h_{\alpha\beta}(m)\cdot g_{1})\cdot g_{2})    
    
  
  
  
       =    ψ  α  ′   ​   (   ρ  ⁡   (    ψ  α   ′     −  1     ​   (    ψ  α  ′   ​   (   k  ⁡   (    ψ  β   −  1    ​   (  m  ,   g  1   )    )    )    )    ,   g  2   )    )     \displaystyle=\psi_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\rho(\psi_{\alpha}^{\prime-1}(\psi_{\alpha}^{\prime}(k(\psi_{\beta}^{-1}(m,g_{1})))),g_{2}))    
    
  
  
  
        =    ψ  α  ′   ​   (   ρ  ⁡   (   k  ⁡   (    ψ  β   −  1    ​   (  m  ,   g  1   )    )    ,   g  2   )    )     ,   \displaystyle=\psi_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\rho(k(\psi_{\beta}^{-1}(m,g_{1})),g_{2})),    
    
 
 so    k  k    intertwines the principal right actions (which I both denoted by    ρ  \rho   ). For the converse statement, note that the above also tells us how we should define     h   α  ​  β    h_{\alpha\beta}    in terms of    k  k   . It is easily checked that     h   α  ​  β    h_{\alpha\beta}    is a map of cocycles if    k  k    intertwines the actions. 
 
 
 
  1.1.4  A Coordinate-Free Equivalence 

 
 Note that, in the case of an effective action      G  ×  S    ⟶  λ   S   G\times S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda}}{{\longrightarrow}}S   , the strict equivalence of     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -bundle structures can also be formulated as the statement that their union is again a     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -bundle structure with respect to the    G  G   -valued transition functions which are uniquely determined since    G  G    acts effectively. Therefore we can associate its maximal element to each equivalence class (given by the total union). We can also lift a map of     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -fibre bundles in a canonical way to a map between the maximal representatives of the equivalence classes. Indeed, define      (  m  ,     h   α  ​  β    ​   (  m  )    ⋅  s   )   :=     ψ  ~   α   ∘  k  ∘    ψ  β   −  1    ​   (  m  ,  s  )      (m,h_{\alpha\beta}(m)\cdot s):=\tilde{\psi}_{\alpha}\circ k\circ\psi_{\beta}^{-1}(m,s)   , if    k  k    is the given fibre bundle map and     (   U  α   ,   ψ  α   )   (U_{\alpha},\psi_{\alpha})    and     (    U  ~   α   ,    ψ  ~   α   )   (\tilde{U}_{\alpha},\tilde{\psi}_{\alpha})    are the trivialisations of the maximal atlases on both    G  G   -bundles. (Note that this defines     h   α  ​  β    h_{\alpha\beta}    uniquely, since    λ  \lambda    is effective.) Therefore, we obtain a canonical section      F  ​   B  λ     ⟶   Σ  λ     C  ​  F  ​   B  λ     FB^{\lambda}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma^{\lambda}}}{{\longrightarrow}}CFB^{\lambda}    of the quotient functor     Q  λ   Q^{\lambda}   . This will lead to a natural coordinate-free treatment of     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -fibre bundles when    λ  \lambda    is effective. 
 
 
 In light of this discussion on a coordinate-free notion of a     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -fibre bundle, it is natural to wonder if we can find an atlas-independent form of theorem   1.1.1  . We can hope to succeed since the construction of     Σ  λ   \Sigma^{\lambda}    is canonical. It turns out that we are at least able to obtain such a form for the equivalence between     C  ​  F  ​   B  λ    CFB^{\lambda}    and     C  ​  F  ​   B  μ    CFB^{\mu}    for     λ  ,  μ   \lambda,\mu    effective    G  G   -actions. That is, we have the following. 
 
 
   Theorem 1.1.3   .  
 
  Let      G  ×  S    ⟶  λ   S   G\times S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda}}{{\longrightarrow}}S    and      G  ×   S  ′     ⟶  μ    S  ′    G\times S^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu}}{{\longrightarrow}}S^{\prime}    be effective actions, i.e.     λ  ,  μ   \lambda,\mu    define (algebraic) embeddings of groups      G  ↪   Diff  ​  S    ,   Diff  ​   S  ′     G\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Diff}\;S,\;\mathrm{Diff}\;S^{\prime}   . We then have an equivalence of categories  
 

  
  
     F  B     μ   ⇆      C   λ  ​  μ         C   μ  ​  λ     F  B     λ   .   FB^{\mu}\mathop{\leftrightarrows}^{C^{\lambda\mu}}_{C^{\mu\lambda}}FB^{\lambda}.    
    
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
  The idea of the proof will be to use the functors of theorem   1.1.1   to construct the equivalence:                C  ​  F  ​   B  μ    {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces CFB^{\mu}}               C  ​   C  G    {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces CC^{G}}               C  ​  F  ​   B  λ    {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces CFB^{\lambda}}                 F  ​   B  μ    {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FB^{\mu}}                F  ​   B  λ    .   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FB^{\lambda}.}                         C  ​   B  μ    \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces CB^{\mu}}                       C  ​   U  μ    \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces CU^{\mu}}                           Q  μ   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q^{\mu}}                       C  ​   U  λ    \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces CU^{\lambda}}                       C  ​   B  λ    \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces CB^{\lambda}}                           Q  λ   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q^{\lambda}}                       C   λ  ​  μ    \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\lambda\mu}}                       C   μ  ​  λ    \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\mu\lambda}}           
  Put differently, it gives a coordinate-independent characterisation of    G  G   -bundles. To realise this, we use the canonical sections     Σ  λ   \Sigma^{\lambda}    and     Σ  μ   \Sigma^{\mu}    for     Q  λ   Q^{\lambda}    and     Q  μ   Q^{\mu}    respectively, to construct the functors     C   λ  ​  μ    C^{\lambda\mu}    and     C   μ  ​  λ    C^{\mu\lambda}    exactly as one would expect. (     Σ  λ   ∘   Q  λ    \Sigma^{\lambda}\circ Q^{\lambda}    extends the atlas to the maximal compatible one. The resulting coordinate     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -fibre bundle is by definition strictly equivalent to the one we started with.)  
 
 
  The proof will rest on the following observation:       C  ​   U  λ    ∘   Σ  λ   ∘   Q  λ   ∘   C  ​   B  λ     ≅   id   C  ​   C  G      CU^{\lambda}\circ\Sigma^{\lambda}\circ Q^{\lambda}\circ CB^{\lambda}\cong\mathrm{id}_{CC^{G}}   . Therefore, we first verify this. Let     𝒞  :=   (   U  α   ,   g   α  ​  β    )    \mathcal{C}:=(U_{\alpha},g_{\alpha\beta})    be a    G  G   -cocycle on    M  M   . Then      C  ​   B  λ   ​   (   U  α   ,   g   α  ​  β    )    =   (  π  ,   U  α   ,   ψ  α   ,   g   α  ​  β    )    CB^{\lambda}(U_{\alpha},g_{\alpha\beta})=(\pi,U_{\alpha},\psi_{\alpha},g_{\alpha\beta})    is the corresponding coordinate     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -bundle. Now,       Σ  λ   ∘   Q  λ   ∘   C  ​   B  λ   ​   (   U  α   ,   g   α  ​  β    )     =   (  π  ,    U  ~   α   ,    ψ  ~   α   ,    g  ~    α  ​  β    )    \Sigma^{\lambda}\circ Q^{\lambda}\circ CB^{\lambda}(U_{\alpha},g_{\alpha\beta})=(\pi,\tilde{U}_{\alpha},\tilde{\psi}_{\alpha},\tilde{g}_{\alpha\beta})    is the compatible maximal coordinate     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -bundle. Therefore, there exist smooth maps       U  β   ∩    U  ~   α     ⟶   t   α  ​  β     G   U_{\beta}\cap\tilde{U}_{\alpha}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{\alpha\beta}}}{{\longrightarrow}}G   , such that         ψ  ~   α   ∘   ψ  β   −  1     ​   (  m  ,  s  )    =   (  m  ,     t   α  ​  β    ​   (  m  )    ⋅  s   )    \tilde{\psi}_{\alpha}\circ\psi_{\beta}^{-1}(m,s)=(m,t_{\alpha\beta}(m)\cdot s)   , for all     s  ∈  S   s\in S   . Now, this allows us to define a map of    G  G   -cocycles      ϵ  𝒞   :=   (   id  M   ,   t   α  ​  β    )    \epsilon_{\mathcal{C}}:=(\mathrm{id}_{M},t_{\alpha\beta})    from     𝒞  =   (   U  α   ,   g   α  ​  β    )    \mathcal{C}=(U_{\alpha},g_{\alpha\beta})    to      𝒞  ~   :=    C  ​   U  λ    ∘   Σ  λ   ∘   Q  λ   ∘   C  ​   B  λ   ​   (  𝒞  )     =   (    U  ~   α   ,    g  ~    α  ​  β    )    \tilde{\mathcal{C}}:=CU^{\lambda}\circ\Sigma^{\lambda}\circ Q^{\lambda}\circ CB^{\lambda}(\mathcal{C})=(\tilde{U}_{\alpha},\tilde{g}_{\alpha\beta})   . It is easily verified that this is indeed a map of cocycles. Indeed,  
 

  
  
     (  m  ,       t   α  ​  β    ​   (  m  )    ⋅   g   β  ​  γ     ​   (  m  )    ⋅  s   )   \displaystyle(m,t_{\alpha\beta}(m)\cdot g_{\beta\gamma}(m)\cdot s)    
       =     ψ  ~   α   ∘   ψ  β   −  1    ∘   ψ  β   ∘    ψ  γ   −  1    ​   (  m  ,  s  )      \displaystyle=\tilde{\psi}_{\alpha}\circ\psi_{\beta}^{-1}\circ\psi_{\beta}\circ\psi_{\gamma}^{-1}(m,s)    
    
  
  
  
       =      ψ  ~   α   ∘   ψ  γ   −  1     ​   (  m  ,  s  )     \displaystyle=\tilde{\psi}_{\alpha}\circ\psi_{\gamma}^{-1}(m,s)    
    
  
  
  
       =   (  m  ,     t   α  ​  γ    ​   (  m  )    ⋅  s   )    \displaystyle=(m,t_{\alpha\gamma}(m)\cdot s)    
    
 
  and therefore         t   α  ​  β    ​   (  m  )    ⋅   g   β  ​  γ     ​   (  m  )    =    t   α  ​  γ    ​   (  m  )     t_{\alpha\beta}(m)\cdot g_{\beta\gamma}(m)=t_{\alpha\gamma}(m)   , since    λ  \lambda    is effective. Similarly          g  ~    α  ​  β    ​   (  m  )    ⋅   t   β  ​  γ     ​   (  m  )    =    t   α  ​  γ    ​   (  m  )     \tilde{g}_{\alpha\beta}(m)\cdot t_{\beta\gamma}(m)=t_{\alpha\gamma}(m)   . Moreover,     ϵ  𝒞   \epsilon_{\mathcal{C}}    is immediately seen to be an isomorphism. Indeed,     (   id  M   ,   t   α  ​  β   ′   )   (\mathrm{id}_{M},t^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta})   , with       t   α  ​  β   ′   ​   (  m  )    :=    t   β  ​  α    ​    (  m  )    −  1      t^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta}(m):=t_{\beta\alpha}(m)^{-1}    on       U  ~   β   ∩   U  α    \tilde{U}_{\beta}\cap U_{\alpha}   , is its inverse.  
 
 
  Moreover, naturality of this isomorphism in    𝒞  \mathcal{C}    follows immediately. Indeed, let     𝒞   ⟶   (  h  ,   h   α  ​  β    )     𝒞  ′    \mathcal{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(h,h_{\alpha\beta})}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}    be a map of cocycles. Then       ϵ   𝒞  ′    ∘   (  h  ,   h   α  ​  β    )    =   (  h  ,    (    t  ′   ∘  h   )    α  ​  β    )    \epsilon_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}\circ(h,h_{\alpha\beta})=(h,(t^{\prime}\circ h)_{\alpha\beta})   , while       (  h  ,    h  ~    α  ​  β    )   ∘   ϵ  𝒞    =   (  h  ,    (    h  ~   ∘  t   )    α  ​  β    )    (h,\tilde{h}_{\alpha\beta})\circ\epsilon_{\mathcal{C}}=(h,(\tilde{h}\circ t)_{\alpha\beta})   , where, by      (    h  ~   ∘  t   )    α  ​  β    (\tilde{h}\circ t)_{\alpha\beta}   , I mean the composition by the natural pointwise multiplication (as described in paragraph   1.1.1  ). What we have to verify, therefore, is that         t   α  ​  β   ′   ​   (   h  ⁡   (  m  )    )    ⋅   h   β  ​  γ     ​   (  m  )    =       h  ~    α  ​  β    ​   (  m  )    ⋅   t   β  ​  γ     ​   (  m  )     t^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta}(h(m))\cdot h_{\beta\gamma}(m)=\tilde{h}_{\alpha\beta}(m)\cdot t_{\beta\gamma}(m)   , for     m  ∈    U  γ   ∩    U  ~   β   ∩    h   −  1    ​   (    U  ~   α  ′   )    ∩    h   −  1    ​   (   U  β  ′   )      m\in U_{\gamma}\cap\tilde{U}_{\beta}\cap h^{-1}(\tilde{U}^{\prime}_{\alpha})\cap h^{-1}(U^{\prime}_{\beta})   . This is almost immediate, if we write    k  k    for the unique map of fibre bundles that corresponds to     (  h  ,   h   α  ​  β    )   (h,h_{\alpha\beta})   :  
 

  
  
     (   h  ⁡   (  m  )    ,       t   α  ​  β   ′   ​   (   h  ⁡   (  m  )    )    ⋅   h   β  ​  γ     ​   (  m  )    ⋅  s   )   \displaystyle(h(m),t^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta}(h(m))\cdot h_{\beta\gamma}(m)\cdot s)    
       =      ψ  ~   α  ′   ∘   ψ    ′  β   −  1     ​   (   h  ⁡   (  m  )    ,     h   β  ​  γ    ​   (  m  )    ⋅  s   )     \displaystyle=\tilde{\psi}^{\prime}_{\alpha}\circ{\psi^{\prime}}_{\beta}^{-1}(h(m),h_{\beta\gamma}(m)\cdot s)    
    
  
  
  
       =     ψ  ~   α  ′   ∘   ψ    ′  β   −  1    ∘   ψ  β  ′   ∘  k  ∘    ψ  γ   −  1    ​   (  m  ,  s  )      \displaystyle=\tilde{\psi}^{\prime}_{\alpha}\circ{\psi^{\prime}}_{\beta}^{-1}\circ\psi^{\prime}_{\beta}\circ k\circ\psi_{\gamma}^{-1}(m,s)    
    
  
  
  
     =    ψ  ~     ′  α     ∘  k  ∘    ψ  ~     ′  β      ∘       −  1      ψ  ~     ′  β     ∘   ψ  γ   −  1     (  m  ,  s  )    \displaystyle={\tilde{\psi}^{\prime}}_{\alpha}\circ k\circ{\tilde{\psi}^{\prime}}_{\beta}{}^{-1}\circ{\tilde{\psi}^{\prime}}_{\beta}\circ\psi_{\gamma}^{-1}(m,s)    
    
  
  
  
     =    ψ  ~   α  ′   ∘  k  ∘    ψ  ~     ′  β      ∘       −  1     (  h   (  m  )   ,   t   β  ​  γ     (  m  )   ⋅  s  )    \displaystyle=\tilde{\psi}^{\prime}_{\alpha}\circ k\circ{\tilde{\psi}^{\prime}}_{\beta}{}^{-1}\circ(h(m),t_{\beta\gamma}(m)\cdot s)    
    
  
  
  
        =   (   h  ⁡   (  m  )    ,        h  ~    α  ​  β    ​   (  m  )    ⋅   t   β  ​  γ     ​   (  m  )    ⋅  s   )    .   \displaystyle=(h(m),\tilde{h}_{\alpha\beta}(m)\cdot t_{\beta\gamma}(m)\cdot s).    
    
 
  Effectiveness of the action does the rest. So we conclude that       C  ​   U  λ    ∘   Σ  λ   ∘   Q  λ   ∘   C  ​   B  λ     ≅   id   C  ​   C  G      CU^{\lambda}\circ\Sigma^{\lambda}\circ Q^{\lambda}\circ CB^{\lambda}\cong\mathrm{id}_{CC^{G}}   . Of course, if we interchange    μ  \mu    and    λ  \lambda   , we also find that       C  ​   U  μ    ∘   Σ  μ   ∘   Q  μ   ∘   C  ​   B  μ     ≅   id   C  ​   C  G      CU^{\mu}\circ\Sigma^{\mu}\circ Q^{\mu}\circ CB^{\mu}\cong\mathrm{id}_{CC^{G}}   .  
 
 
  We define      C   λ  ​  μ    :=        Q  μ   ∘  C   ​   B  μ    ∘  C   ​   U  λ    ∘   Σ  λ     C^{\lambda\mu}:=Q^{\mu}\circ CB^{\mu}\circ CU^{\lambda}\circ\Sigma^{\lambda}    and     C   μ  ​  λ    C^{\mu\lambda}    with    λ  \lambda    and    μ  \mu    interchanged. Then  
 

  
  
      C   λ  ​  μ    ∘   C   μ  ​  λ     \displaystyle C^{\lambda\mu}\circ C^{\mu\lambda}    
       =    (        Q  μ   ∘  C   ​   B  μ    ∘  C   ​   U  λ    ∘   Σ  λ    )   ∘   (        Q  λ   ∘  C   ​   B  λ    ∘  C   ​   U  μ    ∘   Σ  μ    )     \displaystyle=(Q^{\mu}\circ CB^{\mu}\circ CU^{\lambda}\circ\Sigma^{\lambda})\circ(Q^{\lambda}\circ CB^{\lambda}\circ CU^{\mu}\circ\Sigma^{\mu})    
    
  
  
  
       =      Q  μ   ∘  C   ​   B  μ    ∘   (    C  ​   U  λ    ∘   Σ  λ   ∘   Q  λ   ∘   C  ​   B  λ     )   ∘   C  ​   U  μ    ∘   Σ  μ     \displaystyle=Q^{\mu}\circ CB^{\mu}\circ(CU^{\lambda}\circ\Sigma^{\lambda}\circ Q^{\lambda}\circ CB^{\lambda})\circ CU^{\mu}\circ\Sigma^{\mu}    
    
  
  
  
     ≅  Q     μ   ∘  C  B     μ   ∘  id      C  ​   C  G     ∘  C  U     μ   ∘  Σ     μ   (because of the above)     \displaystyle\cong Q^{\mu}\circ CB^{\mu}\circ\mathrm{id}_{CC^{G}}\circ CU^{\mu}\circ\Sigma^{\mu}\quad\textnormal{(because of the above)}\quad    
    
  
  
  
     =  Q     μ   ∘  Σ     μ    (because of theorem     1.1.1    )      \displaystyle=Q^{\mu}\circ\Sigma^{\mu}\quad\textnormal{(because of theorem \ref{cfbcon})}\quad    
    
  
  
  
        =   id   F  ​   B  μ      .   \displaystyle=\mathrm{id}_{FB^{\mu}}.    
    
 
  Again, we can interchange the roles of    μ  \mu    and    λ  \lambda    to obtain the second part of the equivalence of categories. This proves the theorem.
∎  
 
 
 
   Remark 1.1.4   .  
 
 Perhaps the reader was expecting a direct analogue of theorem   1.1.1   in the form of an equivalence of categories between     F  ​   B  λ    FB^{\lambda}    and a category     C  G   C^{G}    that is obtained from     C  ​   C  G    CC^{G}    by dividing out an equivalence relation on the objects and arrows. Indeed, I initially expected that such an equivalence would be an easy stepping stone to obtain the final result of theorem   1.1.1  , which I really was trying to work towards. My candidate for the category     C  G   C^{G}    was one of cohomology classes of    G  G   -cocycles as objects and some corresponding equivalence classes of arrows. This seemed the natural thing to do. Indeed, since my initial attempt I have found a result in  [ 15 ]  that establishes that there exists a (not necessarily natural) bijective correspondence between isomorphism classes of     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -fibre bundles and cohomology classes of    G  G   -cocycles. However, my attempt failed because of the difficulty of constructing a canonical section of the quotient functor that sends a cocycle to its cohomology class. To obtain the categorical analogue of the result in Husemöller, one would like to have such a section to obtain one of the natural isomorphisms in the suspected equivalence of categories between     F  ​   B  λ    FB^{\lambda}    and     C  G   C^{G}   . However, it seems plausible that the result can still be proven without such a canonical section. One would then have to pick an arbitrary representative of every cohomology class and show that the final result does not depend on the choice that was made. 
 
 
 
 
  1.1.5  Associated Bundles and Generalised Frame Bundles 

 
 We have seen that, given a    G  G   -cocycle on    M  M   , there is one preferred    G  G   -bundle that we can always construct: the principal    G  G   -bundle. Therefore, given a     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -fibre bundle, we can construct the corresponding principal bundle from it, using theorem   1.1.3  . The question arises what the relation is between our original fibre bundle and the principal bundle we constructed from it. 
 
 
 The answer is that the principal bundle can be given the interpretation of a  bundle of generalised frames  for the original fibre bundle or, reasoning in the other direction, that the fibre bundle is simply an  associated bundle  to the principal bundle. Both constructions can be understood to be functors. If we write    L  L    for the action of    G  G    on itself by left multiplication and let    λ  \lambda    be an effective left    G  G    action on    S  S   , the generalised frame bundle functor     F  λ   F^{\lambda}    is easily seen to be (naturally isomorphic to)     C   λ  ​  L    C^{\lambda L}   , while we will see that the associated bundle functor     −   [  λ  ]    -[\lambda]    corresponds to     C   L  ​  λ    C^{L\lambda}   . Theorem   1.1.3   states that these two functors are each other’s pseudoinverse. I will discuss the explicit realisation of both constructions, starting with that of the so-called associated bundles.
 
 Suppose we are given a principal bundle     P   ⟶   π  ~    M   P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{\pi}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    with structure group    G  G   . Then, for each (not necessarily effective) left action      G  ×  S    ⟶  λ   S   G\times S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda}}{{\longrightarrow}}S    on a manifold    S  S   , we can construct a right action      P  ×  S  ×  G    ⟶  R    P  ×  S    P\times S\times G\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R}}{{\longrightarrow}}P\times S    by      (  p  ,  s  ,  g  )   ↦   (   p  ⋅  g   ,    g   −  1    ⋅  s   )    (p,s,g)\mapsto(p\cdot g,g^{-1}\cdot s)   . This action is obviously free, since the principal action is. Properness can also easily be verified; however, this is somewhat technical, and we will therefore not write it out. Moreover, the action respects the fibre, and hence the quotient manifold theorem tells us that we can construct a so-called associated fibre bundle       P  ×  S   /  R   =    :    P  ⁡   [  λ  ]     ⟶  π   M    P\times S/R=:P[\lambda]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}M   . (Here,    π  \pi    is induced by the    G  G   -invariant map      P  ×  S    ⟶    π  ~   ∘   π  1     M   P\times S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{\pi}\circ\pi_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M   .) However, following  [ 19 ] , we can also obtain the smooth structure on     P  ⁡   [  λ  ]    P[\lambda]    in a different way, which ties in directly with the rest of our discussion. 
 
 
   Claim 1.1.5   .  
 
      P  ⁡   [  λ  ]     ⟶  π   M   P[\lambda]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}M     is a fibre bundle over    M  M    with standard fibre    S  S   , in such a way that for each     p  ∈  P   p\in P   ,       {  p  }   ×  S    ⟶   q  p     P  ​    [  λ  ]     π  ~   ​   (  p  )       \{p\}\times S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle q_{p}}}{{\longrightarrow}}P[\lambda]_{\tilde{\pi}(p)}   , that is, the restriction of the quotient map      P  ×  S    ⟶  q    P  ⁡   [  λ  ]     P\times S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle q}}{{\longrightarrow}}P[\lambda]   , is a diffeomorphism to the fibre of     P  ⁡   [  λ  ]    P[\lambda]    above      π  ~   ​   (  p  )    \tilde{\pi}(p)    and the equation      q  ⁡   (  p  ,  s  )    =  v   q(p,s)=v    has a unique smooth solution      P   ×  M    P  ⁡   [  λ  ]      ⟶  τ   S   P\times_{M}P[\lambda]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\longrightarrow}}S   .  
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
  Suppose     (   U  α   ,   ϕ  α   )   (U_{\alpha},\phi_{\alpha})    is the maximal principal bundle atlas for    P  P   . (So        π  ~    −  1    ​   (   U  α   )     ⟶   ϕ  α      U  α   ×  G    \tilde{\pi}^{-1}(U_{\alpha})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi_{\alpha}}}{{\longrightarrow}}U_{\alpha}\times G   .) From this we will construct a     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -fibre bundle atlas for     P  ⁡   [  λ  ]    P[\lambda]   . Let       π   −  1    ​   (   U  α   )     ⟶   ψ  α      U  α   ×  S    \pi^{-1}(U_{\alpha})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\psi_{\alpha}}}{{\longrightarrow}}U_{\alpha}\times S    be defined by       ψ  α   −  1    ​   (  m  ,  s  )    :=   q  ⁡   (    ϕ  α   −  1    ​   (  m  ,  e  )    ,  s  )     \psi_{\alpha}^{-1}(m,s):=q(\phi_{\alpha}^{-1}(m,e),s)   , where    e  e    denotes the identity element in    G  G    and    q  q    is the quotient map      P  ×  S    ⟶  q    P  ⁡   [  λ  ]     P\times S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle q}}{{\longrightarrow}}P[\lambda]   . (We will show that     ψ  α   −  1    \psi_{\alpha}^{-1}    is indeed invertible in a minute.) Note that, defined this way,     ψ  α   −  1    \psi_{\alpha}^{-1}    respects the fibre and is smooth.  
 
 
  For     v  ∈    π   −  1    ​   (   U  α   )     v\in\pi^{-1}(U_{\alpha})   , choose a representative     v  =   q  ⁡   (  p  ,  s  )     v=q(p,s)    with       π  ~   ​   (  p  )    =  m  ∈   U  α    \tilde{\pi}(p)=m\in U_{\alpha}   .
