Since     P  m   P_{m}    is a free and transitive right    G  G   -space, there is a unique     g  ∈  G   g\in G    such that     p  =     ϕ  α   −  1    ​   (  m  ,  e  )    ⋅  g    p=\phi_{\alpha}^{-1}(m,e)\cdot g   . Hence     v  =   q  ⁡   (    ϕ  α   −  1    ​   (  m  ,  e  )    ,   g  ⋅  s   )     v=q(\phi_{\alpha}^{-1}(m,e),g\cdot s)   , and this representative is unique. Therefore     ψ  α   −  1    \psi_{\alpha}^{-1}    is indeed bijective, so our notation was appropriate.  
 
 
  Now, if we agree to write     g   α  ​  β    g_{\alpha\beta}    for the transition function, i.e.        ϕ  α   ∘   ϕ  β   −  1     ​   (  m  ,  g  )    =   (  m  ,     g   α  ​  β    ​   (  m  )    ⋅  g   )    \phi_{\alpha}\circ\phi_{\beta}^{-1}(m,g)=(m,g_{\alpha\beta}(m)\cdot g)   , then  
 

  
  
      ψ  β   −  1    ​   (  m  ,  s  )    \displaystyle\psi_{\beta}^{-1}(m,s)    
     =  q   (   ϕ  β   −  1     (  m  ,  e  )   ,  s  )   (*)     \displaystyle=q(\phi_{\beta}^{-1}(m,e),s)\quad\textnormal{(*)}\quad    
    
  
  
  
       =   q  ⁡   (    ϕ  α   −  1    ​   (  m  ,    g   α  ​  β    ​   (  m  )    )    ,  s  )     \displaystyle=q(\phi_{\alpha}^{-1}(m,g_{\alpha\beta}(m)),s)    
    
  
  
  
       =   q  ⁡   (      ϕ  α   −  1    ​   (  m  ,  e  )    ⋅   g   α  ​  β     ​   (  m  )    ,  s  )     \displaystyle=q(\phi_{\alpha}^{-1}(m,e)\cdot g_{\alpha\beta}(m),s)    
    
  
  
  
       =   q  ⁡   (    ϕ  α   −  1    ​   (  m  ,  e  )    ,     g   α  ​  β    ​   (  m  )    ⋅  s   )     \displaystyle=q(\phi_{\alpha}^{-1}(m,e),g_{\alpha\beta}(m)\cdot s)    
    
  
  
  
        =    ψ  α   −  1    ​   (  m  ,     g   α  ​  β    ​   (  m  )    ⋅  s   )     .   \displaystyle=\psi_{\alpha}^{-1}(m,g_{\alpha\beta}(m)\cdot s).    
    
 
  We conclude that        ψ  α   ∘   ψ  β   −  1     ​   (  m  ,  s  )    =   (  m  ,     g   α  ​  β    ​   (  m  )    ⋅  s   )    \psi_{\alpha}\circ\psi_{\beta}^{-1}(m,s)=(m,g_{\alpha\beta}(m)\cdot s)   . Since we know     g   α  ​  β    g_{\alpha\beta}    to satisfy the cocycle condition (   P  P    is a fibre bundle) we conclude that, by the fibre bundle construction theorem,     P  ⁡   [  λ  ]    P[\lambda]    has a unique structure as a fibre bundle with the    G  G   -atlas     (   U  α   ,   ψ  α   )   (U_{\alpha},\psi_{\alpha})   .
Note that     (  ∗  )   (*)    implies that    q  q    is a smooth submersion with respect to the smooth structure that     P  ⁡   [  λ  ]    P[\lambda]    obtains from this fibre bundle atlas. Therefore this smooth structure coincides with the one we would obtain from an application of the quotient manifold theorem.  
 
 
  For the last assertion, we observe that by definition of     ψ  α   \psi_{\alpha}                     P  |    U  α    ×  S   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P|_{U_{\alpha}}\times S}                U  α   ×  G  ×  S   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\alpha}\times G\times S}                   P  ⁡   [  λ  ]    |    U  α    {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P[\lambda]|_{U_{\alpha}}}                 U  α   ×  S   ,   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\alpha}\times S,}                          ϕ  α   ×   id  S    \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\alpha}\times\mathrm{id}_{S}}                      q  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}                    ↺  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circlearrowleft}                        id   U  α    ×  ev   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{U_{\alpha}}\times\mathrm{ev}}                       ψ  α   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{\alpha}}           
  where      G  ×  S    ⟶  ev   S   G\times S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{ev}}}{{\longrightarrow}}S    is given by      (  g  ,  s  )   ↦   g  ⁡   (  s  )     (g,s)\mapsto g(s)   .
Moreover, the horizontals in the diagram are diffeomorphisms, so we see that     q  p   q_{p}    is indeed a diffeomorphism.  
 
 
  Now, consider the equation      q  ⁡   (  p  ,  s  )    =  v   q(p,s)=v   . Note that      det    D  s   ​  q  ​   (  p  ,  s  )     ≠  0   \det D_{s}q(p,s)\neq 0   , since     q  p   q_{p}    is a diffeomorphism for each    p  p   . (By     D  s   D_{s}    we mean the square matrix of partial derivatives with respect to coordinates corresponding to    s  s   .) The implicit function theorem (in local coordinates) then tells us that a unique smooth solution      P   ×  M    P  ⁡   [  λ  ]      ⟶  τ   S   P\times_{M}P[\lambda]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\longrightarrow}}S    exists such that      q  ⁡   (  p  ,   τ  ⁡   (  p  ,  v  )    )    =  v   q(p,\tau(p,v))=v   .
∎  
 
 
 
 Again, we would like to extend this definition on objects to a functor      P  ​   B  G     ⟶   −   [  λ  ]      F  ​   B  λ     PB^{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle-[\lambda]}}{{\longrightarrow}}FB^{\lambda}   . Of course, there is only one natural way to do this. We send a morphism     P   ⟶  k    P  ′    P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle k}}{{\longrightarrow}}P^{\prime}    of principal bundles to the map      k  ⁡   [  λ  ]    =    (   k  ×   id  S    )   /  G    k[\lambda]=(k\times\mathrm{id}_{S})/G   , i.e.       (   k  ⁡   [  λ  ]    )   ​   (   [   p  ,  s   ]   )    =   [   k  ⁡   (  p  )    ,  s  ]    (k[\lambda])([p,s])=[k(p),s]     7    7 
                 7 
                
                
                
              Here     [  p  ,  s  ]   [p,s]    denotes the equivalence class of     (  p  ,  s  )   (p,s)   .   . This map is well-defined. Indeed,       (   k  ⁡   [  λ  ]    )   ​   (   [    (  p  ,  s  )   ⋅  g   ]   )    =   [    k  ⁡   (   p  ⋅  g   )    ,    g   −  1    ⋅  s    ]   =   [    k  ⁡   (  p  )    ⋅  g   ,    g   −  1    ⋅  s   ]   =   [   k  ⁡   (  p  )    ,  s  ]   =    (   k  ⁡   [  λ  ]    )   ​   (   [  p  ,  s  ]   )     (k[\lambda])([(p,s)\cdot g])=[k(p\cdot g),g^{-1}\cdot s]=[k(p)\cdot g,g^{-1}\cdot s]=[k(p),s]=(k[\lambda])([p,s])   , where the second identity holds precisely because    k  k    is a map of principal bundles, i.e.    G  G   -equivariant. Functoriality is clear, i.e.       (    k  ′   ∘  k   )   ​   [  λ  ]    =     k  ′   ​   [  λ  ]    ∘   k  ⁡   [  λ  ]      (k^{\prime}\circ k)[\lambda]=k^{\prime}[\lambda]\circ k[\lambda]   . 
 
 
 Note that, by definition of     k  ⁡   [  λ  ]    k[\lambda]   , the following square commutes               P  ×  S   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\times S}                P  ′   ×  S   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}\times S}                 P  ⁡   [  λ  ]    {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P[\lambda]}                 P  ′   ​   [  λ  ]    .   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}[\lambda].}                         k  ×   id  S    \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\times\mathrm{id}_{S}}                      q  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}                       q  ′   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}}                       k  ⁡   [  λ  ]    \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k[\lambda]}           
 Therefore      k  ⁡   [  λ  ]    ∘  q   k[\lambda]\circ q    is a smooth map, since      q  ′   ∘   (   k  ×   id  S    )    q^{\prime}\circ(k\times\mathrm{id}_{S})    is so. Since    q  q    is a surjective submersion, we conclude that     k  ⁡   [  λ  ]    k[\lambda]    is smooth. 
 
 
 To conclude our discussion of the associated bundle, we check that     k  ⁡   [  λ  ]    k[\lambda]    is a morphism of     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -bundles. Denote by     π  ~   \tilde{\pi}    and      π  ~   ′   \tilde{\pi}^{\prime}   , respectively, the projections from     P  ⁡   [  λ  ]    P[\lambda]    and      P  ′   ​   [  λ  ]    P^{\prime}[\lambda]    to    M  M    and     M  ′   M^{\prime}   . Note that     k  ×   id  S    k\times\mathrm{id}_{S}    is a map of fibre bundles:               P  ×  S   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\times S}                P  ′   ×  S   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}\times S}                M  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}                M  ′   .   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime}.}                         k  ×   id  S    \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\times\mathrm{id}_{S}}                        π  ~   ∘  q   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\pi}\circ q}                    ↺  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circlearrowleft}                         π  ~   ′   ∘   q  ′    \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\pi}^{\prime}\circ q^{\prime}}                       k  M   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{M}}           
 Therefore       k  M   ∘   π  ~   ∘  q   =     π  ~   ′   ∘   q  ′   ∘   (   k  ×   id  S    )    =     π  ~   ′   ∘   k  ⁡   [  λ  ]    ∘  q    k_{M}\circ\tilde{\pi}\circ q=\tilde{\pi}^{\prime}\circ q^{\prime}\circ(k\times\mathrm{id}_{S})=\tilde{\pi}^{\prime}\circ k[\lambda]\circ q   . Now, since    q  q    is a surjection, we conclude that       k  M   ∘   π  ~    =     π  ~   ′   ∘   k  ⁡   [  λ  ]      k_{M}\circ\tilde{\pi}=\tilde{\pi}^{\prime}\circ k[\lambda]   , so     k  ⁡   [  λ  ]    k[\lambda]    is a map of fibre bundles. 
 
 
 The last thing we want to do is to verify that this map acts by    λ  \lambda   . Let     p  ∈   P  m    p\in P_{m}    and choose      p  ′   ∈   P    k  M   ​   (  m  )    ′    p^{\prime}\in P^{\prime}_{k_{M}(m)}   . For this paragraph, write       q  ¯   p   :   S  →   P  ​    [  λ  ]   m      \bar{q}_{p}:S\to P[\lambda]_{m}    and       q  ¯    p  ′   ′   :   S  →    P  ′   ​    [  λ  ]     k  M   ​   (  m  )        \bar{q}^{\prime}_{p^{\prime}}:S\to P^{\prime}[\lambda]_{k_{M}(m)}    for the maps     s  ↦   [   p  ,  s   ]    s\mapsto[p,s]    and     s  ↦   [    p  ′   ,  s   ]    s\mapsto[p^{\prime},s]   . Since    G  G    acts freely and transitively on the fibres of     P  ′   P^{\prime}   , there is a unique     g  ∈  G   g\in G    such that      k  ⁡   (  p  )    =    p  ′   ⋅  g    k(p)=p^{\prime}\cdot g   . Then 
 

  
  
        k  ⁡   [  λ  ]    ​   (     q  ¯   p   ​   (  s  )    )    =   [    k  ⁡   (  p  )    ,  s   ]   =   [    p  ′   ⋅  g   ,  s  ]   =   [   p  ′   ,   g  ⋅  s   ]   =     q  ¯    p  ′   ′   ​   (    λ  g   ​   (  s  )    )     .   k[\lambda](\bar{q}_{p}(s))=[k(p),s]=[p^{\prime}\cdot g,s]=[p^{\prime},g\cdot s]=\bar{q}^{\prime}_{p^{\prime}}(\lambda_{g}(s)).    
    
 
 Thus, in the fibre coordinates determined by    p  p    and     p  ′   p^{\prime}   ,     k  ⁡   [  λ  ]    k[\lambda]    is given by the action map      λ  g   :   S  →  S    \lambda_{g}:S\to S   . 
 
 
   Corollary 1.1.6   .  
 
  In the case of an effective action    λ  \lambda   , the associated bundle construction can equivalently be described as
     −   [  λ  ]    ≅   C   L  ​  λ     -[\lambda]\cong C^{L\lambda}   .  
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
  The statement that      P  ⁡   [  λ  ]    ≅    C   L  ​  λ    ​   (  P  )     P[\lambda]\cong C^{L\lambda}(P)    follows immediately by comparison of the conclusion that        ψ  α   ∘   ψ  β   −  1     ​   (  m  ,  s  )    =   (  m  ,     g   α  ​  β    ​   (  m  )    ⋅  s   )    \psi_{\alpha}\circ\psi_{\beta}^{-1}(m,s)=(m,g_{\alpha\beta}(m)\cdot s)    in the proof of claim   1.1.5   with the construction in the proof of theorem   1.1.1  . The naturality of the isomorphism should be clear from the discussion above.
∎  
 
 
 
 Therefore, there is also a general (pseudo)inverse construction to that of the associated bundle corresponding to an effective action. Indeed, suppose we are given a fibre bundle     F   ⟶  π   M   F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}M   , with fibre    S  S    and structure group    G  G    that acts by an action    λ  \lambda   . Then, as a consequence of theorem   1.1.3  ,     C   λ  ​  L    C^{\lambda L}    is a pseudoinverse to     C   L  ​  λ    C^{L\lambda}    and therefore also to     −   [  λ  ]    -[\lambda]   . 
 
 
 We can also find an interpretation of this inverse construction. Note that we can consider the elements of      C   λ  ​  L    ​   (  F  )    C^{\lambda L}(F)    as generalised frames  8    8 
                 8 
                
                
                
              That is, a (linear) frame being a linear isomorphism (and thus a diffeomorphism)      𝕜  k    ⟶      V  m    \Bbbk^{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}V_{m}   , if    V  V    is a vector bundle and either     𝕜  =  ℝ   \Bbbk=\mathbb{R}    or     𝕜  =  ℂ   \Bbbk=\mathbb{C}\;   .    for    F  F   . We identify a     p  ∈    C   λ  ​  L    ​   (  F  )     p\in C^{\lambda L}(F)    with     S  ≅    {  p  }   ×  S    ⟶   q  p      (    C   λ  ​  L    ​   (  F  )    )   ​    [  λ  ]     π  ~   ​   (  p  )      ≅   F    π  ~   ​   (  p  )      S\cong\{p\}\times S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle q_{p}}}{{\longrightarrow}}(C^{\lambda L}(F))[\lambda]_{\tilde{\pi}(p)}\cong F_{\tilde{\pi}(p)}   , which is a diffeomorphism according to claim   1.1.5  . This enables us to see      C   λ  ​  L    ​    (  F  )   m    C^{\lambda L}(F)_{m}    as a subset       F  λ   ​    (  F  )   m    ⊂   Diff  ⁡   (  S  ,   F  m   )     F^{\lambda}(F)_{m}\subset\mathrm{Diff}(S,F_{m})   . Note that      F  λ   ​   (  F  )    F^{\lambda}(F)    contains precisely the frames corresponding to the fibre components of arrows between     S  ×  U   S\times U    and      F  |   U   F|_{U}    in     F  ​   B  U  λ    FB_{U}^{\lambda}   , if    U  U    is some open in    M  M   . In particular, sections of      F  λ   ​   (  F  )    F^{\lambda}(F)    correspond to trivialisations of    F  F   . Also note that the action of     C   λ  ​  L    C^{\lambda L}    on arrows corresponds in a natural way to postcomposition in the identification       C   λ  ​  L    ​   (  F  )    ≅    F  λ   ​   (  F  )     C^{\lambda L}(F)\cong F^{\lambda}(F)   , i.e. 
 

  
  
        q  ⁡   (    C   λ  ​  L    ​   (  k  )   ​   (  p  )    ,  −  )    =   q    C   λ  ​  L    ​   (  k  )   ​   (  p  )     =   k  ∘   q  p    =   k  ∘   q  ⁡   (  p  ,  −  )      ,  (*)      q(C^{\lambda L}(k)(p),-)=q_{C^{\lambda L}(k)(p)}=k\circ q_{p}=k\circ q(p,-),\quad\textnormal{(*)}\quad    
    
 
 if     F   ⟶  k    F  ′    F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle k}}{{\longrightarrow}}F^{\prime}    is a map of     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -fibre bundles. We have obtained the following result. 
 
 
   Corollary 1.1.7   .  
 
  We have a natural isomorphism      C   λ  ​  L    ≅   F  λ    C^{\lambda L}\cong F^{\lambda}   , where     F  λ   F^{\lambda}    is defined as described above.  
 
 
 
 Summarising, we have made the equivalence of categories               P  ​   B  G    {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PB^{G}}               F  ​   B  λ    {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FB^{\lambda}}                         C   λ  ​  L    \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\lambda L}}                       C   L  ​  λ    \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{L\lambda}}           
 (which we had established for an effective action      G  ×  S    ⟶  λ   S   G\times S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda}}{{\longrightarrow}}S   ) concrete in the form of the associated bundle construction      −   [  λ  ]    ≅   C   L  ​  λ     -[\lambda]\cong C^{L\lambda}    and that of the generalised frame bundle      F  λ   ≅   C   λ  ​  L     F^{\lambda}\cong C^{\lambda L}   . Naturally, these constructions are also each other’s pseudoinverse:               P  ​   B  G    {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PB^{G}}                F  ​   B  λ    .   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FB^{\lambda}.}                         F  λ   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\lambda}}                       −   [  λ  ]    \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-[\lambda]}           
 Therefore, whenever we encounter a     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -fibre bundle over a manifold    M  M   , we can interpret it as an associated bundle of some principal    G  G   -bundle over    M  M   , which then takes on the role of a bundle of generalised frames for the original fibre bundle. This interpretation is of importance when trying to understand why principal bundles arise in physics. 
 
 
   Remark 1.1.8   .  
 
 The reader might wonder why we have to demand that    λ  \lambda    be effective to obtain the result that the associated bundle functor defines an equivalence of categories between     P  ​   B  G    PB^{G}    and     F  ​   B  λ    FB^{\lambda}   . Indeed, we have seen that the associated bundle functor can be defined for arbitrary    G  G   -actions. To see what can go wrong, consider the following (somewhat extreme) example. Let      G  ×  S    ⟶  λ   S   G\times S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda}}{{\longrightarrow}}S    be the trivial action of a non-trivial Lie group    G  G    on a one-point manifold     S  =   {  p  }    S=\{p\}   . Then we can construct a category      F  ​   B  λ    ≅  Mfd   FB^{\lambda}\cong\mathrm{Mfd}   , which is, of course, just isomorphic to the category of smooth manifolds. Moreover, we have an associated bundle functor      P  ​   B  G     ⟶   −   [  λ  ]      F  ​   B  λ    ≅  Mfd   PB^{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle-[\lambda]}}{{\longrightarrow}}FB^{\lambda}\cong\mathrm{Mfd}   , which just sends each principal bundle to its base space. Obviously, this functor is not faithful  9    9 
                   9 
                  
                  
                  
                As is easily checked, it is full and essentially surjective on objects, though.   , so it cannot possibly define an equivalence of categories. 
 
 
 
 
  1.1.6  Vector Bundles and Principal     G  ​  L  ​   (   𝕜  k   )    GL(\Bbbk^{k})   -Bundles 

 
 An important special case of this equivalence of categories is that of the standard action    λ  \lambda    of     G  ​  L  ​   (   𝕜  k   )    GL(\Bbbk^{k})    on the vector space     𝕜  k   \Bbbk^{k}   , where either     𝕜  =  ℝ   \Bbbk=\mathbb{R}    or     𝕜  =  ℂ   \Bbbk=\mathbb{C}\;   . Note that the objects of     F  ​   B  λ    FB^{\lambda}    are then just the    k  k   -vector bundles over the field    𝕜  \Bbbk   , while the arrows are morphisms of vector bundles (morphisms of fibre bundles that are linear on the fibre) that are invertible on each fibre. We therefore also denote this category by     V  ​   B  𝕜  k    VB^{k}_{\Bbbk}   . 
 
 
 Let     V   ⟶  π   M   V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    be a    k  k   -vector bundle. The corresponding     G  ​  L  ​   (   𝕜  k   )    GL(\Bbbk^{k})   -principal bundle of linear frames       F  λ   ​   (  V  )     ⟶   π  ~    M   F^{\lambda}(V)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{\pi}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    is often simply called  the frame bundle . This can be regarded as the open subbundle of the    Hom  \mathrm{Hom}   -bundle     Hom  ⁡   (    𝕜  k   ×  M   ,  V  )    \mathrm{Hom}(\Bbbk^{k}\times M,V)    defined by the condition that the determinant is non-zero. Observe that the existence of a canonical basis for     𝕜  k   \Bbbk^{k}    tells us that a (local) frame for a    k  k   -vector bundle is the same as a set of    k  k    linearly independent (local) sections of the bundle. The inverse construction      P  ​   B   G  ​  L  ​   (   𝕜  k   )       ⟶   −   [  λ  ]      V  ​   B  𝕜  k     PB^{GL(\Bbbk^{k})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle-[\lambda]}}{{\longrightarrow}}VB^{k}_{\Bbbk}    is sometimes called  the associated vector bundle construction . 
 
 
   Remark 1.1.9   .  
 
 One could argue that     V  ​   B  𝕜  k    VB_{\Bbbk}^{k}    is not the right category to study when discussing vector bundles, since we would also like to consider maps that are not invertible on the fibre. One could advocate that the statement of the equivalence of categories between     V  ​   B  𝕜  k    VB_{\Bbbk}^{k}    and     P  ​   B   G  ​  L  ​   (   𝕜  k   )      PB^{GL(\Bbbk^{k})}     could be restricted to the statement that we have a bijective correspondence on objects. However, this equivalence of categories tells us more. It tells us that this bijective correspondence is natural with respect to a set of morphisms of vector bundles (and principal bundles), which includes the isomorphisms. Note that in the case in which we only consider the corresponding categories of bundles over a manifold    M  M    with morphisms that are the identity (or some automorphism) on    M  M   , this set of arrows consists precisely of the isomorphisms. 
 
 
 
 
  1.1.7  Orthogonal Frame Bundles 

 
 We can also start with a vector bundle equipped with a metric, i.e. a smooth fibrewise symmetric bilinear form (or Hermitian form in the complex case)    g  g    that is non-degenerate in the sense that it identifies each fibre with its dual (antilinearly in the Hermitian case). This is a situation that we encounter all the time in physics.    g  g    can either be a Euclidean, Hermitian, or Lorentzian metric. The examples are numerous. In these physical examples, we are usually not so much interested in the category of all vector bundles, but in the category     M  ​  V  ​   B   𝕜  ,   (  p  ,  q  )    k    MVB_{\Bbbk,(p,q)}^{k}    of metrised    k  k   -dimensional vector bundles over the field    𝕜  \Bbbk    with signature     (  p  ,  q  )   (p,q)   . Of course, its objects are just    k  k   -vector bundles together with a specified     (  p  ,  q  )   (p,q)   -metric while its arrows are vector bundle morphisms which are also isometries. (Note that isometries between    k  k   -vector spaces are automatically invertible, so these morphisms are indeed fibrewise invertible.) We can equivalently describe these metrised vector bundles in the following way. 
 
 
   Claim 1.1.10   .  
 
  Let       O  ⁡   (  𝕜  ,   (  p  ,  q  )   )    ×   𝕜  k     ⟶  λ    𝕜  k    O(\Bbbk,(p,q))\times\Bbbk^{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Bbbk^{k}    be the standard action of the orthogonal group corresponding to the standard     (  p  ,  q  )   (p,q)   -inner product     ⟨   ⋅  ,  ⋅   ⟩   \langle\>\cdot\,,\cdot\>\rangle    on     𝕜  k   \Bbbk^{k}   . Then we have an isomorphism of categories                M  ​  V  ​   B   𝕜  ,   (  p  ,  q  )    k    {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces MVB_{\Bbbk,(p,q)}^{k}}                F  ​   B  λ    .   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FB^{\lambda}.}                         M  k   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{k}}                       D  k   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{k}}           
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
  We first construct a metrisation functor     M  k   M^{k}   . Let     ℱ  :=   (  π  ,   [    U  α   ,   ψ  α   ,   g   α  ​  β     ]   )   ∈   F  ​   B  λ     \mathcal{F}:=(\pi,[U_{\alpha},\psi_{\alpha},g_{\alpha\beta}])\in FB^{\lambda}    be an     (   O  ⁡   (  𝕜  ,   (  p  ,  q  )   )    ,  λ  )   (O(\Bbbk,(p,q)),\lambda)   -fibre bundle (which is of course a vector bundle). Pick the corresponding maximal atlas structure, i.e.       Σ  λ   ​   (  ℱ  )    =   (  π  ,   (   U  α   ,   ψ  α   ,   g   α  ​  β    )   )    \Sigma^{\lambda}(\mathcal{F})=(\pi,(U_{\alpha},\psi_{\alpha},g_{\alpha\beta}))   . Then each     ψ  α   \psi_{\alpha}    gives us a (pseudo) inner product     g  α   g_{\alpha}    on      π   −  1    ​   (   U  α   )    \pi^{-1}(U_{\alpha})    (from the standard inner product on     𝕜  k   \Bbbk^{k}   ). Moreover, these inner products agree on chart overlaps, since the transition functions act by the standard action of the orthogonal group. The sheaf property of smooth functions gives us a unique inner product    g  g    on    π  \pi   . We write       M  k   ​   (  ℱ  )    :=   (  ℱ  ,  g  )    M^{k}(\mathcal{F}):=(\mathcal{F},g)    for the metrised vector bundle we have constructed. Note that arrows in     F  ​   B  λ    FB^{\lambda}    are indeed isometries. Indeed, in charts, they are described by an action of the orthogonal group, so we can define     M  k   M^{k}    to be the identity on maps.  
 
 
  The demetrisation functor     D  k   D^{k}    will just be the functor that forgets the metric and is the identity on arrows. However, we have to show that a metrised vector bundle has an atlas with transition functions that only take values in      O  ⁡   (  𝕜  ,   (  p  ,  q  )   )    ⊂   G  ​  L  ​   (   𝕜  k   )     O(\Bbbk,(p,q))\subset GL(\Bbbk^{k})   . This is obvious when we see that we can construct local orthonormal frames for the bundle.  
 
 
  Indeed, let     (   V   ⟶  π   M   ,  g  )   (V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}M,g)    be a metrised vector bundle. Let     (   U  α   ,   ψ  α   ,   g   α  ​  β    )   (U_{\alpha},\psi_{\alpha},g_{\alpha\beta})    be its maximal vector bundle atlas (so     (   g   α  ​  β    )   (g_{\alpha\beta})    have values in     G  ​  L  ​   (   𝕜  k   )    GL(\Bbbk^{k})   ). Let     (   f   α  ,  j    )   (f_{\alpha,j})   ,      U  α    ⟶   f   α  ,  j       V  |    U  α     U_{\alpha}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{\alpha,j}}}{{\longrightarrow}}V|_{U_{\alpha}}    be a local frame for     V   ⟶  π   M   V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}M   . After refining the cover if necessary, the pointwise application of the Gram-Schmidt procedure gives us an orthonormal frame     (   f   α  ,  j   ′   )   (f^{\prime}_{\alpha,j})   , i.e. a frame such that      g  ⁡   (    f   α  ,  j   ′   ​   (  m  )    ,    f   α  ,  k   ′   ​   (  m  )    )    =  0   g(f^{\prime}_{\alpha,j}(m),f^{\prime}_{\alpha,k}(m))=0    if     j  ≠  k   j\neq k    and      g  ⁡   (    f   α  ,  j   ′   ​   (  m  )    ,    f   α  ,  j   ′   ​   (  m  )    )    =   ±  1    g(f^{\prime}_{\alpha,j}(m),f^{\prime}_{\alpha,j}(m))=\pm 1   , where the    −  -   -sign occurs when     j  ≤  p   j\leq p    and the    +  +   -sign holds for the other    q  q    indices. Of course, these orthonormal frames define a strictly equivalent atlas     (   U  α   ,   ψ  α  ′   ,   g   α  ​  β   ′   )   (U_{\alpha},\psi^{\prime}_{\alpha},g^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta})   , by       ψ  α   ′     −  1     ​   (  m  ,   (   v  1   ,  …  ,   v  k   )   )    :=    ∑   j  =  1   k     v  j   ​   f   α  ,  j   ′   ​   (  m  )      \psi^{\prime-1}_{\alpha}(m,(v^{1},\ldots,v^{k})):=\sum_{j=1}^{k}v^{j}f_{\alpha,j}^{\prime}(m)   . Then, clearly,      ⟨  v  ,  w  ⟩   =  g   (   ψ  α  ′     (  m  ,  v  )        −  1    ,   ψ  α  ′     (  m  ,  w  )        −  1    )    \langle\>v\,,w\>\rangle=g(\psi^{\prime}_{\alpha}{}^{-1}(m,v),\psi^{\prime}_{\alpha}{}^{-1}(m,w))   , where     ⟨   ⋅  ,  ⋅   ⟩   \langle\>\cdot\,,\cdot\>\rangle    is the     (  p  ,  q  )   (p,q)   -inner product on     𝕜  k   \Bbbk^{k}   . It follows that  
 

  
  
     ⟨   v  ,  w   ⟩   \displaystyle\langle\>v\,,w\>\rangle    
     =  g   (   ψ  β  ′     (  m  ,  v  )        −  1    ,   ψ  β  ′     (  m  ,  w  )        −  1    )    \displaystyle=g(\psi^{\prime}_{\beta}{}^{-1}(m,v),\psi^{\prime}_{\beta}{}^{-1}(m,w))    
    
  
  
  
     =  g   (   ψ  α  ′     (  m  ,   g   α  ​  β   ′    (  m  )   ⋅  v  )        −  1    ,   ψ  α  ′     (  m  ,   g   α  ​  β   ′    (  m  )   ⋅  w  )        −  1    )    \displaystyle=g(\psi^{\prime}_{\alpha}{}^{-1}(m,g^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta}(m)\cdot v),\psi^{\prime}_{\alpha}{}^{-1}(m,g^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta}(m)\cdot w))    
    
  
  
  
        =   ⟨      g   α  ​  β   ′   ​   (  m  )    ⋅  v   ,     g   α  ​  β   ′   ​   (  m  )    ⋅  w    ⟩    .   \displaystyle=\langle\>g^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta}(m)\cdot v\,,g^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta}(m)\cdot w\>\rangle.    
    
 
  We conclude that the image of     g   α  ​  β   ′   g^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta}    lies in     O  ⁡   (  𝕜  ,   (  p  ,  q  )   )    O(\Bbbk,(p,q))   . This proves the claim, since     M  k   M^{k}    and     D  k   D^{k}    are clearly each other’s inverse.
∎  
 
 
 
   Remark 1.1.11   .  
 
 Since we can construct a Riemannian metric on each vector bundle by using partitions of unity, we see that we can always specify a    k  k   -dimensional vector bundle over    𝕜  \Bbbk    by transition functions with values in     O  ⁡   (  𝕜  ,   (  k  ,  0  )   )    O(\Bbbk,(k,0))   . Put differently, by choosing a metric, we can interpret it as a     (   O  ⁡   (  𝕜  ,   (  k  ,  0  )   )    ,  λ  )   (O(\Bbbk,(k,0)),\lambda)   -fibre bundle. Note that this interpretation is highly non-canonical, depending on the choice of a metric. 
 
 
 
   Corollary 1.1.12   .  
 
  Now, theorem   1.1.3   tells us that we obtain an equivalence of categories  
 

  
  
       M  ​  V  ​   B   𝕜  ,   (  p  ,  q  )    k    ⇆   P  ​   B   O  ⁡   (  𝕜  ,   (  p  ,  q  )   )       .   MVB_{\Bbbk,(p,q)}^{k}\mathop{\leftrightarrows}PB^{O(\Bbbk,(p,q))}.    
    
 
  Similarly, we obtain an equivalence of categories  
 

  
  
      O  ​  M  ​  V  ​   B   𝕜  ,   (  p  ,  q  )    k    ⇆   P  ​   B   S  ​  O  ​   (  𝕜  ,   (  p  ,  q  )   )       OMVB_{\Bbbk,(p,q)}^{k}\mathop{\leftrightarrows}PB^{SO(\Bbbk,(p,q))}    
    
 
  between the categories     O  ​  M  ​  V  ​   B   𝕜  ,   (  p  ,  q  )    k    OMVB_{\Bbbk,(p,q)}^{k}    of oriented     (  p  ,  q  )   (p,q)   -metrised    k  k   -vector bundles and that of     S  ​  O  ​   (  𝕜  ,   (  p  ,  q  )   )    SO(\Bbbk,(p,q))   -principal bundles  10    10 
                      10  
                    
                    
                    
                   Here      S  ​  O  ​   (  𝕜  ,   (  p  ,  q  )   )    SO(\Bbbk,(p,q))     is the subgroup of orientation-preserving elements of      O  ⁡   (  𝕜  ,   (  p  ,  q  )   )    O(\Bbbk,(p,q))     (those with determinant      +  1   +1    ). Of course, in this somewhat clumsy notation,       U  ⁡   (  n  )    =   O  ⁡   (  ℂ  ,   (  n  ,  0  )   )     U(n)=O(\mathbb{C}\;\,,(n,0))     and       O  ⁡   (  p  ,  q  )    =   O  ⁡   (  ℝ  ,   (  p  ,  q  )   )     O(p,q)=O(\mathbb{R}\,,(p,q))     and similarly for their orientation-preserving counterparts.      
 
 
 
 An important advantage of using an orthonormal frame bundle instead of a general frame bundle when describing physics is that its structure group    G  G    is in many cases (in the case of a positive definite metric) compact. This means, for example, that, using the Haar measure, we can integrate over it to average quantities, making them invariant under the principal right action. Since the principal right action represents a change of basis, or gauge transformation, in the original vector bundle, this can be an important demand for quantities that are of physical interest. Moreover, in a very similar way, it will allow us to define invariant inner products on vector spaces on which we have a linear    G  G   -action (in particular an    Ad  \mathrm{Ad}   -invariant inner product on    𝔤  \mathfrak{g}   ), which will be crucial ingredients in Yang-Mills theories. 
 
 
 This turns out to be a very useful way of looking at these bundles. Indeed, modern classical field theory is not formulated in the language of metrised vector bundles but in that of principal bundles with orthogonal structure groups. For example, the electromagnetic force is dealt with on a principal     U  ⁡   (  1  )    U(1)   -bundle, the weak force on a principal     S  ​  U  ​   (  2  )    SU(2)   -bundle and the strong force on a principal     S  ​  U  ​   (  3  )    SU(3)   -bundle.  [ 13 ]  Moreover, the spacetime metric in general relativity gives rise to an     O  ⁡   (  1  ,  3  )    O(1,3)   -principal bundle that has the Lorentz frames as elements. Using this principal bundle, we can hope to define spinors on a curved spacetime, a construction that would be incomprehensible without the language of principal bundles. 
 
 
   Remark 1.1.13   .  
 
 Of course, we never used the symmetry of the inner product in the proof of claim   1.1.10  . A similar result therefore also holds, for example, for symplectic forms: the category of     2  ​  k   2k   -dimensional symplectic vector bundles and the category of principal bundles with     S  ​  p  ​   (   2  ​  k   ,  𝕜  )    Sp(2k,\Bbbk)    as a structure group are equivalent. One encounters an example of this fact when one constructs the (two-dimensional) spinor bundle corresponding to a spin structure on a manifold. Since this is an associated bundle to a     S  ​  L  ​   (  2  ,  ℂ  )    SL(2,\mathbb{C}\;)   -principal bundle and      S  ​  L  ​   (  2  ,  ℂ  )    =   S  ​  p  ​   (  2  ,  ℂ  )     SL(2,\mathbb{C}\;)=Sp(2,\mathbb{C}\;)   , we naturally obtain a symplectic form on the spinor bundle, from the canonical symplectic form on     ℂ  2   \mathbb{C}\;^{2}   . This symplectic form is used intensively by physicists, since it is closely related to the spacetime metric in general relativity, when we consider spin structures on a spacetime manifold. 
 
 
 
 
  1.1.8  How do sections transform under the equivalence? 

 
 In field theories, e.g. classical electromagnetism, physical quantities are described by sections of certain vector bundles over spacetime. While doing physics, we like to do calculations in concrete situations to derive concrete results. To do this, it is very convenient to drop the abstract notation we have been using, to pick a basis for each fibre and do the calculations in coordinates corresponding to this basis. Of course, we want this basis to vary smoothly with the base point. So, briefly, we pick a local frame for our vector bundle when we want to do computations. 
 
 
 This is where the frame bundle comes in. A local frame is, of course, just a local section of the frame bundle. So we have a natural idea of how sections of principal     G  ​  L  ​   (   𝕜  k   )    GL(\Bbbk^{k})   -bundles should transform under the equivalence of categories: they correspond to frames of the canonical associated bundle. 
 
 
 The converse question also naturally arises. The quantities of physical interest are sections of a vector bundle. If we want to describe the physics in terms of the corresponding frame bundle, we would like to know what the counterparts of these sections are in this context. The answer (in a slightly more general context) turns out to be as follows. 
 
 
   Claim 1.1.14   .  
 
  Let     (  P  ,  π  ,  M  ,  G  )   (P,\pi,M,G)    be a principal fibre bundle and let      G  ×  S    ⟶  λ   S   G\times S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda}}{{\longrightarrow}}S    be an effective action. Denote by      C  ∞   ​    (  P  ,  S  )   G    C^{\infty}(P,S)^{G}    the space of    G  G   -equivariant mappings in the sense that      Φ  ⁡   (   p  ⋅  g   )    =    g   −  1    ⋅   Φ  ⁡   (  p  )      \Phi(p\cdot g)=g^{-1}\cdot\Phi(p)    for all     g  ∈  G   g\in G   ,     p  ∈  P   p\in P    and     Φ  ∈    C  ∞   ​    (  P  ,  S  )   G     \Phi\in C^{\infty}(P,S)^{G}   . Then, sections     σ  ∈   Γ  ⁡   (   P  ⁡   [  λ  ]    )     \sigma\in\Gamma(P[\lambda])    of the associated bundle are in one-to-one correspondence with    G  G   -equivariant mappings     Φ  ∈    C  ∞   ​    (  P  ,  S  )   G     \Phi\in C^{\infty}(P,S)^{G}   .  
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
  Let     Φ  ∈    C  ∞   ​    (  P  ,  S  )   G     \Phi\in C^{\infty}(P,S)^{G}   . Then we define a section     σ  Φ   \sigma_{\Phi}    of the associated bundle as follows. Obviously     P   ⟶    id  P   ×  Φ     P  ×  S    P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{id}_{P}\times\Phi}}{{\longrightarrow}}P\times S    is    G  G   -equivariant. Taking the quotient by the    G  G   -action we obtain a smooth section      σ  Φ   =    (    id  P   ×  Φ   )   /  G   :   M  ≅   P  /  G    ⟶       (   P  ×  S   )   /  G   =   P  ⁡   [  λ  ]      \sigma_{\Phi}=(\mathrm{id}_{P}\times\Phi)/G:M\cong P/G\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}(P\times S)/G=P[\lambda]   . More explicitly,       σ  Φ   ​   (  m  )    :=   [   p  ,   Φ  ⁡   (  p  )     ]    \sigma_{\Phi}(m):=[p,\Phi(p)]   , where     p  ∈     π  ~    −  1    ​   (  m  )     p\in\tilde{\pi}^{-1}(m)   . This expression is well-defined, for if       π  ~   ​   (  p  )    =    π  ~   ​   (  q  )    ∈  M   \tilde{\pi}(p)=\tilde{\pi}(q)\in M   , then there exists a     g  ∈  G   g\in G    such that     q  =   p  ⋅  g    q=p\cdot g   . Then      [   q  ,   Φ  ⁡   (  q  )     ]   =   [   p  ⋅  g   ,   Φ  ⁡   (   p  ⋅  g   )    ]   =   [   p  ⋅  g   ,    g   −  1    ​  Φ  ​   (  p  )    ]   =   [  p  ,   Φ  ⁡   (  p  )    ]    [q,\Phi(q)]=[p\cdot g,\Phi(p\cdot g)]=[p\cdot g,g^{-1}\Phi(p)]=[p,\Phi(p)]   , where the second equality holds since    Φ  \Phi    is    G  G   -equivariant. Moreover, we see that     σ  Φ   \sigma_{\Phi}    is indeed a section of     P  ⁡   [  λ  ]    P[\lambda]   . Further,       σ  Φ   ∘   π  ~    =   q  ∘   (    id  P   ×  Φ   )     \sigma_{\Phi}\circ\tilde{\pi}=q\circ(\mathrm{id}_{P}\times\Phi)    and thus smooth, where      P  ×  S    ⟶  q    P  ⁡   [  λ  ]     P\times S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle q}}{{\longrightarrow}}P[\lambda]    is the quotient map. Since     π  ~   \tilde{\pi}    is a surjective submersion,     σ  Φ   \sigma_{\Phi}    is smooth as well.  
 
 
  Conversely, given     σ  ∈   Γ  ⁡   (   P  ⁡   [  λ  ]    )     \sigma\in\Gamma(P[\lambda])   , we define      Φ  σ   ∈    C  ∞   ​    (  P  ,  S  )   G     \Phi_{\sigma}\in C^{\infty}(P,S)^{G}    by       Φ  σ   ​   (  p  )    :=    q  p   −  1    ​   (   σ  ⁡   (    π  ~   ​   (  p  )    )    )     \Phi_{\sigma}(p):=q_{p}^{-1}(\sigma(\tilde{\pi}(p)))   ,     q  p   q_{p}    is the restriction of    q  q   , as in claim   1.1.5  . It is easy to see that this gives a    G  G   -equivariant map. Indeed,       q  p   ​   (  s  )    =   [   p  ,  s   ]   =   [    p  ⋅  g   ,    g   −  1    ⋅  s    ]   =    q   p  ⋅  g    ​   (    g   −  1    ⋅  s   )     q_{p}(s)=[p,s]=[p\cdot g,g^{-1}\cdot s]=q_{p\cdot g}(g^{-1}\cdot s)    and therefore       Φ  σ   ​   (   p  ⋅  g   )    =    q   p  ⋅  g    −  1    ​   (   σ  ⁡   (  m  )    )    =    q   p  ⋅  g    −  1    ​   (    q  p   ​   (    Φ  σ   ​   (  p  )    )    )    =     g   −  1    ⋅   Φ  σ    ​   (  p  )     \Phi_{\sigma}(p\cdot g)=q_{p\cdot g}^{-1}(\sigma(m))=q_{p\cdot g}^{-1}(q_{p}(\Phi_{\sigma}(p)))=g^{-1}\cdot\Phi_{\sigma}(p)   . Smoothness of     Φ  σ   \Phi_{\sigma}    is also apparent, if we note that       Φ  σ   ​   (  p  )    =    q  p   −  1    ​   (   σ  ⁡   (    π  ~   ​   (  p  )    )    )    =   τ  ⁡   (  p  ,   σ  ⁡   (    π  ~   ​   (  p  )    )    )     \Phi_{\sigma}(p)=q_{p}^{-1}(\sigma(\tilde{\pi}(p)))=\tau(p,\sigma(\tilde{\pi}(p)))   , where    τ  \tau    is the smooth map defined in claim   1.1.5  .  
 
 
  Finally, these constructions are each other’s inverse, since  
 

  
  
         Φ   σ  Φ    ​   (  p  )    =    q  p   −  1    ​   (    σ  Φ   ​   (    π  ~   ​   (  p  )    )    )    =    q  p   −  1    ​   (   [   p  ,   Φ  ⁡   (  p  )     ]   )    =   Φ  ⁡   (  p  )       and      \Phi_{\sigma_{\Phi}}(p)=q_{p}^{-1}(\sigma_{\Phi}(\tilde{\pi}(p)))=q_{p}^{-1}([p,\Phi(p)])=\Phi(p)\quad\textnormal{and}\quad    
    
 
 

  
  
        σ   Φ  σ    ​   (  m  )    =   [   p  ,    Φ  σ   ​   (  p  )     ]   =   [   p  ,    q  p   −  1    ​   (   σ  ⁡   (  m  )    )     ]   =    q  p   ​   (    q  p   −  1    ​   (   σ  ⁡   (  m  )    )    )    =   σ  ⁡   (  m  )     .   \sigma_{\Phi_{\sigma}}(m)=[p,\Phi_{\sigma}(p)]=[p,q_{p}^{-1}(\sigma(m))]=q_{p}(q_{p}^{-1}(\sigma(m)))=\sigma(m).    
    
 
  ∎  
 
 
 
 This is of course what physicists do all the time: they choose a local frame     e  ∈   Γ  ⁡   (      F  λ   ​   (  V  )    |   U   )     e\in\Gamma(F^{\lambda}(V)|_{U})    for a vector bundle     V   ⟶     M   V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    and identify a section     σ  ∈   Γ  ⁡   (  V  )     \sigma\in\Gamma(V)    with its coordinates     M  ⊃  U   ⟶    Φ  σ   ∘  e     𝕜  k    M\supset U\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Phi_{\sigma}\circ e}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Bbbk^{k}    with respect to the frame    e  e   . Claim   1.1.14   tells us that this identification is valid. 
 
 
 This approach has the advantage that it is very concrete: a section of    V  V    becomes just a function from    M  M    that has some numbers as output, which enables us to do computations. However, we must always remember that the quantity      Φ  σ   ∘  e   \Phi_{\sigma}\circ e    is not an intrinsic characterisation of    σ  \sigma    (    Φ  σ   \Phi_{\sigma}    is though!), since it depends on the frame we choose to describe    σ  \sigma   . 
 
 
   Remark 1.1.15   .  
 
 In the theory of relativity, this principle has some remarkable manifestations. For example, the electromagnetic field is represented mathematically by a    2  2   -form     M   ⟶  F     ⋀  2     T  ∗   ​  M     M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle F}}{{\longrightarrow}}\bigwedge^{2}T^{*}M    on the spacetime manifold    M  M   . An observer has an associated coordinate frame     e  ∈      F  λ   ​   (  V  )    |   U    e\in F^{\lambda}(V)|_{U}   , with      e   μ  ​  ν    =    d  ​   x  μ    ∧   d  ​   x  ν      e_{\mu\nu}=dx^{\mu}\wedge dx^{\nu}   , if     (   x  μ   )   (x^{\mu})    are geodesic normal coordinates in which the observer is at rest. With respect to this frame, we can describe    F  F    in coordinates     M   ⟶     Φ  F   ∘  e   =   (   F   μ  ​  ν    )       ⋀  2     (   ℝ  4   )   ∗     M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Phi_{F}\circ e=(F_{\mu\nu})}}{{\longrightarrow}}\bigwedge^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})^{*}   . However, another observer will have their own coordinate frame     e  ′   e^{\prime}    and with respect to this frame    F  F    will have different coordinates     (   F   μ  ​  ν   ′   )   (F_{\mu\nu}^{\prime})   . Components of    F  F    that one observer would describe as magnetic fields will be electric fields for the other and vice versa. 
 
 
 
 
  1.1.9  Categories of Principal Bundles Revisited 

 
 Principal bundles have turned out to play a fundamental role in modern physics. It is therefore important to stop and consider their properties. 
 
 
 There is some ambiguity when dealing with principal bundles from a categorical point of view. Of course, everyone would agree on what we would like to call a principal bundle. However, what maps should we consider? We have two choices to make: 
 
  1.  
 
 Do we allow different base spaces    M  M    (in which case it seems natural to allow the maps to be non-trivial on the base)? 
  
  2.  
 
 Do we allow different structure groups    G  G    (in which case it seems natural to allow the maps to be non-trivial on the group)? 
  
 
 This gives us our four categories of principal bundles, which turn out to be very useful: of course there is the biggest one,     P  ​  B   PB   , where we allow everything to happen, then there is     P  ​   B  G    PB^{G}    (the one of  [ 15 ] ), where the answers to the questions are, respectively, yes and no,     P  ​   B  M    PB_{M}    (the one of  [ 18 ] ), where the answers are the other way around, and finally     P  ​   B  M  G    PB_{M}^{G}   , where we answer no to both questions. The first category is essential when we want to emphasise the role of a principal bundle as a special case of a     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -fibre bundle. On the other hand, the second two are more workable when we want to do physics. In particular, we have the following results that will be important for gauge theory. 
 
 
   Claim 1.1.16   .  
 
     P  ​   B  M    PB_{M}     has finite products. To be precise, if      P  i    ⟶    π  ~   i    M   P_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{\pi}_{i}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    is a principal     G  i   G_{i}   -bundle, then the fibred product       P  1    ×  M    P  2     ⟶   π  ~    M   P_{1}\times_{M}P_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{\pi}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    is a principal      G  1   ×   G  2    G_{1}\times G_{2}   -bundle. This is the categorical product.  
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
  We take the projection from     π  ~   \tilde{\pi}    to      π  ~   i   \tilde{\pi}_{i}    to be the map of principal bundles that is the obvious projection onto the    i  i   -th component as a map of fibre bundles and the projection       G  1   ×   G  2     ⟶      G  i    G_{1}\times G_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}G_{i}    on the structure group. The universal property of the product is easily checked.
∎  
 
 
 
 It should be noted that physicists usually call     π  ~   \tilde{\pi}    the  spliced principal bundle  of      π  ~   1   \tilde{\pi}_{1}    and      π  ~   2   \tilde{\pi}_{2}   .   11    11 
                 11 
                
                
                
              Although the importance of this construction might not be evident from this thesis, it is one of the more important tools in gauge theory. When we deal with particles that interact with multiple gauge fields or with particles with spin, the construction is essential to obtain the correct principal bundle on which the physics is described.    
 
 
 The following is a definition that is of the most fundamental importance in gauge theory. 
 
 
   Definition   .  
 
 Let     P   ⟶   π  ~    M  ∈   P  ​   B  M  G     P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{\pi}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M\in PB^{G}_{M}    be a principal    G  G   -bundle. We call     Aut  ⁡   (   π  ~   )    \mathrm{Aut}(\tilde{\pi})   , the group of automorphisms of     π  ~   \tilde{\pi}    in     P  ​   B  M  G    PB^{G}_{M}   , the group of  gauge transformations  of     π  ~   \tilde{\pi}    and denote it by     Gau  ⁡   (   π  ~   )    \mathrm{Gau}(\tilde{\pi})   . To be explicit, this group consists of diffeomorphisms     P   ⟶  k   P   P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle k}}{{\longrightarrow}}P   , such that       π  ~   ∘  k   =   π  ~    \tilde{\pi}\circ k=\tilde{\pi}    and such that       ρ  g   ∘  k   =   k  ∘   ρ  g     \rho_{g}\circ k=k\circ\rho_{g}   , for all     g  ∈  G   g\in G   . Note that any arrow in     P  ​   B  M  G    PB^{G}_{M}    is an isomorphism, so     Gau  ​   π  ~    \mathrm{Gau}\,\tilde{\pi}    consists precisely of the endomorphisms of     π  ~   \tilde{\pi}    in     P  ​   B  M  G    PB^{G}_{M}   . 
 
 
 
 Just like     Diff  ​  S   \mathrm{Diff}\,S   ,     Gau  ​   π  ~    \mathrm{Gau}\,\tilde{\pi}    has the structure of an infinite-dimensional manifold on which the group operations are smooth.  [ 22 ]  In the line of thought of the previous paragraph, we have the following result. 
 
 
   Claim 1.1.17   .  
 
  There is an isomorphism of groups       C  ∞   ​    (  P  ,  G  )   G    ≅   Gau  ​   π  ~     C^{\infty}(P,G)^{G}\cong\mathrm{Gau}\,\tilde{\pi}   , where we take    G  G    to act on itself on the left by conjugation. Claim   1.1.14   now tells us that we can see gauge transformations as sections of the obvious associated     (  G  ,  C  )   (G,C)   -fibre bundle.  
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
  Let     Φ  ∈    C  ∞   ​    (  P  ,  G  )   G     \Phi\in C^{\infty}(P,G)^{G}   . Then we define a map     P   ⟶   k  Φ    P   P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle k_{\Phi}}}{{\longrightarrow}}P    by       k  Φ   ​   (  p  )    :=   p  ⋅   Φ  ⁡   (  p  )      k_{\Phi}(p):=p\cdot\Phi(p)   . Obviously, this is a map of fibre bundles that is the identity on    M  M   . Moreover       k  Φ   ​   (   p  ⋅  g   )    =    (   p  ⋅  g   )   ⋅   Φ  ⁡   (   p  ⋅  g   )     =    (   p  ⋅  g   )   ⋅   g   −  1    ⋅   Φ  ⁡   (  p  )    ⋅  g   =   p  ⋅   Φ  ⁡   (  p  )    ⋅  g   =     k  Φ   ​   (  p  )    ⋅  g    k_{\Phi}(p\cdot g)=(p\cdot g)\cdot\Phi(p\cdot g)=(p\cdot g)\cdot g^{-1}\cdot\Phi(p)\cdot g=p\cdot\Phi(p)\cdot g=k_{\Phi}(p)\cdot g   , so     k  Φ   k_{\Phi}    is a map of principal bundles that is the identity on    G  G   . We conclude that      k  Φ   ∈   Gau  ​   π  ~     k_{\Phi}\in\mathrm{Gau}\,\tilde{\pi}   .  
 
 
  Conversely, let     k  ∈   Gau  ​   π  ~     k\in\mathrm{Gau}\,\tilde{\pi}    and define     P   ⟶   Φ  k    G   P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Phi_{k}}}{{\longrightarrow}}G    by the implicit relation      k  ⁡   (  p  )    =    p  ⋅   Φ  k    ​   (  p  )     k(p)=p\cdot\Phi_{k}(p)   . (Note that this is a good definition since the principal right action is transitive and free on the fibre. Moreover, smoothness is immediate from the smoothness of the group multiplication, if we write out the definition in a chart.) Now,      p  ⋅  g  ⋅    Φ  k   ​   (   p  ⋅  g   )     =   k  ⁡   (   p  ⋅  g   )    =    k  ⁡   (  p  )    ⋅  g   =     p  ⋅   Φ  k    ​   (  p  )    ⋅  g    p\cdot g\cdot\Phi_{k}(p\cdot g)=k(p\cdot g)=k(p)\cdot g=p\cdot\Phi_{k}(p)\cdot g   . Therefore       Φ  k   ​   (   p  ⋅  g   )    =      g   −  1    ⋅   Φ  k    ​   (  p  )    ⋅  g    \Phi_{k}(p\cdot g)=g^{-1}\cdot\Phi_{k}(p)\cdot g   . We conclude that      Φ  k   ∈    C  ∞   ​    (  P  ,  G  )   G     \Phi_{k}\in C^{\infty}(P,G)^{G}   .  
 
 
  Now, obviously, these constructions are each other’s inverse. Finally, let     Φ  ,    Φ  ′   ∈    C  ∞   ​    (  P  ,  G  )   G      \Phi,\Phi^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}(P,G)^{G}   . Then        k  Φ   ∘   k   Φ  ′     ​   (  p  )    =     p  ⋅   Φ  ′    ​   (  p  )    ⋅   Φ  ⁡   (    p  ⋅   Φ  ′    ​   (  p  )    )     =       p  ⋅   Φ  ′    ​   (  p  )    ⋅   Φ  ′    ​    (  p  )    −  1     ⋅   Φ  ⁡   (  p  )    ⋅    Φ  ′   ​   (  p  )     =   p  ⋅   Φ  ⁡   (  p  )    ⋅    Φ  ′   ​   (  p  )     =    k   Φ  ⋅   Φ  ′     ​   (  p  )     k_{\Phi}\circ k_{\Phi^{\prime}}(p)=p\cdot\Phi^{\prime}(p)\cdot\Phi(p\cdot\Phi^{\prime}(p))=p\cdot\Phi^{\prime}(p)\cdot\Phi^{\prime}(p)^{-1}\cdot\Phi(p)\cdot\Phi^{\prime}(p)=p\cdot\Phi(p)\cdot\Phi^{\prime}(p)=k_{\Phi\cdot\Phi^{\prime}}(p)   .
∎  
 
 
 
   Remark 1.1.18   .  
 
 These gauge transformations are a generalisation to arbitrary spacetimes of what physicists call gauge transformations. Indeed, every principal bundle over Minkowski spacetime     M  =   ℝ  4    M=\mathbb{R}^{4}    is trivial, since     ℝ  4   \mathbb{R}^{4}    is contractible.  [ 28 ]  Therefore, in the case of a principal    G  G   -bundle     π  ~   \tilde{\pi}    over     M  =   ℝ  4    M=\mathbb{R}^{4}    (which we know to be trivial), we can construct a special class of gauge transformations from maps     M   ⟶  ϕ   G   M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi}}{{\longrightarrow}}G   , by defining      f  ϕ   ∈   Gau  ​   π  ~     f_{\phi}\in\mathrm{Gau}\,\tilde{\pi}    by       f  ϕ   ​   (  m  ,  g  )    =   (  m  ,    ϕ  ⁡   (  m  )    ⋅  g   )    f_{\phi}(m,g)=(m,\phi(m)\cdot g)   . Indeed, for a trivial principal bundle, we easily see that every gauge transformation is of this form. These transformations are what physicists call a local gauge transformation, in contrast to the case in which they demand that    ϕ  \phi    be constant, in which case they talk about global gauge transformations. It is important to see, however, that in general, such a difference cannot be made and we can only talk about plain gauge transformations. 
 
 
 
 
 
  1.2  Connections on Fibre Bundles 

 
 In this section, I introduce the notion of a connection on a fibre bundle, which is central both in the general theory of relativity, where it determines the geodesics along which a point particle moves through spacetime (and in some sense is a potential for the gravitational field), and in elementary particle physics, where it describes the gauge potentials, the mathematical abstractions of the fundamental interactions of matter. 
 
 
 Many different notions of a connection are used in the literature. I will use a particularly general geometric definition, in accordance with  [ 19 ] , and will treat a few special cases separately. I hope that this abstract approach will help the reader to see the connections between the mathematics used in the different theories of physics, which are insufficiently emphasised in many references. 
 
 
  1.2.1  A Geometric Definition 

 
 Let     F   ⟶  π   M   F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    be a fibre bundle. Note that at each point     f  ∈  F   f\in F    the kernel     ker  ⁡    T  f   ​  π    \ker T_{f}\pi    is a linear subspace of      T  f   ​  F   T_{f}F   . This is easily seen to define a subbundle      ker  ⁡   T  ​  π    ⊂   T  ​  F    \ker T\pi\subset TF    over    F  F   , called the  vertical bundle  and denoted     V  ​  F   VF   . 
 
 
 This bundle can also be given another interpretation. To see this, note that 
 

  
  
     0   ⟶       T  f   ​   F  m     ⟶    T  f   ​   i  m       T  f   ​  F    ⟶    T  f   ​  π      T  m   ​  M    ⟶     0   0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}T_{f}F_{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle T_{f}i_{m}}}{{\longrightarrow}}T_{f}F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle T_{f}\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}T_{m}M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}0    
    
 
 is a short exact sequence. Here     m  =   π  ⁡   (  f  )     m=\pi(f)    and      F  m    ⟶   i  m    F   F_{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i_{m}}}{{\longrightarrow}}F    denotes the inclusion. (Indeed     π  ∘   i  m    \pi\circ i_{m}    has constant value m, so      im  ​   T  f   ​   i  m    ⊂   V  ​   F  f     \mathrm{im}\;T_{f}i_{m}\subset VF_{f}   . Moreover their dimensions are equal.)   12    12 
                 12 
                
                
                
              Furthermore, if    F  F    is a vector bundle, we can identify       T  f   ​   F  m    ≅   F  m    T_{f}F_{m}\cong F_{m}   , so, in that case, we can see the vertical subspace     V  ​   F  f    VF_{f}    at a point    f  f    as a copy of     F  m   F_{m}   .    The short exact sequence above also tells us that we can identify the quotient space        T  f   ​  F   /  V   ​   F  f    T_{f}F/VF_{f}    with      T  m   ​  M   T_{m}M   . This gives us the following identity of vector bundles over    F  F   : 
 

  
  
         T  ​  F   /  V   ​  F   ≅    π  ∗   ​  T  ​  M    ,   TF/VF\cong\pi^{*}TM,    
    
 
 where      π  ∗   ​  T  ​  M   \pi^{*}TM    denotes the pullback bundle of     T  ​  M   TM    along    π  \pi   . Of course, this calls for an explicit realisation of     T  ​  F   TF    as a direct sum of     V  ​  F   VF    and      π  ∗   ​  T  ​  M   \pi^{*}TM   . This is given by a connection. 
 
 
   Definition     (Connection) .  
 
 Let     F   ⟶  π   M   F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    be a fibre bundle. Let       π  ∗   ​  T  ​  M    ⟶  ι    T  ​  F    \pi^{*}TM\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota}}{{\longrightarrow}}TF    be an embedding of vector bundles over    F  F    such that       T  ​  π   ∘   ι  ⁡   (  f  ,  X  )     =  X   T\pi\circ\iota(f,X)=X   , and denote its image by     H  ​  F   HF   . We call     H  ​  F   HF    a  connection  if        T  ​  F   =    H  ​  F   ⊕   V  ​  F     TF=HF\oplus VF    and     H  ​   F  f    HF_{f}    is called the  horizontal subspace  at     f  ∈  F   f\in F   . The projections      H  ​  F    ⟵      T  ​  F    ⟶      V  ​  F    HF\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longleftarrow}}TF\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}VF    are denoted by     P  H   P_{H}    and     P  V   P_{V}    respectively.  [ 5 ]  
 
 
 
   Remark 1.2.1   .  
 
 Although I will interchangeably use the vertical projection     P  V   P_{V}    and the horizontal bundle     H  ​  F   HF    to denote a connection, what I mean by a connection is the whole product diagram      H  ​  F    ⟵   P  H     T  ​  F    ⟶   P  V     V  ​  F    HF\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P_{H}}}{{\longleftarrow}}TF\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P_{V}}}{{\longrightarrow}}VF    in the category     F  ​   B  F    FB_{F}    of fibre bundles over    F  F   . 
 
 
 
 
  1.2.2  Curvature 

 
 Note that we can see     H  ​  F   HF    and     V  ​  F   VF    as distributions on    F  F   . We can of course ask whether these distributions are integrable. Recall Frobenius’ theorem, which gives a useful criterion for integrability  [ 33 ] : 
 
 
   Theorem 1.2.2     (Frobenius) .  
 
  A    l  l   -dimensional distribution    H  H    on a manifold    F  F    is integrable, i.e. there exists a    l  l   -dimensional foliation    ℱ  \mathcal{F}    such that for all     f  ∈  F  :     f\in F:        H  f   H_{f}    is the tangent space      T  f   ​   ℱ  f    T_{f}\mathcal{F}_{f}    to the leaf     ℱ  f   \mathcal{F}_{f}    through    f  f   , if and only if it is involutive, i.e. for each pair of vector fields     X  ,   Y  ∈   Γ  ⁡   (  H  )    ⊂   𝒳  ⁡   (  F  )      X,Y\in\Gamma(H)\subset\mathcal{X}(F)   ,      [   X  ,  Y   ]   ∈   Γ  ⁡   (  H  )     [X,Y]\in\Gamma(H)   .  
 
 
 
 Obviously,     V  ​  F   VF    is integrable. 
 
 
   Claim 1.2.3   .  
 
     V  ​  F   VF     is integrable.  
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
  Suppose      X  ,  Y   ∈   Γ  ⁡   (   V  ​  F   )     X,Y\in\Gamma(VF)   . For the flow of    X  X   , we have  
 

  
  
        d   d  ​  t    ​   (   π  ∘   exp  ⁡   (   t  ​  X   )     )   ​   (  f  )    =    T    exp  ⁡   (   t  ​  X   )    ​   (  f  )     ​  π  ​   (   X  ⁡   (    exp  ⁡   (   t  ​  X   )    ​   (  f  )    )    )    =  0   ,   \frac{d}{dt}(\pi\circ\exp(tX))(f)=T_{\exp(tX)(f)}\pi\bigl(X(\exp(tX)(f))\bigr)=0,    
    
 
  so      π  ∘   exp  ⁡   (   t  ​  X   )     =  π   \pi\circ\exp(tX)=\pi    for all times for which the flow is defined. Therefore  
 

  
  
       T  ​  π  ​   (   [   X  ,  Y   ]   )    =    T  ​  π  ​   d   d  ​  t    ​  T  ​   (   exp  ⁡   (   −   t  ​  X    )    )    ∘  Y  ∘   exp  ⁡     (   t  ​  X   )   |    t  =  0      =     d   d  ​  t    ​  T  ​   (   π  ∘   exp  ⁡   (   −   t  ​  X    )     )    ∘  Y  ∘   exp  ⁡     (   t  ​  X   )   |    t  =  0      =     d   d  ​  t    ​  T  ​  π   ∘  Y  ∘   exp  ⁡     (   t  ​  X   )   |    t  =  0      =  0   ,   T\pi([X,Y])=T\pi\frac{d}{dt}T(\exp(-tX))\circ Y\circ\exp(tX)|_{t=0}=\frac{d}{dt}T(\pi\circ\exp(-tX))\circ Y\circ\exp(tX)|_{t=0}=\frac{d}{dt}T\pi\circ Y\circ\exp(tX)|_{t=0}=0,    
    
 
  where, in the last equality, we used that       T  ​  π   ∘  Y   =  0   T\pi\circ Y=0   .
∎  
 
 
 
 The question turns out to be more subtle for     H  ​  F   HF   . We begin by noting the following. (This proof is based on the discussion in  [ 9 ] .) 
 
 
   Claim 1.2.4   .  
 
  Let    H  H    be a distribution on    F  F   . For      X  ,  Y   ∈   Γ  ⁡   (  H  )     X,Y\in\Gamma(H)   ,       [   X  ,  Y   ]   ​   (  f  )   ​  mod  ​   H  f    ∈     T  f   ​  F   /   H  f     [X,Y](f)\;\mathrm{mod}\;H_{f}\in T_{f}F/H_{f}    depends only on the values of    X  X    and    Y  Y    at    f  f   . In particular, the question of involutivity can be answered pointwise.  
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
  Let     r  =   rank  ​  H    r=\mathrm{rank}\,H    and     n  =   dim  F    n=\dim F   . Choose a neighbourhood    U  U    of    f  f    and vector fields      E  1   ,  …  ,   E  r    E_{1},\ldots,E_{r}    spanning      H  |   U   H|_{U}   . Extend them to a local frame      E  1   ,  …  ,   E  n    E_{1},\ldots,E_{n}    of       T  ​  F   |   U   TF|_{U}   , and let      α   r  +  1    ,  …  ,   α  n    \alpha^{r+1},\ldots,\alpha^{n}    be the corresponding dual coframe elements annihilating      H  |   U   H|_{U}   . For      X  ,  Y   ∈   Γ  ⁡   (  H  )     X,Y\in\Gamma(H)   ,       α  j   ​   (  X  )    =    α  j   ​   (  Y  )    =  0   \alpha^{j}(X)=\alpha^{j}(Y)=0   , hence  
 

  
  
         α  j   ​   (   [   X  ,  Y   ]   )    =   −   d  ​   α  j   ​   (  X  ,  Y  )         (   j  =   r  +  1    ,  …  ,  n  )    .   \alpha^{j}([X,Y])=-d\alpha^{j}(X,Y)\qquad(j=r+1,\ldots,n).    
    
 
  The right-hand side at    f  f    depends only on     X  ⁡   (  f  )    X(f)    and     Y  ⁡   (  f  )    Y(f)   . The values of the     α  j   \alpha^{j}    determine the class of a tangent vector modulo     H  f   H_{f}   , so      [   X  ,  Y   ]   ​   (  f  )   ​  mod  ​   H  f    [X,Y](f)\;\mathrm{mod}\;H_{f}    depends only on     X  ⁡   (  f  )    X(f)    and     Y  ⁡   (  f  )    Y(f)   .
∎  
 
 
 
 Let us introduce the following    𝕜  \Bbbk   -bilinear map       𝒳  ⁡   (  F  )    ×   𝒳  ⁡   (  F  )      ⟶  R    Γ  ⁡   (   V  ​  F   )     \mathcal{X}(F)\times\mathcal{X}(F)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Gamma(VF)   ; 
 

  
  
       −   R  ⁡   (  X  ,  Y  )     :=    P  V   ​   (   [     P  H   ​  X   ,    P  H   ​  Y    ]   )     .   -R(X,Y):=P_{V}([P_{H}X,P_{H}Y]).    
    
 
 Because of claim   1.2.4  , the following is true. 
 
 
   Corollary 1.2.5   .  
 
  For      X  ,  Y   ∈   𝒳  ⁡   (  F  )     X,Y\in\mathcal{X}(F)   ,     R  ​   (  X  ,  Y  )   ​   (  f  )    R(X,Y)(f)    depends only on the values of    X  X    and    Y  Y    at    f  f   . We can therefore view    R  R    as a map     T  F  ∧     F   T  F   ⟶  R   V  F   TF\wedge_{F}TF\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R}}{{\longrightarrow}}VF    of vector bundles over    F  F   .  
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
      P  V   ​   (   [     P  H   ​  X   ,    P  H   ​  Y    ]   )    P_{V}([P_{H}X,P_{H}Y])     is the unique representative of      [     P  H   ​  X   ,    P  H   ​  Y    ]   ​  mod  ​  H  ​  F   [P_{H}X,P_{H}Y]\;\mathrm{mod}\;HF    in     ker  ​   P  H    \mathrm{ker}\;P_{H}   . Since       P  H   ​  X   ,     P  H   ​  Y   ∈   Γ  ⁡   (   H  ​  F   )      P_{H}X,P_{H}Y\in\Gamma(HF)   , claim   1.2.4   now tells us that we can see    R  R    as a map        H  ​  F   ⊗  H   ​  F    ⟶  R    V  ​  F    HF\otimes HF\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R}}{{\longrightarrow}}VF    of vector bundles over    F  F   . To complete the proof, make the observation that     R  ​   (  X  ,  Y  )   ​   (  f  )    R(X,Y)(f)    depends anti-symmetrically on    X  X    and    Y  Y   .
∎  
 
 
 
 This map is known as the  curvature  of the connection. Of course, by the theorem of Frobenius,    R  R    measures whether     H  ​  F   HF    is integrable. This is the case precisely if     R  =  0   R=0   . 
 
 
 
  1.2.3  Horizontal Lifts 

 
 It follows from the definition of a connection that the restriction of      T  f   ​  π   T_{f}\pi    to     H  ​   F  f    HF_{f}    defines a linear isomorphism to      T   π  ⁡   (  f  )     ​  M   T_{\pi(f)}M   . This allows us to lift a vector     v  ∈    T   π  ⁡   (  f  )     ​  M    v\in T_{\pi(f)}M    to a unique horizontal vector      v  hor   ∈   H  ​   F  f     v_{\mathrm{hor}}\in HF_{f}   . Let     X  ∈   𝒳  ⁡   (  M  )     X\in\mathcal{X}(M)    be a vector field. Then we can also construct its pointwise  horizontal lift      F   ⟶   X  hor     H  ​  F   ⊂   T  ​  F    F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle X_{\mathrm{hor}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}HF\subset TF   . This is again a smooth vector field since it is nothing other than              F  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}               F  ×  M   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\times M}                π  ∗   ​  T  ​  M   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}TM}                 H  ​  F   ⊂   T  ​  F    ,   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HF\subset TF,}                          id  F   ×  π   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{F}\times\pi}                        id  F   ×  X   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{F}\times X}                      ι  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}           
 where we understand      π  ∗   ​  T  ​  M   \pi^{*}TM    to be embedded in      F  ×  T   ​  M   F\times TM    and where      T  ​  M    ⟶   π  M    M   TM\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{M}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    is the bundle projection. Summarising, we have obtained a unique horizontal lift:              F  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}                H  ​  F   ⊂     {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HF\hskip 4.0pt\subset}               T  ​  F   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TF}                M  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}                T  ​  M   .   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM.}                         X  hor   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\mathrm{hor}}}                      π  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}                    ↺  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circlearrowleft}                       T  ​  π   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi}                      X  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}           
 
 
 How are the flows of these vector fields related? If    γ  \gamma    is a solution curve for the equation        d  ​  f   /  d   ​  t   =    X  hor   ​   (  f  )     df/dt=X_{\mathrm{hor}}(f)   , then     π  ∘  γ   \pi\circ\gamma    satisfies        d  ​  m   /  d   ​  t   =   X  ⁡   (  m  )     dm/dt=X(m)   , so we see that 
 

  
  
       π  ∘   e   t  ​   X  hor      =    e   t  ​  X    ∘  π    .   \pi\circ e^{tX_{\mathrm{hor}}}=e^{tX}\circ\pi.    
    
 
 Therefore, for any two      X  ,  Y   ∈   𝒳  ⁡   (  M  )     X,Y\in\mathcal{X}(M)   : 
 

  
  
        T  f   ​  π  ​   (    [    X  hor   ,   Y  hor    ]   ​   (  f  )    )    =    [   X  ,  Y   ]   ​   (   π  ⁡   (  f  )    )     .   T_{f}\pi([X_{\mathrm{hor}},Y_{\mathrm{hor}}](f))=[X,Y](\pi(f)).    
    
 
 We conclude that 
 

  
  
     −   R  ⁡   (    X  hor   ​   (  f  )    ,    Y  hor   ​   (  f  )    )     \displaystyle-R(X_{\mathrm{hor}}(f),Y_{\mathrm{hor}}(f))    
       =    P  V   ​   (   [    X  hor   ,   Y  hor    ]   )   ​   (  f  )     \displaystyle=P_{V}([X_{\mathrm{hor}},Y_{\mathrm{hor}}])(f)    
    
  
  
  
        =     [    X  hor   ,   Y  hor    ]   ​   (  f  )    −     [   X  ,  Y   ]   hor   ​   (  f  )      .   \displaystyle=[X_{\mathrm{hor}},Y_{\mathrm{hor}}](f)-[X,Y]_{\mathrm{hor}}(f).    
  
  (1.1)    
 
 where      T  ​  F    ⟶   P  V     V  ​  F    TF\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P_{V}}}{{\longrightarrow}}VF    is the projection onto the vertical bundle. We have found the following. 
 
 
   Corollary 1.2.6   .  
 
  If we use the identification       π  ∗   ​  T  ​  M    ≅  ι    H  ​  F   ⊂   T  ​  F    \pi^{*}TM\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota}}{{\cong}}HF\subset TF   , we can view    R  R    as a map       π  ∗   ​   (    T  ​  M   ∧   T  ​  M    )     ⟶  R    V  ​  F    \pi^{*}(TM\wedge TM)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R}}{{\longrightarrow}}VF    given by      (    X  m   ∧   Y  m    ,  f  )   ↦   (   X  ∧  Y   ,  f  )   ↦      [   X  ,  Y   ]   hor   ​   (  f  )    −    [    X  hor   ,   Y  hor    ]   ​   (  f  )      (X_{m}\wedge Y_{m},f)\mapsto(X\wedge Y,f)\mapsto[X,Y]_{\mathrm{hor}}(f)-[X_{\mathrm{hor}},Y_{\mathrm{hor}}](f)   , where     X  ,  Y   X,Y    are vector fields on    M  M    such that       X  ⁡   (  m  )    =   X  m    ,    Y  ⁡   (  m  )    =   Y  m   ∈    T  m   ​  M     X(m)=X_{m},Y(m)=Y_{m}\in T_{m}M    and     m  =   π  ⁡   (  f  )     m=\pi(f)   .  
 
 
 
 
  1.2.4  Connections and Trivialisations 

 
 The connections on a fibre bundle     F   ⟶  π   M   F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    form a sheaf. It is therefore very useful to understand what a connection looks like locally. 
 
 
 In particular, let us take a local trivialisation     (   U  α   ,   ψ  α   )   (U_{\alpha},\psi_{\alpha})    for our fibre bundle around a point     f  ∈  F   f\in F   . If we write    S  S    for the standard fibre,       F  |    U  α     ⟶   ψ  α      U  α   ×  S    F|_{U_{\alpha}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\psi_{\alpha}}}{{\longrightarrow}}U_{\alpha}\times S   . Then in this trivialisation 
 

  
  
     T  ​   ψ  α   ​   (     H  ​  F   |    U  α    )    \displaystyle T\psi_{\alpha}(HF|_{U_{\alpha}})    
       =    T  ​   ψ  α    ∘   ι  ⁡   (    π  ∗   ​   (     T  ​  M   |    U  α    )    )      \displaystyle=T\psi_{\alpha}\circ\iota(\pi^{*}(TM|_{U_{\alpha}}))    
    
  
  
  
       =   {    T  ​   ψ  α    ∘   ι  ⁡   (  X  ,  f  )     |     π  M   ​   (  X  )    =   π  ⁡   (  f  )     }    \displaystyle=\{T\psi_{\alpha}\circ\iota(X,f)\;|\;\pi_{M}(X)=\pi(f)\;\}    
    
  
  
  
        =   {    (  X  ,   −    Γ  α   ​   (  X  ,  s  )     )   ∈     T  ​   U  α    ×  T   ​  S    |    (  X  ,  s  )   ∈      T  ​  M   |    U  α    ×  S    }    ,   \displaystyle=\{(X,-\Gamma^{\alpha}(X,s))\in TU_{\alpha}\times TS\;|\;(X,s)\in TM|_{U_{\alpha}}\times S\},    
    
 
 for some smooth function         T  ​  M   |    U  α    ×  S    ⟶   Γ  α     T  ​  S    TM|_{U_{\alpha}}\times S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Gamma^{\alpha}}}{{\longrightarrow}}TS   , known as the Christoffel form for the trivialisation. This is of course defined as the composition               (  X  ,  s  )   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X,s)}               (  X  ,    ψ  α   −  1    ​   (    π  M   ​   (  X  )    ,  s  )    )   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X,\psi_{\alpha}^{-1}(\pi_{M}(X),s))}                    T  ​  M   |    U  α    ×  S   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM|_{U_{\alpha}}\times S}                 (    T  ​  M    ×  M   F   )   |    U  α    {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(TM\times_{M}F)|_{U_{\alpha}}}                    H  ​  F       ∩       T  ​  F      {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}HF\\
\cap\\
TF\end{array}}               ι  ⁡   (  X  ,    ψ  α   −  1    ​   (    π  M   ​   (  X  )    ,  s  )    )    {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota(X,\psi_{\alpha}^{-1}(\pi_{M}(X),s))}                     T  ​  M   |    U  α    ×  T   ​  S   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM|_{U_{\alpha}}\times TS}               (  X  ,   −    Γ  α   ​   (  X  ,  s  )     )   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X,-\Gamma^{\alpha}(X,s))}                 T  ​  S   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TS}                 Γ  α   ​   (  X  ,  s  )    .   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{\alpha}(X,s).}                                      id     T  ​  M   |    U  α     ×   (    ψ  α   −  1    ∘   (    π  M   ×   id  S    )    )    \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{TM|_{U_{\alpha}}}\times(\psi_{\alpha}^{-1}\circ(\pi_{M}\times\mathrm{id}_{S}))}                       Γ  α   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{\alpha}}                      ι  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}                                   T  ​   ψ  α    \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\psi_{\alpha}}                                   −   π   T  ​  S     \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\pi_{TS}}                       
 More explicitly, 
 

  
  
        Γ  α   ​   (  X  ,  s  )    =      π   T  ​  S    ∘  T   ​   ψ  α    ∘   P  V   ∘   T  ​   ψ  α   −  1    ​   (  X  ,   0  s   )      ,   \Gamma^{\alpha}(X,s)=\pi_{TS}\circ T\psi_{\alpha}\circ P_{V}\circ T\psi_{\alpha}^{-1}(X,0_{s}),    
    
 
 where        T  ​   U  α    ×  T   ​  S    ⟶   π   T  ​  S      T  ​  S    TU_{\alpha}\times TS\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{TS}}}{{\longrightarrow}}TS    is the projection onto the second component and     0  s   0_{s}    denotes the zero element in      T  s   ​  S   T_{s}S   .
Then the projection     P  V   P_{V}    looks as follows: 
 

  
  
          π   T  ​  S    ∘  T   ​   ψ  α    ∘   P  V   ∘   T  ​   ψ  α   −  1    ​   (  X  ,  η  )     =   η  +    Γ  α   ​   (  X  ,    π  S   ​   (  η  )    )      ,   \pi_{TS}\circ T\psi_{\alpha}\circ P_{V}\circ T\psi_{\alpha}^{-1}(X,\eta)=\eta+\Gamma^{\alpha}(X,\pi_{S}(\eta)),    
  
  (1.2)    
 
 where     η  ∈   T  ​  S    \eta\in TS    and      T  ​  S    ⟶   π  S    S   TS\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{S}}}{{\longrightarrow}}S    is the canonical projection. 
 
 
 
  1.2.5  The Pullback 

 
 The pullback is an essential tool when working with fibre bundles. It is a well-known fact that the categorical pullback of a fibre bundle along an arbitrary map exists in the smooth category. This follows from the fact that a fibre bundle is a submersion and therefore transversal to any map  13    13 
                 13 
                
                
                
              Remember that a pair of maps      M  1    ⟶   f  1    N   M_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}N   ,      M  2    ⟶   f  2    N   M_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}N    is said to be transversal if       im  ​   T   m  1    ​   f  1    +   im  ​   T   m  2    ​   f  2     =    T  n   ​  N    \mathrm{im}\;T_{m_{1}}f_{1}+\mathrm{im}\;T_{m_{2}}f_{2}=T_{n}N    for every triple      (   m  1   ,   m  2   ,  n  =   f  1    (   m  1   )   =   f  2    (   m  2   )   )   ∈   M  1   ×   M  2   ×  N   (m_{1},m_{2},n=f_{1}(m_{1})=f_{2}(m_{2}))\in M_{1}\times M_{2}\times N   . It is a direct consequence of the submersion theorem that the topological pullback of a pair of transversal maps is a smooth submanifold of      M  1   ×   M  2    M_{1}\times M_{2}   .   . Therefore, given a fibre bundle     F   ⟶  π   M   F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    with standard fibre    S  S    and a map     N   ⟶  ϕ   M   N\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi}}{{\longrightarrow}}M   , we can form a pullback                ϕ  ∗   ​  F   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{*}F}              F  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}                N  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}              M  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}                          π  ∗   ​  ϕ   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}\phi}                        ϕ  ∗   ​  π   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{*}\pi}               ⌟  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}                     π  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}                      ϕ  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}           
 in the smooth category. Moreover, we then have the following. 
 
 
   Claim 1.2.7   .  
 
  The pullback      ϕ  ∗   ​  π   \phi^{*}\pi    is a smooth fibre bundle over    N  N    with standard fibre    S  S   . Furthermore, if    π  \pi    is equipped with a connection     P  V   P_{V}   , then the pullback induces a unique connection      ϕ  ∗   ​   P  V    \phi^{*}P_{V}    on      ϕ  ∗   ​  π   \phi^{*}\pi    such that        T  ⁡   (    π  ∗   ​  ϕ   )    ∘   ϕ  ∗    ​   P  V    =    P  V   ∘   T  ⁡   (    π  ∗   ​  ϕ   )      T(\pi^{*}\phi)\circ\phi^{*}P_{V}=P_{V}\circ T(\pi^{*}\phi)   .  
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
  Choose some fibre bundle atlas     (   U  α   ,   ψ  α   )   (U_{\alpha},\psi_{\alpha})    for    π  \pi   . Then     (    ϕ   −  1    ​   (   U  α   )    ,   (    ϕ  ∗   ​  π   ,    π  S   ∘   ψ  α   ∘    π  ∗   ​  ϕ    )   )   (\phi^{-1}(U_{\alpha}),(\phi^{*}\pi,\pi_{S}\circ\psi_{\alpha}\circ\pi^{*}\phi))    is seen to be a fibre bundle atlas for      ϕ  ∗   ​  π   \phi^{*}\pi   .  
 
 
  Why is      ϕ  ∗   ​   P  V    \phi^{*}P_{V}    a connection, i.e. a projection      T  ⁡   (    ϕ  ∗   ​  F   )     ⟶      V  ⁡   (    ϕ  ∗   ​  F   )     T(\phi^{*}F)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}V(\phi^{*}F)   ? The main observation is that      T  ξ   ​   (    π  ∗   ​  ϕ   )    T_{\xi}(\pi^{*}\phi)    restricts to a linear isomorphism       (   V  ​   ϕ  ∗   ​  F   )   ξ   =    T  ξ   ​    (    ϕ  ∗   ​  F   )     ϕ  ∗   ​  π  ​   (  ξ  )       ⟶  ≅     T    π  ∗   ​  ϕ  ​   (  ξ  )     ​   F   π  ⁡   (    π  ∗   ​  ϕ  ​   (  ξ  )    )      =    (   V  ​  F   )     π  ∗   ​  ϕ  ​   (  ξ  )      (V\phi^{*}F)_{\xi}=T_{\xi}(\phi^{*}F)_{\phi^{*}\pi(\xi)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}T_{\pi^{*}\phi(\xi)}F_{\pi(\pi^{*}\phi(\xi))}=(VF)_{\pi^{*}\phi(\xi)}   . (Indeed,      π  ∗   ​  ϕ   \pi^{*}\phi    is an isomorphism       (    ϕ  ∗   ​  F   )     ϕ  ∗   ​  π  ​   (  ξ  )      ⟶      F   π  ⁡   (    π  ∗   ​  ϕ  ​   (  ξ  )    )      (\phi^{*}F)_{\phi^{*}\pi(\xi)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}F_{\pi(\pi^{*}\phi(\xi))}   .)  
 
 
  This means that the following commuting square uniquely defines      ϕ  ∗   ​   P  V    \phi^{*}P_{V}   .
