  Finally, note that        T  ξ   ​   (    π  ∗   ​  ϕ   )    ∘    (    ϕ  ∗   ​   P  V    )   ξ  2    =       (   P  V   )     π  ∗   ​  ϕ  ​   (  ξ  )     ∘   T  ξ    ​   (    π  ∗   ​  ϕ   )    ∘    (    ϕ  ∗   ​   P  V    )   ξ    =      (   P  V   )     π  ∗   ​  ϕ  ​   (  ξ  )    2   ∘   T  ξ    ​   (    π  ∗   ​  ϕ   )    =      (   P  V   )     π  ∗   ​  ϕ  ​   (  ξ  )     ∘   T  ξ    ​   (    π  ∗   ​  ϕ   )    =     T  ξ   ​   (    π  ∗   ​  ϕ   )    ∘    (    ϕ  ∗   ​   P  V    )   ξ     T_{\xi}(\pi^{*}\phi)\circ(\phi^{*}P_{V})_{\xi}^{2}=(P_{V})_{\pi^{*}\phi(\xi)}\circ T_{\xi}(\pi^{*}\phi)\circ(\phi^{*}P_{V})_{\xi}=(P_{V})_{\pi^{*}\phi(\xi)}^{2}\circ T_{\xi}(\pi^{*}\phi)=(P_{V})_{\pi^{*}\phi(\xi)}\circ T_{\xi}(\pi^{*}\phi)=T_{\xi}(\pi^{*}\phi)\circ(\phi^{*}P_{V})_{\xi}    and therefore, since       (   V  ​   ϕ  ∗   ​  F   )   ξ    ⟶    T  ξ   ​   (    π  ∗   ​  ϕ   )       (   V  ​  F   )     π  ∗   ​  ϕ  ​   (  ξ  )      (V\phi^{*}F)_{\xi}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle T_{\xi}(\pi^{*}\phi)}}{{\longrightarrow}}(VF)_{\pi^{*}\phi(\xi)}    is an isomorphism,       (    ϕ  ∗   ​   P  V    )   ξ  2   =    (    ϕ  ∗   ​   P  V    )   ξ    (\phi^{*}P_{V})_{\xi}^{2}=(\phi^{*}P_{V})_{\xi}   . The same defining square shows that      (    ϕ  ∗   ​   P  V    )   ξ   (\phi^{*}P_{V})_{\xi}    is the identity on      (   V  ​   ϕ  ∗   ​  F   )   ξ   (V\phi^{*}F)_{\xi}   ; hence its image is exactly     V  ​   ϕ  ∗   ​  F   V\phi^{*}F    and its kernel is a complementary horizontal subspace.
∎  
 
 
 
   Remark 1.2.8   .  
 
 Suppose we have two fibre bundles      F  1    ⟶   π  1    M   ⟵   π  2     F  2    F_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{2}}}{{\longleftarrow}}F_{2}   . We know that     F  ​   B  M    FB_{M}    has finite products. Now, suppose     π  i   \pi_{i}    is equipped with a connection      (   P  V   )   i   (P_{V})_{i}    and write      F  1    ⟵   π  1      F  1    ×  M    F  2     ⟶   π  2     F  2    F_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi^{1}}}{{\longleftarrow}}F_{1}\times_{M}F_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi^{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}F_{2}    for the projections of the product. Then we can construct a connection      π  1     (   P  V   )   1        ∗   +   π  2     (   P  V   )   2        ∗   =    (   P  V   )   1    ×  M     (   P  V   )   2    \pi^{1}{}^{*}(P_{V})_{1}+\pi^{2}{}^{*}(P_{V})_{2}=(P_{V})_{1}\times_{M}(P_{V})_{2}    on      F  1    ×  M    F  2    F_{1}\times_{M}F_{2}   , which is called the  product connection . 
 
 
 
 
  1.2.6  Parallel Transport 

 
 As noted above, there are many different (more or less) equivalent definitions of connections. I find one approach to be particularly intuitive, much more so than the one I have taken so far. It can be argued that a connection should be viewed as a rule that identifies fibres of a fibre bundle along paths in the base space    M  M   . This identification is called  parallel transport . 
 
 
 I will show how to derive a rule for parallel transport from our definition of a connection. Moreover, it can be shown that the connection can be recovered from its parallel transport. (See  [ 10 ] .) Apart from being a very intuitive approach to connections and being a very practical tool in, say, general relativity, this definition generalises well to higher categorical analogues of fibre bundles (which appear in higher gauge theory). (See  [ 26 ] .) 
 
 
 In section   1.2.3  , we have seen that we can construct a unique horizontal lift      X  hor   ∈   𝒳  ⁡   (  F  )     X_{\mathrm{hor}}\in\mathcal{X}(F)    for each vector field     X  ∈   𝒳  ⁡   (  M  )     X\in\mathcal{X}(M)   . This is by lifting each vector pointwise. Suppose we are given a curve      ]  a  ,  b  [    ⟶  c   M   ]a,b[\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\longrightarrow}}M   . Notice that the tangent vectors to this curve are elements of     T  ​  M   TM    and can therefore be uniquely lifted to vectors in      H  ​  F   ⊂   T  ​  F    HF\subset TF   . We can ask whether these vectors give rise to a (local) curve in    F  F   . The answer is the following. 
 
 
   Theorem 1.2.9     (Parallel transport) .  
 
  Let     H  ​  F   HF    be a connection on a fibre bundle     F   ⟶  π   M   F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    and let     I   ⟶  c   M   I\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    be a smooth curve. (Here,    I  I    is a connected neighbourhood of    0  0    in    ℝ  \mathbb{R}   .) Then there exists a neighbourhood     Ω  c   \Omega_{c}    of      F   c  ⁡   (  0  )     ×   {  0  }    F_{c(0)}\times\{0\}    in      F   c  ⁡   (  0  )     ×  I   F_{c(0)}\times I    and a smooth map      Ω  c    ⟶   T  c    F   \Omega_{c}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle T_{c}}}{{\longrightarrow}}F    such that       T  c   ​   (  f  ,  0  )    =  f   T_{c}(f,0)=f    and, for all      (  f  ,  t  )   ∈   Ω  c    (f,t)\in\Omega_{c}   :  
 
  1.  
 
      π  ⁡   (    T  c   ​   (  f  ,  t  )    )    =   c  ⁡   (  t  )     \pi(T_{c}(f,t))=c(t)    ; 
  
  
  2.  
 
       d   d  ​  t    ​   T  c   ​   (  f  ,  t  )    ∈   H  ​  F    \frac{d}{dt}T_{c}(f,t)\in HF    ; 
  
  
  3.  
 
    T  T     is reparametrisation invariant: If      J   ⟶  ϕ   I   J\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi}}{{\longrightarrow}}I     is an open interval, then, for all      s  ∈  J   s\in J    :        T  c   ​   (  f  ,   ϕ  ⁡   (  s  )    )    =    T   c  ∘  ϕ    ​   (    T  c   ​   (  f  ,   ϕ  ⁡   (  0  )    )    ,  s  )     T_{c}(f,\phi(s))=T_{c\circ\phi}(T_{c}(f,\phi(0)),s)    .  
  
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
  My favourite proof of this theorem is the following. We construct the following two pullbacks along    c  c   :                 c  ∗   ​  H  ​  F   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{*}HF}                c  ∗   ​  F   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{*}F}              I  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}                 H  ​  F   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HF}              F  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}               M  .   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M.}                          c  ∗   ​   π  F    \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{*}\pi_{F}}                        π  F  ∗   ​   π  ∗   ​  c   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{F}^{*}\pi^{*}c}               ⌟  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}                       c  ∗   ​  π   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{*}\pi}                        π  ∗   ​  c   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}c}               ⌟  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}                     c  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}                       π  F   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{F}}                      π  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}           
  Then      c  ∗   ​  H  ​  F   c^{*}HF    is a connection on       c  ∗   ​  F    ⟶    c  ∗   ​  π    I   c^{*}F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c^{*}\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}I   . Note that this distribution has dimension    1  1   , since it is isomorphic to       (    c  ∗   ​  π   )   ∗   ​  T  ​  I   (c^{*}\pi)^{*}TI   . This implies that it is involutive and by Frobenius, it is therefore integrable to a foliation    ℱ  \mathcal{F}    of      c  ∗   ​  F   c^{*}F    in    1  1   -dimensional leaves. Let     ℱ   f  ,  0    \mathcal{F}_{f,0}    denote the leaf of      (  f  ,  0  )   ∈    c  ∗   ​  F    (f,0)\in c^{*}F   . Then, since       T   (  f  ,  0  )    ​   ℱ   f  ,  0     =    c  ∗   ​  H  ​   F   (  f  ,  0  )      T_{(f,0)}\mathcal{F}_{f,0}=c^{*}HF_{(f,0)}    is an algebraic supplement to      ker  ⁡   T   (  f  ,  0  )     ​   c  ∗   ​  π   \ker T_{(f,0)}c^{*}\pi    (indeed,      c  ∗   ​  H  ​  F   c^{*}HF    is a connection), we see (by the inverse function theorem) that        c  ∗   ​  π   |    ℱ   f  ,  0     c^{*}\pi|_{\mathcal{F}_{f,0}}    defines a local diffeomorphism around     (  f  ,  0  )   (f,0)   . This map obviously satisfies the initial condition and conditions 1 and 2; the same argument after reparametrising    c  c    gives condition 3.
 
 The following is more explicit.
 Note that       c  ′   ​   (  t  )    ∈    T   c  ⁡   (  t  )     ​  M    c^{\prime}(t)\in T_{c(t)}M   . By using cut-off functions, we can extend      c  ′   ​   (  t  )    c^{\prime}(t)    locally to a time-dependent vector field     M  ⊃  U   ⟶   X  t       T  ​  M   |   U    M\supset U\stackrel{{\scriptstyle X_{t}}}{{\longrightarrow}}TM|_{U}   . Let     X   t  ,  hor    X_{t,\mathrm{hor}}    be its horizontal lift. The time-dependent ODE  
 

  
  
         d   d  ​  t    ​  f  ​   (  t  )    =    X   t  ,  hor    ​   (   f  ⁡   (  t  )    )     ,    f  ⁡   (  0  )    =  f    ,   \frac{d}{dt}f(t)=X_{t,\mathrm{hor}}(f(t)),\qquad f(0)=f,    
    
 
  has a unique local solution. Its projection solves       d   d  ​  t    ​  π  ​   (   f  ⁡   (  t  )    )    =    X  t   ​   (   π  ⁡   (   f  ⁡   (  t  )    )    )     \frac{d}{dt}\pi(f(t))=X_{t}(\pi(f(t)))    with initial value     c  ⁡   (  0  )    c(0)   , and hence equals     c  ⁡   (  t  )    c(t)    after shrinking the interval if necessary. Therefore       T  c   ​   (  f  ,  t  )    :=   f  ​   (  t  )     T_{c}(f,t):=f(t)    satisfies all conditions.
 
 Finally, the standard proof is the following.
 Let     (   U  α   ,   ψ  α   )   (U_{\alpha},\psi_{\alpha})    be a local trivialisation for    F  F    around     c  ⁡   (  0  )    c(0)   . Note that   2.   can equivalently be stated by demanding that      P  ⁡   (    d   d  ​  t    ​   T  c   ​   (  f  ,  t  )    )    =  0   P(\frac{d}{dt}T_{c}(f,t))=0   , if we write    P  P    for the projection onto the vertical bundle. In the local chart this condition becomes  
 

  
  
        d   d  ​  t    ​   Φ  c   ​   (   s  0   ,  t  )    =   −    Γ  α   ​   (    d   d  ​  t    ​  c  ​   (  t  )    ,    Φ  c   ​   (   s  0   ,  t  )    )      ,   \frac{d}{dt}\Phi_{c}(s_{0},t)=-\Gamma^{\alpha}(\frac{d}{dt}c(t),\Phi_{c}(s_{0},t)),    
    
 
  where we write      Φ  c   ​   (   s  0   ,  t  )    \Phi_{c}(s_{0},t)    for       π  S   ​   (    ψ  α   ​   (    T  c   ​   (    ψ  α   −  1    ​   (   c  ⁡   (  0  )    ,   s  0   )    ,  t  )    )    )    ∈  S   \pi_{S}(\psi_{\alpha}(T_{c}(\psi_{\alpha}^{-1}(c(0),s_{0}),t)))\in S   . Since     ψ  α   \psi_{\alpha}    is a diffeomorphism, we obtain our      Ω  c    ⟶   T  c    F   \Omega_{c}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle T_{c}}}{{\longrightarrow}}F    by       T  c   ​   (  f  ,  t  )    =    ψ  α   −  1    ​   (   c  ⁡   (  t  )    ,    Φ  c   ​   (    π  S   ​   (    ψ  α   ​   (  f  )    )    ,  t  )    )     T_{c}(f,t)=\psi_{\alpha}^{-1}(c(t),\Phi_{c}(\pi_{S}(\psi_{\alpha}(f)),t))   .  
 
 
  Property 3 is also easily checked. Indeed, write       c  hor   ​   (  t  )    :=    T  c   ​   (  f  ,  t  )     c_{\mathrm{hor}}(t):=T_{c}(f,t)    for a horizontal lift of    c  c   . Then       d   d  ​  s    ​   (    c  hor   ∘   ϕ  ⁡   (  s  )     )    =      ϕ  ′   ​   (  s  )    ⋅   c  hor  ′    ​   (   ϕ  ⁡   (  s  )    )     \frac{d}{ds}(c_{\mathrm{hor}}\circ\phi(s))=\phi^{\prime}(s)\cdot c_{\mathrm{hor}}^{\prime}(\phi(s))    and      π  ∘   c  hor   ∘  ϕ   =   c  ∘  ϕ    \pi\circ c_{\mathrm{hor}}\circ\phi=c\circ\phi   , so      c  hor   ∘  ϕ   c_{\mathrm{hor}}\circ\phi    is a horizontal lift of     c  ∘  ϕ   c\circ\phi   .   ∎  
 
 
 
      T  c   ​   (  f  ,  t  )    T_{c}(f,t)    is called the parallel transport of    f  f    along    c  c    over time    t  t   . Note that this parallel transport might not be defined for all     t  ∈  I   t\in I   .
 
  
 
 
 
  1.2.7  Complete Connections and Holonomy 

 
 Unfortunately, the existence of parallel transport is only proved locally by theorem   1.2.9  . Global transport might not be possible for all connections. However, many useful connections allow global parallel transport. Principal connections and affine connections, with the Levi-Civita connections of a (pseudo)-Riemannian manifold, come to mind. Such connections are said to be  complete . 
 
 
 Now, it is easily verified that, in the proof of theorem   1.2.9  , we only needed    c  c    to be a piecewise-    C  1   C^{1}   -curve  14    14 
                 14 
                
                
                
              It can be shown that one can equivalently take the curves to be     C  k   C^{k}   , for any     k  ∈   ℕ     >  0     k\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}   , or even     C  ∞   C^{\infty}   .  [ 18 ]    . Thus, for a complete connection, parallel transport along piecewise-    C  1   C^{1}    loops based at     m  ∈  M   m\in M    assigns to each such loop a diffeomorphism of the fibre     F  m   F_{m}   :               Loop  ⁡   (  M  ,  m  )    {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Loop}(M,m)}               Diff  ⁡   (   F  m   )    {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Diff}(F_{m})}                c  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}                 T  c   ​   (  −  ,  1  )    .   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{c}(-,1).}                         Hol  m   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hol}_{m}}                       
 This assignment is compatible with concatenation and reversal of paths, so its image is a subgroup of     Diff  ⁡   (   F  m   )    \mathrm{Diff}(F_{m})   , known as the  holonomy group  at    m  m   . This group is a useful tool for classification of fibre bundles with connections, (pseudo)-Riemannian manifolds with the Levi-Civita connection being an important example. 
 
 
 
  1.2.8  Curvature as Infinitesimal Holonomy 

 
 We have seen that holonomy cannot be defined for arbitrary connections, since the parallel transport might not be globally defined along any loop. However, if we make the loops small enough, one would expect that the transport would be possible. Here, ”small enough” will mean infinitesimal. We will see that this infinitesimal transport is nothing but the curvature, which, indeed, is defined for any connection. 
 
 
 The following result, in some sense, tells us that we can view the curvature as a limit of the holonomy if we take smaller and smaller loops. (We take loops corresponding to two commuting vector fields, or equivalently (by Frobenius), two local coordinate vector fields.) 
 
 
   Claim 1.2.10     (Curvature as infinitesimal holonomy) .  
 
  If      X  ,  Y   ∈   𝒳  ⁡   (  M  )     X,Y\in\mathcal{X}(M)    are two commuting vector fields, we have that  
 

  
  
       −    R  ⁡   (  X  ,  Y  )    ​   (  f  )     =       ∂  2    ∂   s  ​   ∂  t      ​   (    e   −   t  ​   Y  hor      ∘   e   −   s  ​   X  hor      ∘   e   t  ​   Y  hor     ∘    e   s  ​   X  hor     ​   (  f  )     )    |    s  =  t  =  0     .   -R(X,Y)(f)=\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial s\partial t}\left(e^{-tY_{\mathrm{hor}}}\circ e^{-sX_{\mathrm{hor}}}\circ e^{tY_{\mathrm{hor}}}\circ e^{sX_{\mathrm{hor}}}(f)\right)\Big|_{s=t=0}.    
    
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
  We have already derived formula   1.1  , which expresses the curvature in terms of the horizontal lifts of    X  X    and    Y  Y   . If      [   X  ,  Y   ]   =  0   [X,Y]=0   , this reduces to the equality  
 

  
  
       −    R  ⁡   (  X  ,  Y  )    ​   (  f  )     =    [    X  hor   ,   Y  hor    ]   ​   (  f  )     .   -R(X,Y)(f)=[X_{\mathrm{hor}},Y_{\mathrm{hor}}](f).    
    
 
  The claim follows by using the following well-known identity for the bracket of two vector fields:  
 

  
  
        [    X  hor   ,   Y  hor    ]   ​   (  f  )    =       ∂  2    ∂   s  ​   ∂  t      ​   (    e   −   t  ​   Y  hor      ∘   e   −   s  ​   X  hor      ∘   e   t  ​   Y  hor     ∘    e   s  ​   X  hor     ​   (  f  )     )    |    s  =  t  =  0     .   [X_{\mathrm{hor}},Y_{\mathrm{hor}}](f)=\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial s\partial t}\left(e^{-tY_{\mathrm{hor}}}\circ e^{-sX_{\mathrm{hor}}}\circ e^{tY_{\mathrm{hor}}}\circ e^{sX_{\mathrm{hor}}}(f)\right)\Big|_{s=t=0}.    
    
 
  ∎  
 
 
 
 
 
  1.3  Connections on Principal Bundles 

 
 Suppose that     P   ⟶   π  ~    M   P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{\pi}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    is a principal    G  G   -bundle. We ask ourselves what a good notion of a connection on a principal bundle is.    G  G   -equivariant connections turn out to be particularly interesting. Later, we will see that, in the case     G  =   G  ​  L  ​   (   𝕜  k   )     G=GL(\Bbbk^{k})   , precisely these connections correspond to the ordinary affine connections on the canonical associated vector bundle     P  ⁡   [  λ  ]    P[\lambda]   . In this sense, they are a generalisation of affine connections to general principal bundles. Before we explain the precise condition of equivariance that we put on the connection, however, let us formulate another, more conventional way of looking at connections on principal bundles. 
 
 
  1.3.1  Principal Connection 1-forms 

 
 We note that the principal right action (which we write as    ρ  \rho   ) defines fundamental vector fields for the Lie algebra    𝔤  \mathfrak{g}    of    G  G    as follows. For     Z  ∈  𝔤   Z\in\mathfrak{g}    and     p  ∈  P   p\in P   , we write       Z  P   ​   (  p  )    :=      d   d  ​  t    ​   ρ   exp  ⁡   (   t  ​  Z   )     ​   (  p  )    |    t  =  0    =   T  ​   ρ  p   ​   (  Z  )     Z_{P}(p):=\frac{d}{dt}\rho_{\exp(tZ)}(p)|_{t=0}=T\rho^{p}(Z)   , where     G   ⟶   ρ  p    P   G\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rho^{p}}}{{\longrightarrow}}P    denotes the smooth map     g  ↦   ρ  ⁡   (  p  ,  g  )     g\mapsto\rho(p,g)   . For a right action this assignment is a Lie-algebra homomorphism     𝔤   ⟶      𝒳  ⁡   (  P  )     \mathfrak{g}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{X}(P)   . Recall that, since the vertical bundle is integrable,     Γ  ⁡   (   V  ​  P   )    \Gamma(VP)    forms a Lie subalgebra of     𝒳  ⁡   (  P  )    \mathcal{X}(P)   . Now, since the orbits of the principal right action are the fibres of    P  P   , the same assignment gives a linear map     𝔤   ⟶      Γ  ⁡   (   V  ​  P   )     \mathfrak{g}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Gamma(VP)   . Moreover, this map trivialises     V  ​  P   VP   . 
 
 
   Claim 1.3.1   .  
 
  The map                P  ×  𝔤   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\times\mathfrak{g}}               V  ​  P   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces VP}                 (  p  ,  Z  )   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p,Z)}                Z  P   ​   (  p  )    {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{P}(p)}                        ζ  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}                       
  defines an isomorphism of vector bundles over    P  P   .  
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
    ζ  \zeta     respects the fibre, since       Z  P   ​   (  p  )    ∈   V  ​   P  p     Z_{P}(p)\in VP_{p}   . (Put differently,    ζ  \zeta    is the identity map on    P  P   .) Linearity on each fibre follows because     Z  ↦    Z  P   ​   (  p  )    =    T  e   ​   ρ  p   ​   (  Z  )     Z\mapsto Z_{P}(p)=T_{e}\rho^{p}(Z)    is linear:  
 

  
  
         (   Z  +   λ  ​   Z  ′     )   P   ​   (  p  )    =    T  e   ​   ρ  p   ​   (   Z  +   λ  ​   Z  ′     )    =     Z  P   ​   (  p  )    +   λ  ​   Z  P  ′   ​   (  p  )      .   (Z+\lambda Z^{\prime})_{P}(p)=T_{e}\rho^{p}(Z+\lambda Z^{\prime})=Z_{P}(p)+\lambda Z^{\prime}_{P}(p).    
    
 
  Let     p  ∈  P   p\in P    and let     (  U  ,  ψ  )   (U,\psi)    be a trivialisation of    P  P    around    p  p   .  
 
 
  Now,      ζ  ⁡   (  p  ,  Z  )    =   T  ​   ρ  p   ​   (  Z  )     \zeta(p,Z)=T\rho^{p}(Z)   . Note that     ρ  p   \rho^{p}    is a diffeomorphism     G   ⟶      P  p    G\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}P_{p}   . Therefore     𝔤  =    T  e   ​  G    ⟶    T  e   ​   ρ  p       T  p   ​   P  p    =   V  ​   P  p     \mathfrak{g}=T_{e}G\stackrel{{\scriptstyle T_{e}\rho^{p}}}{{\longrightarrow}}T_{p}P_{p}=VP_{p}    is an isomorphism of vector spaces.    ζ  \zeta    is obviously smooth, as      ζ  ⁡   (  p  ,  Z  )    =   T  ​   ρ  p   ​   (  Z  )    =   T  ​  ρ  ​   (   0  p   ,  Z  )     \zeta(p,Z)=T\rho^{p}(Z)=T\rho(0_{p},Z)   . Since    ζ  \zeta    is a smooth map of fibre bundles that is a diffeomorphism on both the base space (there it is even the identity) and on each fibre, we conclude that it is a diffeomorphism on the total space.
∎  
 
 
 
 There is a generalised notion of a differential form that is very useful in this situation. Let    M  M    be a manifold and let     V   ⟶  π   M   V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    be a vector bundle. We define a     π  \pi   -valued differential    k  k   -form  on    M  M    as a map    η  \eta    of vector bundles  15    15 
                 15 
                
                
                
              Note that we can equivalently view these    π  \pi   -valued differential forms as sections of the vector bundle     V  ⊗    ⋀  k     T  ∗   ​  M     V\otimes\bigwedge^{k}T^{*}M   .   :                ⋀  k    T  ​  M    {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigwedge^{k}TM}              V  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}                M  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}              M  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}                        η  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}                              π  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}                   
 The space of such forms is denoted      Ω  k   ​   (  M  ,  π  )    \Omega^{k}(M,\pi)   . In the case of a trivial vector bundle      M  ×  W    ⟶     M   M\times W\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    one also talks about    W  W   -valued differential forms and writes      Ω  k   ​   (  M  ,  W  )    \Omega^{k}(M,W)   . 
 
 
 The previous result tells us that we can equivalently see a (not necessarily principal) connection     P  V   P_{V}    and its curvature    R  R    as maps of vector bundles    ω  \omega    and     Ω  ω   \Omega^{\omega}   :               T  ​  P   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TP}               P  ×  𝔤   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\times\mathfrak{g}}              and  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{and}}                T  ​  P   ∧   T  ​  P    {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TP\wedge TP}               P  ×  𝔤   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\times\mathfrak{g}}                P  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}              P  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}              P  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}               P  .   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P.}                         ω  =    ζ   −  1    ∘   P  V     \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega=\zeta^{-1}\circ P_{V}}                            ↺  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circlearrowleft}                                Ω  ω   =    ζ   −  1    ∘  R    \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\omega}=\zeta^{-1}\circ R}                            ↺  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circlearrowleft}                                   
 We therefore have an interpretation of     P  V   P_{V}    as a    𝔤  \mathfrak{g}   -valued    1  1   -form     ω  ∈    Ω  1   ​   (  P  ,  𝔤  )     \omega\in\Omega^{1}(P;\mathfrak{g})   :       P  V   ​   (   X  p   )    =   ω  ​    (   X  p   )   P   ​   (  p  )     P_{V}(X_{p})=\omega(X_{p})_{P}(p)    and we can see    R  R    as a    𝔤  \mathfrak{g}   -valued    2  2   -form      Ω  ω   ∈    Ω  2   ​   (  P  ,  𝔤  )     \Omega^{\omega}\in\Omega^{2}(P;\mathfrak{g})   :
     R  ⁡   (   X  p   ,   Y  p   )    =     (    Ω  ω   ​   (   X  p   ,   Y  p   )    )   P   ​   (  p  )     R(X_{p},Y_{p})=(\Omega^{\omega}(X_{p},Y_{p}))_{P}(p)   . 
 
 
   Claim 1.3.2   .  
 
  Some     ω  ∈    Ω  1   ​   (  P  ,  𝔤  )     \omega\in\Omega^{1}(P,\mathfrak{g})    represents a connection precisely if      ω  ⁡   (   Z  P   )    =  Z   \omega(Z_{P})=Z    for all     Z  ∈  𝔤   Z\in\mathfrak{g}   .  
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
  If    ω  \omega    represents a connection, then      ζ  ∘  ω   =   P  V    \zeta\circ\omega=P_{V}    is the vertical projection. Hence      ζ  ⁡   (   ω  ⁡   (   Z  P   )    )    =    P  V   ​   (   Z  P   )    =   Z  P    \zeta(\omega(Z_{P}))=P_{V}(Z_{P})=Z_{P}   , because     Z  P   Z_{P}    is vertical. Since     Z  ↦    Z  P   ​   (  p  )     Z\mapsto Z_{P}(p)    is a linear isomorphism     𝔤  →   V  ​   P  p     \mathfrak{g}\to VP_{p}    for each     p  ∈  P   p\in P   , this implies      ω  ⁡   (   Z  P   )    =  Z   \omega(Z_{P})=Z   .  
 
 
  Conversely, suppose      ω  ⁡   (   Z  P   )    =  Z   \omega(Z_{P})=Z    for all     Z  ∈  𝔤   Z\in\mathfrak{g}    and put      P  V   :=   ζ  ∘  ω    P_{V}:=\zeta\circ\omega   . Then     P  V   P_{V}    restricts to the identity on     V  ​  P   VP   , since every vertical vector is of the form      Z  P   ​   (  p  )    Z_{P}(p)   . Thus      P  V  2   =   P  V    P_{V}^{2}=P_{V}    with image     V  ​  P   VP   , and therefore     P  V   P_{V}    is the vertical projection of a connection.
∎  
 
 
 
 We can also express     Ω  ω   \Omega^{\omega}    directly in terms of    ω  \omega   , like we expressed    R  R    in terms of     P  V   P_{V}   . 
 
 
   Claim 1.3.3   .  
 
  The following formula holds for the curvature:  
 

  
  
        Ω  ω   ​   (  X  ,  Y  )    =   −   ω  ⁡   (   [     P  H   ​  X   ,    P  H   ​  Y    ]   )      .   \Omega^{\omega}(X,Y)=-\omega([P_{H}X,P_{H}Y]).    
    
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
  Indeed,      −   ω  ​    (   [     P  H   ​  X   ,    P  H   ​  Y    ]   )   P     =   −    P  V   ​   (   [     P  H   ​  X   ,    P  H   ​  Y    ]   )     =   R  ⁡   (  X  ,  Y  )    =    Ω  ω   ​    (  X  ,  Y  )   P     -\omega([P_{H}X,P_{H}Y])_{P}=-P_{V}([P_{H}X,P_{H}Y])=R(X,Y)=\Omega^{\omega}(X,Y)_{P}   . Again, since    ζ  \zeta    is an isomorphism, the conclusion follows.∎  
 
 
 
 Now, let us formulate what we mean precisely when we say that a connection is equivariant. We say that    P  P    is equipped with a  principal connection , or  equivariant connection , if      H  ​  P    ⟵   P  H     T  ​  P    ⟶   P  V     V  ​  P    HP\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P_{H}}}{{\longleftarrow}}TP\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P_{V}}}{{\longrightarrow}}VP    is equivariant in the following sense:       P  V   ∘   T  ⁡   (   ρ  g   )     =    T  ⁡   (   ρ  g   )    ∘   P  V     P_{V}\circ T(\rho_{g})=T(\rho_{g})\circ P_{V}   , for all     g  ∈  G   g\in G   . (Here I write     ρ   (  −  )    \rho_{(-)}    for the principal right action to be explicit.) Note that we can equivalently demand that horizontal lifts are    G  G   -equivariant or that parallel transport along any curve is. We must ask ourselves what it means for    ω  \omega   , if we are dealing with a principal connection. The answer turns out to be as follows. 
 
 
 Remember that we have the so-called  adjoint representation  of    G  G    on its Lie algebra    𝔤  \mathfrak{g}                 G  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}               G  ​  L  ​   (  𝔤  )    {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GL(\mathfrak{g})}                g  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}                 T  e   ​   C  g    ,   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{e}C_{g},}                        Ad  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Ad}}                       
 where     C     g   =  g  ⋅  −  ⋅  g      −  1     C_{g}=g\cdot-\cdot g^{-1}    is the conjugation map. 
 
 
   Claim 1.3.4   .  
 
  Suppose that     ω  ∈    Ω  1   ​   (  P  ,  𝔤  )     \omega\in\Omega^{1}(P,\mathfrak{g})    is the    𝔤  \mathfrak{g}   -valued 1-form corresponding to a (not necessarily principal) connection     P  V   P_{V}   . Then this is precisely a principal connection if    ω  \omega    is    G  G   -equivariant in the sense that for all     g  ∈  G   g\in G    the following diagram commutes:                T  ​  P   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TP}               T  ​  P   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TP}                𝔤  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}}               𝔤  ,   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g},}                         T  ​   ρ  g    \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\rho_{g}}                      ω  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}                      ω  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}                       Ad  ⁡   (   g   −  1    )    \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})}           
  where     G   ⟶  Ad    G  ​  L  ​   (  𝔤  )     G\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{Ad}}}{{\longrightarrow}}GL(\mathfrak{g})    denotes the adjoint representation of    G  G   .  
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
  Let     X  ∈   𝒳  ⁡   (  P  )     X\in\mathcal{X}(P)   . Then  
 

  
  
      ω  ​    (   T  ​   ρ  g   ​   (  X  )    )   P    ∘   ρ  g    \displaystyle\omega(T\rho_{g}(X))_{P}\circ\rho_{g}    
       =    P  V   ​   (   T  ​   ρ  g   ​   (  X  )    )     \displaystyle=P_{V}(T\rho_{g}(X))    
    
  
  
  
     =  T   ρ  g    (   P  V    (  X  )   )    (equivariance of    P  V   )      \displaystyle=T\rho_{g}(P_{V}(X))\quad\textnormal{(equivariance of $P_{V}$)}\quad    
    
  
  
  
       =   T  ​   ρ  g   ​   (   ω  ​    (  X  )   P    )     \displaystyle=T\rho_{g}(\omega(X)_{P})    
    
  
  
  
       =    T  ​   ρ  g    ∘       d   d  ​  t     ​   ρ   exp  ⁡   (   t  ​  ω  ​   (  X  )    )      |    t  =  0      \displaystyle=T\rho_{g}\circ\frac{d}{dt}\rho_{\exp(t\omega(X))}|_{t=0}    
    
  
  
  
       =      d   d  ​  t     ​   ρ  g    ∘     ρ   exp  ⁡   (   t  ​  ω  ​   (  X  )    )     |    t  =  0      \displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\rho_{g}\circ\rho_{\exp(t\omega(X))}|_{t=0}    
    
  
  
  
       =      d   d  ​  t     ​   ρ  g    ∘   ρ   exp  ⁡   (   t  ​  ω  ​   (  X  )    )     ∘     ρ   g   −  1     |    t  =  0    ∘   ρ  g     \displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\rho_{g}\circ\rho_{\exp(t\omega(X))}\circ\rho_{g^{-1}}|_{t=0}\circ\rho_{g}    
    
  
  
  
       =        d   d  ​  t     ​   ρ    g   −  1    ​   exp  ⁡   (   t  ​  ω  ​   (  X  )    )    ​  g     |    t  =  0    ∘   ρ  g     \displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\rho_{g^{-1}\exp(t\omega(X))g}|_{t=0}\circ\rho_{g}    
    
  
  
  
     =    d   d  ​  t     ρ      exp  ⁡   (   t  ​  Ad  ​   (   g   −  1    )   ​  ω  ​   (  X  )    )     |      t  =  0    ∘  ρ     g   (*)     \displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\rho_{\exp(t\,\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})\omega(X))}|_{t=0}\circ\rho_{g}\quad\textnormal{(*)}\quad    
    
  
  
  
        =     (    Ad  ⁡   (   g   −  1    )    ​  ω  ​   (  X  )    )   P   ∘   ρ  g     ,   \displaystyle=(\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})\omega(X))_{P}\circ\rho_{g},    
    
 
  where, in     (  ∗  )   (*)   , we use the fact that      Ad  ⁡   (  g  )    =    T  e   ​   C  g     \mathrm{Ad}(g)=T_{e}C_{g}   , if      C  g   :   h  ↦   g  ​  h  ​   g   −  1       C_{g}:h\mapsto ghg^{-1}   , which implies that               G  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}              G  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}                𝔤  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}}               𝔤  .   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}.}                         C  g   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{g}}                      exp  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp}                         T  e   ​   C  g    =   Ad  ⁡   (  g  )     \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{e}C_{g}=\mathrm{Ad}(g)}                    ↺  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circlearrowleft}                      exp  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp}           
  Finally, since     ρ  g   \rho_{g}    is a diffeomorphism, equivariance of     P  V   P_{V}    implies the displayed equivariance of    ω  \omega   . Conversely, if    ω  \omega    satisfies the displayed equivariance, the same computation read backwards gives       P  V   ​   (   T  ​   ρ  g   ​   (  X  )    )    =   T  ​   ρ  g   ​   (    P  V   ​   (  X  )    )     P_{V}(T\rho_{g}(X))=T\rho_{g}(P_{V}(X))   . Hence     P  V   P_{V}    is equivariant, and the claim follows.
∎  
 
 
 
 In general, given a representation    λ  \lambda    of    G  G    on a vector space    W  W   , we say that     α  ∈    Ω  k   ​   (  P  ,  W  )     \alpha\in\Omega^{k}(P,W)    is an  equivariant differential form  if      α  ⁡   (   T  ​   ρ  g   ​   (   X  1   )    ,  …  ,   T  ​   ρ  g   ​   (   X  k   )    )    =    λ  ⁡   (   g   −  1    )    ​  α  ​   (   X  1   ,  …  ,   X  k   )     \alpha(T\rho_{g}(X_{1}),\ldots,T\rho_{g}(X_{k}))=\lambda(g^{-1})\alpha(X_{1},\ldots,X_{k})   , for all     g  ∈  G   g\in G   . 
 
 
   Claim 1.3.5   .  
 
  For a principal connection    ω  \omega    on a principal bundle    P  P   ,      ℒ   Z  P    ω  =  −  ad   (  Z  )   ∘  ω   \mathcal{L}_{Z_{P}}\omega=-\mathrm{ad}(Z)\circ\omega   .  
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 

  
  
      ℒ   Z  P    ​  ω   \displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{Z_{P}}\omega    
       :     =     d   d  ​  t     ​   exp  ⁡       (   t  ​   Z  P    )   ∗   ​  ω   |    t  =  0        \displaystyle:=\frac{d}{dt}\exp(tZ_{P})^{*}\omega|_{t=0}    
    
  
  
  
       =       d   d  ​  t     ​  ω   ∘  T   ​   exp  ⁡     (   t  ​   Z  P    )   |    t  =  0       \displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\omega\circ T\exp(tZ_{P})|_{t=0}    
    
  
  
  
       =      d   d  ​  t     ​  ω   ∘     T  ​   ρ   exp  ⁡   (   t  ​  Z   )      |    t  =  0      \displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\omega\circ T\rho_{\exp(tZ)}|_{t=0}    
    
  
  
  
     =    d   d  ​  t     Ad   (  exp   (  −  t  Z  )   )   ∘  ω   |   t  =  0     (by   G  -equivariance)      \displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\mathrm{Ad}(\exp(-tZ))\circ\omega|_{t=0}\quad\textnormal{(by $G$-equivariance)}\quad    
    
  
  
  
     =  −  ad   (  Z  )   ∘  ω  .   \displaystyle=-\mathrm{ad}(Z)\circ\omega.    
    
 
  Here the last equality holds by the chain rule. (Recall that     ad  :=    T  e   ​  Ad    \mathrm{ad}:=T_{e}\mathrm{Ad}   .)
∎  
 
 
 
 The bracket on    𝔤  \mathfrak{g}    defines a bracket on the graded vector space      Ω  ⁡   (  M  ,  𝔤  )    :=    ⨁   k  ∈  ℕ      Ω  k   ​   (  M  ,  𝔤  )      \Omega(M,\mathfrak{g}):=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\Omega^{k}(M,\mathfrak{g})    of all    𝔤  \mathfrak{g}   -valued differential forms on a manifold    M  M   , making it into a graded Lie algebra. For     α  ∈    Ω  k   ​   (  M  ,  𝔤  )     \alpha\in\Omega^{k}(M,\mathfrak{g})    and     β  ∈    Ω  l   ​   (  M  ,  𝔤  )     \beta\in\Omega^{l}(M,\mathfrak{g})   , we define      [   α  ,  β   ]   ∈    Ω   k  +  l    ​   (  M  ,  𝔤  )     [\alpha,\beta]\in\Omega^{k+l}(M,\mathfrak{g})    by 
 

  
  
        [   α  ,  β   ]   ​   (   X  1   ,  …  ,   X   k  +  l    )    :=    1     k  !   ​  l   !    ​    ∑  σ    sign  ​  σ  ​   [    α  ⁡   (   X   σ  ⁡   (  1  )     ,  …  ,   X   σ  ⁡   (  k  )     )    ,   β  ⁡   (   X   σ  ⁡   (   k  +  1   )     ,  …  ,   X   σ  ⁡   (   k  +  l   )     )     ]       ,   [\alpha,\beta](X_{1},\ldots,X_{k+l}):=\frac{1}{k!l!}\sum_{\sigma}\mathrm{sign}\,\sigma[\alpha(X_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,X_{\sigma(k)}),\beta(X_{\sigma(k+1)},\ldots,X_{\sigma(k+l)})],    
    
 
 where    σ  \sigma    ranges over all permutations of     {   1  ,  …  ,   k  +  l    }   \{1,\ldots,k+l\}    and      X  i   ∈   T  ​  M    X_{i}\in TM   . 
 
 
 The ordinary exterior derivative extends componentwise to    𝔤  \mathfrak{g}   -valued forms. For     α  ∈    Ω  k   ​   (  M  ,  𝔤  )     \alpha\in\Omega^{k}(M,\mathfrak{g})    and     β  ∈    Ω  l   ​   (  M  ,  𝔤  )     \beta\in\Omega^{l}(M,\mathfrak{g})    it satisfies the graded Leibniz rule 
 

  
  
       d  ⁡   [   α  ,  β   ]    =    [    d  ​  α   ,  β   ]   +     (   −  1   )   k   ​   [   α  ,   d  ​  β    ]      .   d[\alpha,\beta]=[d\alpha,\beta]+(-1)^{k}[\alpha,d\beta].    
    
 
 For a    𝔤  \mathfrak{g}   -valued    1  1   -form,      d  ​  ω  ​   (  X  ,  Y  )    :=     ℒ  X   ​   (   ω  ⁡   (  Y  )    )    −    ℒ  Y   ​   (   ω  ⁡   (  X  )    )    −   ω  ⁡   (   [   X  ,  Y   ]   )      d\omega(X,Y):=\mathcal{L}_{X}(\omega(Y))-\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\omega(X))-\omega([X,Y])   . This implies that the following proposition holds for    G  G   -equivariant connections. 
 
 
 First, however, we introduce one more notation that makes the proposition somewhat cleaner. Moreover, the notation is used in practically all physics literature on gauge theory. Recall that we used     P  H   P_{H}    to designate the projection onto the horizontal bundle. This allows us to define a map 
 

  
  
      Ω  ⁡   (  P  ,  W  )     ⟶   P  H  ∗     Ω  ⁡   (  P  ,  W  )     \Omega(P,W)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P_{H}^{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Omega(P,W)    
    
 
 

  
  
        (    P  H  ∗   ​  α   )   ​   (   X  1   ,  …  ,   X  k   )    :=   α  ⁡   (    P  H   ​   (   X  1   )    ,  …  ,    P  H   ​   (   X  k   )    )     .   (P_{H}^{*}\alpha)(X_{1},\ldots,X_{k}):=\alpha(P_{H}(X_{1}),\ldots,P_{H}(X_{k})).    
    
 
 We might call this the projection onto the subspace      Ω  hor   ​   (  P  ,  W  )    \Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}(P,W)    of  horizontal differential forms . Obviously, these are precisely the differential forms that vanish on vertical vectors. Finally, we define the  exterior covariant derivative  or  covariant differential  associated with the connection    ω  \omega    as the map       Ω  k   ​   (  P  ,  W  )     ⟶   d  ω      Ω   k  +  1    ​   (  P  ,  W  )     \Omega^{k}(P,W)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d^{\omega}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Omega^{k+1}(P,W)   , given by      d  ω   :=    P  H  ∗   ∘  d    d^{\omega}:=P_{H}^{*}\circ d   . 
 
 
   Theorem 1.3.6     (Cartan’s second structural equation) .  
 
  For a principal connection    ω  \omega    on    P  P    the following formula holds for the curvature:
     Ω  ω   =    d  ​  ω   +    1  2   ​   [   ω  ,  ω   ]     =    d  ω   ​  ω    \Omega^{\omega}=d\omega+\frac{1}{2}[\omega,\omega]=d^{\omega}\omega   .  
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
  Let     X  ,  Y   X,Y    be horizontal vector fields on    P  P   . Then      R  ⁡   (  X  ,  Y  )    :=   −    P  V   ​   (   [   X  ,  Y   ]   )     =    (   −   ω  ⁡   (   [   X  ,  Y   ]   )     )   P    R(X,Y):=-P_{V}([X,Y])=(-\omega([X,Y]))_{P}    and  
 

  
  
      (    d  ​  ω   +     1  2    ​   [   ω  ,  ω   ]     )   ​   (  X  ,  Y  )    \displaystyle(d\omega+\frac{1}{2}[\omega,\omega])(X,Y)    
       =      ℒ  X   ​   (   ω  ⁡   (  Y  )    )    −    ℒ  Y   ​   (   ω  ⁡   (  X  )    )    −   ω  ⁡   (   [   X  ,  Y   ]   )     +   [   ω  ⁡   (  X  )    ,   ω  ⁡   (  Y  )    ]     \displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{X}(\omega(Y))-\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\omega(X))-\omega([X,Y])+[\omega(X),\omega(Y)]    
    
  
  
  
        =   −   ω  ⁡   (   [   X  ,  Y   ]   )      ,   \displaystyle=-\omega([X,Y]),    
    
 
  since     0  =    P  V   ​  X   =    (   ω  ⁡   (  X  )    )   P    0=P_{V}X=(\omega(X))_{P}    implies that      ω  ⁡   (  X  )    =  0   \omega(X)=0    and similarly for    Y  Y   . So on horizontal vector fields, both expressions agree.
 
 Now note that, since the principal right action is transitive on the fibre, any vertical vector      Z  ′   ∈   V  ​   P  p     Z^{\prime}\in VP_{p}    can be written as      Z  P   ​   (  p  )    Z_{P}(p)    for some     Z  ∈  𝔤   Z\in\mathfrak{g}   . Now, trivially,       i   Z  P    ​  R   =    i    P  H   ​   Z  P     ​  R   =  0   i_{Z_{P}}R=i_{P_{H}Z_{P}}R=0   , so       i   Z  P    ​   Ω  ω    =  0   i_{Z_{P}}\Omega^{\omega}=0   .  
 
 
  On the other hand, since       i   Z  P    ​  ω   =  Z   i_{Z_{P}}\omega=Z   , we have that       i   Z  P    ​  d  ​  ω   =    ℒ   Z  P    ​  ω    i_{Z_{P}}d\omega=\mathcal{L}_{Z_{P}}\omega   . So  
 

  
  
      i   Z  P    ​   (    d  ​  ω   +     1  2    ​   [   ω  ,  ω   ]     )    \displaystyle i_{Z_{P}}(d\omega+\frac{1}{2}[\omega,\omega])    
       =     ℒ   Z  P    ​  ω   +     1  2    ​   i   Z  P    ​   [   ω  ,  ω   ]      \displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{Z_{P}}\omega+\frac{1}{2}i_{Z_{P}}[\omega,\omega]    
    
  
  
  
       =     ℒ   Z  P    ​  ω   +   [   Z  ,  ω   ]     \displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{Z_{P}}\omega+[Z,\omega]    
    
  
  
  
       =     ℒ   Z  P    ​  ω   +    ad  ⁡   (  Z  )    ∘  ω     \displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{Z_{P}}\omega+\mathrm{ad}(Z)\circ\omega    
    
 
  (We mean     [  Z  ,  ω  ]   [Z,\omega]    to be the pointwise    𝔤  \mathfrak{g}   -Lie bracket.) This last equality is the well-known result on the adjoint representation. Since    ω  \omega    is    G  G   -equivariant, claim   1.3.5   tells us that this vanishes. Both expressions therefore vanish on vertical vectors. By noting that both are      C  ∞   ​   (  P  )    C^{\infty}(P)   -bilinear maps, the first equality in the theorem follows.  
 
 
  For the second one, note that  
 

  
  
      d  ω   ​  ω  ​   (   X  ∧  Y   )    \displaystyle d^{\omega}\omega(X\wedge Y)    
       =    (    P  H  ∗   ​  d  ​  ω   )   ​   (   X  ∧  Y   )     \displaystyle=(P_{H}^{*}d\omega)(X\wedge Y)    
    
  
  
  
       =   d  ​  ω  ​   (     P  H   ​   (  X  )    ∧    P  H   ​   (  Y  )     )     \displaystyle=d\omega(P_{H}(X)\wedge P_{H}(Y))    
    
  
  
  
       =     ℒ    P  H   ​   (  X  )     ​  ω  ​   (    P  H   ​   (  Y  )    )    −    ℒ    P  H   ​   (  Y  )     ​  ω  ​   (    P  H   ​   (  X  )    )    −   ω  ⁡   (   [     P  H   ​   (  X  )    ,    P  H   ​   (  Y  )     ]   )      \displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{P_{H}(X)}\omega(P_{H}(Y))-\mathcal{L}_{P_{H}(Y)}\omega(P_{H}(X))-\omega([P_{H}(X),P_{H}(Y)])    
    
  
  
  
     =  −  ω   (   [   P  H    (  X  )   ,   P  H    (  Y  )   ]   )    (since   ω   vanishes on horizontal vectors)     .   \displaystyle=-\omega([P_{H}(X),P_{H}(Y)])\quad\textnormal{(since $\omega$ vanishes on horizontal vectors)}\quad.    
    
 
  Note that this expression is equal to     −   ω  ⁡   (   [   X  ,  Y   ]   )     -\omega([X,Y])    (and therefore agrees with the expression above) if     X  ,  Y   X,Y    are horizontal vectors. Moreover it obviously vanishes on vertical vectors. We conclude that the second identity in the theorem holds.
∎  
 
 
 
 There is one more identity regarding the connection that I would like to show, since it is of fundamental importance in physics. It will give us one of the two equations governing the evolution of gauge fields. 
 
 
   Theorem 1.3.7     (Bianchi Identity) .  
 
  The curvature form     Ω  ω   \Omega^{\omega}    of a principal connection    ω  \omega    satisfies the following equation, known as the Bianchi Identity:  
 

  
  
        d  ω   ​   Ω  ω    =  0   .   d^{\omega}\Omega^{\omega}=0.    
    
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
  We have, using the previous result,  
 

  
  
      d  ω   ​   Ω  ω    \displaystyle d^{\omega}\Omega^{\omega}    
       =    d  ω   ​   (    d  ​  ω   +     1  2    ​   [   ω  ,  ω   ]     )     \displaystyle=d^{\omega}(d\omega+\frac{1}{2}[\omega,\omega])    
    
  
  
  
       =     P  H  ∗   ​  d  ​  d  ​  ω   +     1  2    ​   P  H  ∗   ​  d  ​   [   ω  ,  ω   ]      \displaystyle=P_{H}^{*}dd\omega+\frac{1}{2}P_{H}^{*}d[\omega,\omega]    
    
  
  
  
       =     1  2    ​   P  H  ∗   ​   (    [    d  ​  ω   ,  ω   ]   −   [   ω  ,   d  ​  ω    ]    )     \displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}P_{H}^{*}([d\omega,\omega]-[\omega,d\omega])    
    
  
  
  
       =    P  H  ∗   ​   [    d  ​  ω   ,  ω   ]     \displaystyle=P_{H}^{*}[d\omega,\omega]    
    
  
  
  
       =   [     P  H  ∗   ​  d  ​  ω   ,    P  H  ∗   ​  ω    ]    \displaystyle=[P_{H}^{*}d\omega,P_{H}^{*}\omega]    
    
  
  
  
        =  0   .   \displaystyle=0.    
    
 
  The last identity, of course, holds since    ω  \omega    vanishes on horizontal vectors.
∎  
 
 
 
 It should be noted that any connection on a fibre bundle satisfies a variant of this equation: it is not special to the case of a principal connection. However, the general statement would be much more involved. For more information, see  [ 19 ] .
 
 To express the second field equation (known as the Yang-Mills equation) we need a few more definitions. Let us, following convention, first introduce the notation      Ω  hor  k   ​    (  P  ,  W  )   G    \Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{k}(P,W)^{G}    for the space of    W  W   -valued    k  k   -forms that are both horizontal (vanish on vertical vectors) and equivariant. This is a very important class of differential forms. For instance, note that      Ω  ω   ∈    Ω  hor  2   ​    (  P  ,  𝔤  )   G     \Omega^{\omega}\in\Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{2}(P,\mathfrak{g})^{G}   . We have a similar result for    ω  \omega   . 
 
 
   Claim 1.3.8   .  
 
  Let    ω  \omega    be a principal connection on     P   ⟶   π  ~    M   P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{\pi}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M   . Then another      ω  ′   ∈    Ω  1   ​   (  P  ,  𝔤  )     \omega^{\prime}\in\Omega^{1}(P,\mathfrak{g})    is a principal connection precisely if      ω  −   ω  ′    ∈    Ω  hor  1   ​    (  P  ,  𝔤  )   G     \omega-\omega^{\prime}\in\Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{1}(P,\mathfrak{g})^{G}   . In particular, after fixing one principal connection, the space of principal connections is an affine space modelled on      Ω  hor  1   ​    (  P  ,  𝔤  )   G    \Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{1}(P,\mathfrak{g})^{G}   .  
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
  Obviously,     ω  ′   \omega^{\prime}    is precisely equivariant if     ω  −   ω  ′    \omega-\omega^{\prime}    is. Moreover, for      X  V   ∈   V  ​  P    X_{V}\in VP          ζ  ∘   ω  ′    ​   (   X  V   )    =     ζ  ∘   (    ω  ′   −  ω   )    ​   (   X  V   )    +   ζ  ∘   ω  ⁡   (   X  V   )       =  !    ζ  ∘   ω  ⁡   (   X  V   )     =    P  V   ​   (   X  V   )    =   X  V    \zeta\circ\omega^{\prime}(X_{V})=\zeta\circ(\omega^{\prime}-\omega)(X_{V})+\zeta\circ\omega(X_{V})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}\zeta\circ\omega(X_{V})=P_{V}(X_{V})=X_{V}   , where     =  !   \stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}    holds precisely if     ω  −   ω  ′    \omega-\omega^{\prime}    is horizontal.∎  
 
 
 
 Let us write     𝒞  ⁡   (  P  )    \mathscr{C}(P)    for the subset of      Ω  1   ​   (  P  ,  𝔤  )    \Omega^{1}(P,\mathfrak{g})    of principal connections. In fact, this is an (infinite-dimensional) submanifold of      Ω  1   ​   (  P  ,  𝔤  )    \Omega^{1}(P,\mathfrak{g})   .  [ 6 ]  We can interpret      Ω  hor  1   ​    (  P  ,  𝔤  )   G    \Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{1}(P,\mathfrak{g})^{G}    as its tangent space. Indeed, let     ]  −  ϵ  ,  ϵ  [   ⟶  γ   𝒞   (  P  )    ]-\epsilon,\epsilon[\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\gamma}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathscr{C}(P)    be a path. Then,      γ  ⁡   (  t  )    =    γ  ⁡   (  0  )    +   δ  ⁡   (  t  )      \gamma(t)=\gamma(0)+\delta(t)   , with     ]  −  ϵ  ,  ϵ  [   ⟶  δ    Ω  hor  1     (  P  ,  𝔤  )   G    ]-\epsilon,\epsilon[\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\delta}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{1}(P,\mathfrak{g})^{G}   . We see that       γ  ′   ​   (  0  )    ∈    T  0   ​   Ω  hor  1   ​    (  P  ,  𝔤  )   G    ≅    Ω  hor  1   ​    (  P  ,  𝔤  )   G     \gamma^{\prime}(0)\in T_{0}\Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{1}(P,\mathfrak{g})^{G}\cong\Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{1}(P,\mathfrak{g})^{G}   . 
 
 
   Remark 1.3.9   .  
 
 I can imagine the definition of      Ω  hor  k   ​    (  P  ,  W  )   G    \Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{k}(P,W)^{G}    can seem somewhat far-fetched. How precisely should we think about these differential forms? To answer this question note that, by claim   1.1.14  , we have a natural identification       Ω  hor  0   ​    (  P  ,  W  )   G    =    C  ∞   ​    (  P  ,  W  )   G    ≅   Γ  ⁡   (   P  ⁡   [  λ  ]    )     \Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{0}(P,W)^{G}=C^{\infty}(P,W)^{G}\cong\Gamma(P[\lambda])   . Because of the identification      H  ​   P  p    ≅   T  ​   M    π  ~   ​   (  p  )       HP_{p}\cong TM_{\tilde{\pi}(p)}    given by the connection and the transitivity of the principal right action on the fibres of     π  ~   \tilde{\pi}    we therefore obtain a natural identification       Ω  hor  k   ​    (  P  ,  W  )   G    ≅   Γ  ⁡   (    P  ⁡   [  λ  ]    ⊗    ⋀  k     T  ∗   ​  M     )    ≅    Ω  k   ​   (  M  ,    P  ⁡   [  λ  ]     ⟶  π   M   )     \Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{k}(P,W)^{G}\cong\Gamma(P[\lambda]\otimes\bigwedge^{k}T^{*}M)\cong\Omega^{k}(M,P[\lambda]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}M)   . 
 
 
 If    M  M    has an orientation, we can define a Hodge star operation on     Ω  ⁡   (  M  ,  π  )    \Omega(M,\pi)   . This can be transferred to      Ω  hor   ​    (  P  ,  W  )   G    \Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}(P,W)^{G}   , which will be the subject of the next section. It contains some technical definitions that will be essential for the gauge theories. The reader can skip this paragraph and only return to it later when the physics demands these definitions. 
 
 
 
 
  1.3.2  The Covariant Codifferential 

 
 Note that     d  ω   d^{\omega}    restricts to a map 
 

  
  
        Ω  hor  k   ​    (  P  ,  W  )   G     ⟶   d  ω      Ω  hor   k  +  1    ​    (  P  ,  W  )   G     .   \Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{k}(P,W)^{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d^{\omega}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{k+1}(P,W)^{G}.    
    
 
 What we need to formulate the second field equation is an analogue of the  codifferential  for principal bundles, like the covariant differential is an analogue of the ordinary differential. This will be a map 
 

  
  
        Ω  hor  k   ​    (  P  ,  W  )   G     ⟶   δ  ω      Ω  hor   k  −  1    ​    (  P  ,  W  )   G     ,   \Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{k}(P,W)^{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\delta^{\omega}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{k-1}(P,W)^{G},    
    
 
 which we shall call the  covariant codifferential . To define it, we first generalise the  Hodge star operation   16    16 
                 16 
                
                
                
              We assume the reader to be familiar with the principles of the Hodge star for (scalar-valued) differential forms on an oriented (pseudo)-Riemannian manifold. Recall that the definition of the ordinary codifferential    δ  \delta    does not depend on the choice of the orientation. So we can always define    δ  \delta    by choosing an arbitrary orientation locally. The situation here will be similar. We do not need an orientation to define the covariant codifferential.    to      Ω  hor   ​    (  P  ,  W  )   G    \Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}(P,W)^{G}   . 
 
 
 Let     (  M  ,  g  )   (M,g)    be an    n  n   -dimensional (pseudo)-Riemannian manifold. We generalise the Hodge star to vector-valued differential forms to be the unique      C  ∞   ​   (  M  )    C^{\infty}(M)   -linear operator       Ω  k   ​   (  M  ,  W  )     ⟶  ∗     Ω   n  −  k    ​   (  M  ,  W  )     \Omega^{k}(M,W)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Omega^{n-k}(M,W)   , such that     ∗   (  w  ⊗  η  )   =  w  ⊗  ∗  η   *(w\otimes\eta)=w\otimes*\eta   , for all     η  ∈    Ω  k   ​   (  M  )     \eta\in\Omega^{k}(M)    and     w  ∈  W   w\in W   .  [ 3 ]  Now, we seem to run into a problem. There seems to be no way to define a Hodge star on    P  P   , since    P  P    has no metric. We can, however, define the Hodge star on the horizontal differential forms. 
 
 
 Indeed, since we have the isomorphism      H  ​   P  p     ⟶    T  p   ​   π  ~       T    π  ~   ​   (  p  )     ​  M    HP_{p}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle T_{p}\tilde{\pi}}}{{\longrightarrow}}T_{\tilde{\pi}(p)}M   , we can define 
 

  
  
       ⋀  k    (   H  ​   P  p  ∗    )     ⟶    ∗  ~   p      ⋀   n  −  k     (   H  ​   P  p  ∗    )     \bigwedge^{k}(HP_{p}^{*})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{*}_{p}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\bigwedge^{n-k}(HP_{p}^{*})    
    
 
 by       ∗  ~   p    (    π  ~   ∗   τ  )   :=    π  ~   ∗    (   ∗    π  ~   ​   (  p  )     τ  )    \tilde{*}_{p}(\tilde{\pi}^{*}\tau):=\tilde{\pi}^{*}(*_{\tilde{\pi}(p)}\tau)   , for     τ  ∈    ⋀  k     T    π  ~   ​   (  p  )    ∗   ​  M     \tau\in\bigwedge^{k}T_{\tilde{\pi}(p)}^{*}M   . (If we are assuming we have a Hodge star    ∗  *    on    M  M   .) Now, finally, define 
 

  
  
       Ω  hor  k   ​    (  P  ,  W  )   G     ⟶   ∗  ¯      Ω  hor   n  −  k    ​    (  P  ,  W  )   G     \Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{k}(P,W)^{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bar{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{n-k}(P,W)^{G}    
    
 
 by setting      (    ∗  ¯   ​  α   )   p   (\bar{*}\alpha)_{p}    to be the unique    W  W   -valued     (   n  −  k   )   (n-k)   -form, vanishing on vertical vectors, such that         ∗  ¯   ​  α   |    H  ​   P  p     =     ∗  ~   p   ​   (    α  |    H  ​   P  p     )     \bar{*}\alpha|_{HP_{p}}=\tilde{*}_{p}(\alpha|_{HP_{p}})   . 
 
 
 It is easily verified that the Hodge star of an equivariant differential form is again equivariant. Indeed, a volume form     μ  ∈    Ω  n   ​   (  M  )     \mu\in\Omega^{n}(M)    pulls back to a horizontal    n  n   -form        π  ~   ∗   ​  μ   ∈    Ω  hor  n   ​   (  P  )     \tilde{\pi}^{*}\mu\in\Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{n}(P)   . This restricts to a top form on     H  ​   P  p    HP_{p}    that is invariant under the principal right action. Therefore     ∗  ¯   \bar{*}    respects equivariance. 
 
 
 Now, in analogy with the ordinary codifferential, we define 
 

  
  
        Ω  hor  k   ​    (  P  ,  W  )   G     ⟶   δ  ω      Ω  hor   k  −  1    ​    (  P  ,  W  )   G     ,   \Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{k}(P,W)^{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\delta^{\omega}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{k-1}(P,W)^{G},    
    
 
 

  
  
       δ  ω   :=    sign  ⁡   (  g  )    ​    (   −  1   )     n  ​  k   +  n  +  1    ​   ∗  ¯   ​   d  ω   ​   ∗  ¯     .   \delta^{\omega}:=\mathrm{sign}\,(g)(-1)^{nk+n+1}\bar{*}d^{\omega}\bar{*}.    
    
 
 This will turn out to be an essential tool in physics, as the second field equation (in the homogeneous case) taking the form 
 

  
  
        δ  ω   ​   Ω  ω    =  0   .   \delta^{\omega}\Omega^{\omega}=0.    
    
 
 As was the case with the ordinary codifferential, this covariant codifferential can be understood to be the adjoint of the covariant differential with respect to a suitable inner product. 
 
 
   Definition     (Inner product on tensor space) .  
 
 Let    E  E    be a    𝕜  \Bbbk   -vector space with an inner product    g  g   . Note that in the real case this defines an isomorphism     E  ≅   E  ∗    E\cong E^{*}   , and in the Hermitian case the corresponding antilinear identification with     E  ∗   E^{*}   . Therefore we immediately obtain an inner product     g  ∗   g^{*}    on the dual space     E  ∗   E^{*}    as well as the pullback of    g  g    along this identification. Moreover, suppose that we have a second    𝕜  \Bbbk   -inner product space     (   E  ′   ,   g  ′   )   (E^{\prime},g^{\prime})   . Then, we obtain an inner product     g  ​   g  ′    gg^{\prime}    on the tensor space     E  ⊗   E  ′    E\otimes E^{\prime}   . Indeed, we put      g  ​   g  ′   ​   (   e  ⊗   e  ′    ,   f  ⊗   f  ′    )    :=    g  ⁡   (  e  ,  f  )    ​   g  ′   ​   (   e  ′   ,   f  ′   )     gg^{\prime}(e\otimes e^{\prime},f\otimes f^{\prime}):=g(e,f)g^{\prime}(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})    and extend this definition to all of     E  ⊗   E  ′    E\otimes E^{\prime}    by sesqui-/bilinearity. In particular, if we start with an inner product    g  g    on    E  E    we obtain an inner product on the space       ⨂   k  ,  l    E   =     ⨂  k   E   ⊗    ⨂  l    E  ∗      \bigotimes^{k,l}E=\bigotimes^{k}E\otimes\bigotimes^{l}E^{*}    of     (  k  ,  l  )   (k,l)   -tensors. For convenience, we also denote this inner product by    g  g   . Of course, this restricts to the space      ⋀  k    E  ∗    \bigwedge^{k}E^{*}    of    k  k   -forms. 
 
 
 
 Let us now note that we have an inner product      h  ¯   p   \bar{h}_{p}    on     H  ​   P  p    HP_{p}   , i.e. the pullback of     g    π  ~   ​   (  p  )     g_{\tilde{\pi}(p)}    along the isomorphism      H  ​   P  p     ⟶    T  p   ​   π  ~       T    π  ~   ​   (  p  )     ​  M    HP_{p}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle T_{p}\tilde{\pi}}}{{\longrightarrow}}T_{\tilde{\pi}(p)}M   , and let us suppose that we have an inner product     k  W   k_{W}    on    W  W   . By the above reasoning we have a natural inner product      h  ¯   p   \bar{h}_{p}    on      ⋀  k    H  ​   P  p  ∗     \bigwedge^{k}HP_{p}^{*}    and therefore a pointwise inner product       h  ¯   p   ​   k  W    \bar{h}_{p}k_{W}    on the space       ⋀  k    H  ​   P  p  ∗     ⊗  W   \bigwedge^{k}HP_{p}^{*}\otimes W    of    W  W   -valued horizontal    k  k   -forms. We write      h  ¯   ​   k  W    \bar{h}k_{W}    for the induced map 
 

  
  
          Ω  hor  k   ​   (  P  ,  W  )    ×   Ω  hor  k    ​   (  P  ,  W  )     ⟶    h  ¯   ​   k  W       C  ∞   ​   (  P  )     .   \Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{k}(P,W)\times\Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{k}(P,W)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bar{h}k_{W}}}{{\longrightarrow}}C^{\infty}(P).    
    
 
 
 
 Finally, suppose that     k  W   k_{W}    is invariant for the    G  G   -action on    W  W   . Then we have that, for     α  ,   β  ∈    Ω  hor  k   ​    (  P  ,  W  )   G      \alpha,\beta\in\Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{k}(P,W)^{G}   ,      h  ¯   ​   k  W   ​   (  α  ,  β  )    \bar{h}k_{W}(\alpha,\beta)    is constant on the fibres of     P   ⟶   π  ~    M   P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{\pi}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M   . We conclude that, in this case, we obtain a map 
 

  
  
          Ω  hor  k   ​    (  P  ,  W  )   G    ×   Ω  hor  k    ​    (  P  ,  W  )   G     ⟶    h  ¯   ​   k  W       C  ∞   ​   (  M  )     .   \Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{k}(P,W)^{G}\times\Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{k}(P,W)^{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bar{h}k_{W}}}{{\longrightarrow}}C^{\infty}(M).    
    
 
 Now, we can interpret the covariant codifferential as an adjoint. 
 
 
 Let us define the  projected support  of a map     F   ⟶  ψ   V   F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\psi}}{{\longrightarrow}}V    of fibre bundles over    M  M   , where     V   ⟶     M   V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    is a vector bundle and     F   ⟶  π   M   F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    is an arbitrary fibre bundle, as the topological closure of the set     {  π   (  f  )   ∈  M  |  f  ∈  F  and  ψ   (  f  )   ≠  0  }   \{\pi(f)\in M\;|\;f\in F\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\psi(f)\neq 0\}   . 
 
 
   Claim 1.3.10   .  
 
  Suppose we have a global orientation    μ  \mu    on    M  M   , that is compatible with the metric. Let     U  ⊂  M   U\subset M    be open with compact closure, and let     α  ∈    Ω  hor  k   ​    (  P  ,  W  )   G     \alpha\in\Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{k}(P,W)^{G}    and     β  ∈    Ω  hor   k  +  1    ​    (  P  ,  W  )   G     \beta\in\Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{k+1}(P,W)^{G}    have projected supports in    U  U   . Then  
 

  
  
        ∫  U     h  ¯   ​   k  W   ​   (    d  ω   ​  α   ,  β  )   ​  μ    =    ∫  U     h  ¯   ​   k  W   ​   (  α  ,    δ  ω   ​  β   )   ​  μ     .   \int_{U}\bar{h}k_{W}(d^{\omega}\alpha,\beta)\mu=\int_{U}\bar{h}k_{W}(\alpha,\delta^{\omega}\beta)\mu.    
    
 
 
 
 
 This statement is just a technical generalisation of the well-known result for the case in which     W  =  𝕜   W=\Bbbk    and     P  =  M   P=M   . The proof can be found in  [ 6 ] . 
 
 
   Remark 1.3.11   .  
 
 We have seen that we can construct a product connection when we are dealing with fibre bundles. Moreover, we know that     P  ​   B  M    PB_{M}    has finite products. The question is whether the product of two principal connections is again a principal connection. Obviously, the answer is yes.  [ 6 ]  This connection is very important in gauge theory. Physicists often call it the  spliced connection , in line with their interesting terminology for the product of two principal bundles. 
 
 
 
 
  1.3.3  Induced Connections 

 
 We have seen that, given a principal    G  G   -bundle     P   ⟶   π  ~    M   P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{\pi}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    and an action      G  ×  S    ⟶  λ   S   G\times S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda}}{{\longrightarrow}}S    of    G  G    on a manifold    S  S   , we can construct an associated fibre bundle     P  ⁡   [  λ  ]    P[\lambda]    with fibre    S  S   . Suppose that    P  P    is also equipped with a principal connection      T  ​  P    ⟶    P  V   =   ζ  ∘  ω      V  ​  P    TP\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P_{V}=\zeta\circ\omega}}{{\longrightarrow}}VP   . The question is whether this induces a connection on the associated bundle in some natural way. This is indeed the case and the correspondence is particularly simple. It will be useful especially when we are dealing with the associated bundles that are part of an equivalence, as discussed in section   1.1.6  . Before we construct the induced connection, however, let us stop for a moment to think about what precisely a connection on the associated bundle would have to be. 
 
 
 Remember that we defined the associated bundle     P  ⁡   [  λ  ]    P[\lambda]    as the quotient      P  ×  S   /  G   P\times S/G   , where    G  G    acts on     P  ×  S   P\times S    on the right by      P  ×  S  ×  G    ⟶   ρ  ×   λ   −  1       P  ×  S    P\times S\times G\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rho\times\lambda^{-1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}P\times S   . By this notation I mean      (  p  ,  s  ,  g  )   ↦   (   ρ  ⁡   (  p  ,  g  )    ,   λ  ⁡   (   g   −  1    ,  s  )    )    (p,s,g)\mapsto(\rho(p,g),\lambda(g^{-1},s))   . It is useful to describe tangent vectors to     P  ⁡   [  λ  ]    P[\lambda]    by representatives: every tangent vector at     [  p  ,  s  ]   [p,s]    can be written as     T  ​  q  ​   (   ξ  p   ,   η  s   )    Tq(\xi_{p},\eta_{s})    for some      (   ξ  p   ,   η  s   )   ∈      T  p   ​  P   ×   T  s    ​  S    (\xi_{p},\eta_{s})\in T_{p}P\times T_{s}S   . 
 
 
 We construct the induced connection by the formula 
 

  
  
        P  V   ​   [  λ  ]   ​   (   T  ​  q  ​   (   ξ  p   ,   η  s   )    )    :=   T  ​  q  ​   (    P  V   ​   (   ξ  p   )    ,   η  s   )     .   P_{V}[\lambda](Tq(\xi_{p},\eta_{s})):=Tq(P_{V}(\xi_{p}),\eta_{s}).    
    
 
 This is well-defined. Indeed, the principal-connection condition says that     P  V   P_{V}    commutes with the tangent lift of the principal right action, and on vectors tangent to the    G  G   -orbits it is the identity on the vertical part. Hence changing the representative of     T  ​  q  ​   (   ξ  p   ,   η  s   )    Tq(\xi_{p},\eta_{s})    changes     T  ​  q  ​   (    P  V   ​   (   ξ  p   )    ,   η  s   )    Tq(P_{V}(\xi_{p}),\eta_{s})    by a tangent vector to the same quotient orbit. The formula is fibrewise linear and idempotent. Its image is vertical because      T  ​   π  ~   ​   (    P  V   ​   (   ξ  p   )    )    =  0   T\tilde{\pi}(P_{V}(\xi_{p}))=0   , and it restricts to the identity on vertical tangent vectors, since then     ξ  p   \xi_{p}    may be chosen vertical and       P  V   ​   (   ξ  p   )    =   ξ  p    P_{V}(\xi_{p})=\xi_{p}   . Thus      P  V   ​   [  λ  ]    P_{V}[\lambda]    is a connection on the associated bundle. 
 
 
 The parallel transport of this connection along a curve    c  c    in    M  M    is in a particularly simple correspondence with that of the original principal connection. That is, they are related precisely by the quotient map:                P   c  ⁡   (  0  )     ×  S   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{c(0)}\times S}                P   c  ⁡   (  t  )     ×  S   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{c(t)}\times S}                 P  ​    [  λ  ]    c  ⁡   (  0  )      {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P[\lambda]_{c(0)}}                P  ​    [  λ  ]    c  ⁡   (  t  )      .   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P[\lambda]_{c(t)}.}                           T  c  P   ​   (  −  ,  t  )    ×   id  S    \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{P}_{c}(-,t)\times\mathrm{id}_{S}}                      q  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}                    ↺  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circlearrowleft}                      q  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}                        T  c   P  ⁡   [  λ  ]     ​   (  −  ,  t  )    \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{P[\lambda]}_{c}(-,t)}           
 if we use the notation     T  c  F   T^{F}_{c}    for the parallel transport along    c  c    on a fibre bundle     F   ⟶     M   F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}M   .
This follows almost immediately from the uniqueness of solutions of ODEs. That is, after we observe that 
 

  
  
      P  V   ​   [  λ  ]   ​   (     d   d  ​  t     ​  q  ​   (    T  c  P   ​   (  p  ,  t  )    ,  s  )    )    \displaystyle P_{V}[\lambda](\frac{d}{dt}q(T^{P}_{c}(p,t),s))    
       =    P  V   ​   [  λ  ]   ​   (   T  ​  q  ​   (     d   d  ​  t     ​   T  c  P   ​   (  p  ,  t  )    ,   0  s   )    )     \displaystyle=P_{V}[\lambda](Tq(\frac{d}{dt}T^{P}_{c}(p,t),0_{s}))    
    
  
  
  
       =   T  ​  q  ​   (     P  V   ×   id   T  ​  S     ​   (     d   d  ​  t     ​   T  c  P   ​   (  p  ,  t  )    ,   0  s   )    )     \displaystyle=Tq(P_{V}\times\mathrm{id}_{TS}(\frac{d}{dt}T^{P}_{c}(p,t),0_{s}))    
    
  
  
  
       =   T  ​  q  ​   (    P  V   ​   (     d   d  ​  t     ​   T  c  P   ​   (  p  ,  t  )    )    ,   0  s   )     \displaystyle=Tq(P_{V}(\frac{d}{dt}T^{P}_{c}(p,t)),0_{s})    
    
  
  
  
        =   T  ​  q  ​   (   0    T  c  P   ​   (  p  ,  t  )     ,   0  s   )    =  0   ,   \displaystyle=Tq(0_{T^{P}_{c}(p,t)},0_{s})=0,    
    
 
 where     0  s   0_{s}    denotes the zero element in      T  s   ​  S   T_{s}S    and similarly for     0    T  c  P   ​   (  p  ,  t  )     0_{T^{P}_{c}(p,t)}   . From this, we see that     q  ⁡   (    T  c  P   ​   (  p  ,  t  )    ,  s  )    q(T^{P}_{c}(p,t),s)    satisfies the defining initial value problem for      T  c   P  ⁡   [  λ  ]     ​   (   q  ⁡   (  p  ,  s  )    ,  t  )    T^{P[\lambda]}_{c}(q(p,s),t)   . The reader can check that the curvatures are also related by the quotient map, in the sense that     R   [  λ  ]   ∘   (  T  q   ∧  P   T  q  )   =  T  q  ∘   (  R  ×   0  S   )    R[\lambda]\circ(Tq\wedge_{P}Tq)=Tq\circ(R\times 0_{S})   , where we write     R  ⁡   [  λ  ]    R[\lambda]    for the curvature of      P  V   ​   [  λ  ]    P_{V}[\lambda]    and     0  S   0_{S}    for the zero section of     T  ​  S   TS   .  [ 19 ]  
 
 
 
  1.3.4  Principal Connections Are Complete 

 
 Why are we interested in principal connections in the first place? One major advantage of such a connection is that it is automatically a complete connection: parallel transport is globally defined along any curve. One consequence is that we can construct a holonomy group. 
 
 
   Theorem 1.3.12   .  
 
  Principal connections are complete, i.e. for every curve     I   ⟶  c   M   I\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\longrightarrow}}M   , the parallel transport is defined for all times:       P   c  ⁡   (  0  )     ×  I    ⟶   T  c    P   P_{c(0)}\times I\stackrel{{\scriptstyle T_{c}}}{{\longrightarrow}}P   .  
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
  Let     P   ⟶   π  ~    M   P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{\pi}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    be a principal    G  G   -bundle that is equipped with a principal connection    ω  \omega    and let     I   ⟶  c   M   I\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    be a     C  1   C^{1}   -curve. We want to show that parallel transport is defined globally, so, in the notation of theorem   1.2.9  ,      Ω  c   =    P   c  ⁡   (  0  )     ×  I    \Omega_{c}=P_{c(0)}\times I   .
 
 The idea of the proof is that it is easy to find some lift of    c  c    to    P  P   , which we can transform into a horizontal lift by the action of appropriate group elements. One easily derives the differential equation these group elements have to satisfy. Finally, a maximal solution to this differential equation is obtained by extending a local solution using the group multiplication.
 
 The precise argument is the following. Using local trivialisations, it is easy to construct a curve     I   ⟶  d   P   I\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}P   , such that       π  ~   ∘  d   =  c   \tilde{\pi}\circ d=c    and      d  ⁡   (  0  )    =  p   d(0)=p   , if     p  ∈   P   c  ⁡   (  0  )      p\in P_{c(0)}   . We look for a smooth curve     I   ⟶  g   G   I\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\longrightarrow}}G   , such that       c  hor   ​   (  t  )    :=   ρ  ⁡   (   d  t   ,   g  t   )     c_{\mathrm{hor}}(t):=\rho(d_{t},g_{t})    is a horizontal lift of    c  c   :       π  ~   ∘   c  hor    =  c   \tilde{\pi}\circ c_{\mathrm{hor}}=c    and       c  hor  ′   ​   (  t  )    ∈   H  ​  P    c_{\mathrm{hor}}^{\prime}(t)\in HP   , for all     t  ∈  I   t\in I   . (Here we write     d  t   d_{t}    for     d  ⁡   (  t  )    d(t)    and similarly for     g  t   g_{t}   .)  
 
 
  We note that the principal right action respects the fibres of    P  P   , so the first demand, that       π  ~   ∘   c  hor    =  c   \tilde{\pi}\circ c_{\mathrm{hor}}=c   , is immediately satisfied for any choice of     t  ↦   g  t    t\mapsto g_{t}   . We verify what conditions the second demand imposes on     t  ↦   g  t    t\mapsto g_{t}   . In terms of    ω  \omega    it reads  
 

  
  
      0   =  !    ω  ⁡   (    d   d  ​  t    ​  ρ  ​   (   d  t   ,   g  t   )    )     .   0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}\omega\left(\frac{d}{dt}\rho(d_{t},g_{t})\right).    
    
 
  Now, by the Leibniz rule,       d   d  ​  t    ​  ρ  ​   (   d  t   ,   g  t   )    =    T  ​   ρ   g  t    ​    d  ˙   t    +   T  ​   ρ   d  t    ​    g  ˙   t      \frac{d}{dt}\rho(d_{t},g_{t})=T\rho_{g_{t}}\dot{d}_{t}+T\rho^{d_{t}}\dot{g}_{t}   , where, as before,       ρ  h   ​   (  p  )    :=   ρ  ⁡   (  p  ,  h  )    =    :    ρ  p   ​   (  h  )     \rho_{h}(p):=\rho(p,h)=:\rho^{p}(h)   . Now, by claim   1.3.4  , we know that      ω  ⁡   (   T  ​   ρ   g  t    ​    d  ˙   t    )    =    Ad  ⁡   (   g  t   −  1    )    ​   (   ω  ⁡   (    d  ˙   t   )    )     \omega(T\rho_{g_{t}}\dot{d}_{t})=\mathrm{Ad}(g_{t}^{-1})(\omega(\dot{d}_{t}))   . Moreover, since claim   1.3.2   tells us that    ω  \omega    reproduces the generators of the infinitesimal action, we have that      ω  ⁡   (   T  ​   ρ   d  t    ​   (    g  ˙   t   )    )    =   T  ​   L   g  t   −  1     ​    g  ˙   t     \omega(T\rho^{d_{t}}(\dot{g}_{t}))=TL_{g_{t}^{-1}}\dot{g}_{t}   , where     G   ⟶   L  h    G   G\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L_{h}}}{{\longrightarrow}}G    denotes the left group multiplication by    h  h   . Remember that      Ad  ⁡   (  h  )    =   T  ⁡   (    L  h   ∘   R   h   −  1      )     \mathrm{Ad}(h)=T(L_{h}\circ R_{h^{-1}})   , if     G   ⟶   R  h    G   G\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R_{h}}}{{\longrightarrow}}G    denotes the right multiplication by    h  h   . Therefore, we find that the second condition reduces to the demand that  
 

  
  
         g  ˙   t   =   −   T  ​   R   g  t    ​  ω  ​   (    d  ˙   t   )      .    (*)      \dot{g}_{t}=-TR_{g_{t}}\omega(\dot{d}_{t}).\quad\textnormal{(*)}\quad    
    
 
  I claim that this differential equation admits a unique global solution     I   ⟶  g   G   I\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\longrightarrow}}G   , which is the integral curve of a (time-dependent) right-invariant vector field    X  X   :                G  ×  I   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times I}                T  ⁡   (   G  ×  I   )    ≅    T  ​  G   ×  I  ×  ℝ    {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(G\times I)\cong TG\times I\times\mathbb{R}}                 (  h  ,  s  )   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(h,s)}                  X  s   ​   (  h  )    :=   (   −   T  ​   R  h   ​  ω  ​   (    d  ˙   s   )     ,   d  /     d  ​  t   |   s    )   ∈      T  h   ​  G   ×   T  s    ​  I   ≅     T  h   ​  G   ×  ℝ    .   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{s}(h):=(-TR_{h}\omega(\dot{d}_{s}),d/dt|_{s})\in T_{h}G\times T_{s}I\cong T_{h}G\times\mathbb{R}.}                        X  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}                       
  (Of course, by      d  /  d   ​  t   d/dt    I mean the canonical unit vector in       T  s   ​  I   ≅  ℝ   T_{s}I\cong\mathbb{R}   . Moreover, right-invariance is immediate:       X  s   ​   (    R  a   ​   (  h  )    )    =    X  s   ​   (   h  ​  a   )    =   −   T  ​   R   h  ​  a    ​  ω  ​   (    d  ˙   s   )     =   T  ​   R  a   ​   (   −   T  ​   R  h   ​  ω  ​   (    d  ˙   s   )     )    =   T  ​   R  a   ​   X  s   ​   (  h  )     X_{s}(R_{a}(h))=X_{s}(ha)=-TR_{ha}\omega(\dot{d}_{s})=TR_{a}(-TR_{h}\omega(\dot{d}_{s}))=TR_{a}X_{s}(h)   .)
Clearly, solutions of     (  ∗  )   (*)    correspond precisely with integral curves of    X  X   . The assertion follows from the basic fact that right-invariant vector fields on a Lie group are complete.
∎  
 
 
 
 
  1.3.5  The Ambrose-Singer Theorem 

 
 In the case of a principal connection, the interpretation of the curvature as infinitesimal holonomy that we already observed in claim   1.2.10   takes a particularly simple form. This result is the famous theorem by Ambrose and Singer.  [ 18 ]  
 
 
   Theorem 1.3.13     (Ambrose-Singer) .  
 
  Let     P   ⟶   π  ~    M   P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{\pi}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    be a principal bundle with a principal connection    ω  \omega   . Then its holonomy group at a point      p  0   ∈  P   p_{0}\in P    is a Lie group and its Lie algebra is isomorphic to the subalgebra of    𝔤  \mathfrak{g}    spanned by the elements of the form      Ω  p  ω   ​   (  X  ,  Y  )    \Omega^{\omega}_{p}(X,Y)   , where    p  p    runs over all points that can be connected to     p  0   p_{0}    by a horizontal curve and     X  ,  Y   X,Y    run over all elements of      T  p   ​  P   T_{p}P   .  
 
 
 
 
 
  1.4  Connections on Vector Bundles 

 
 Just as in the case of principal bundles, where we singled out certain connections that are compatible with the group action, we have certain preferred connections on vector bundles: those that are compatible with the linear structure on each fibre. In fact, we will see that, in some sense, these are a special case of the principal connections we have already seen. What does this compatibility mean concretely? 
 
 
 Let     V   ⟶  π   M   V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    be a vector bundle with a connection     P  V   P_{V}   . In section   1.2.1  , we have defined      T  ​  V    ⟶   P  V     V  ​  V   ⊂   T  ​  V    TV\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P_{V}}}{{\longrightarrow}}VV\subset TV    to be a morphism of vector bundles over    V  V   , since this was the only vector bundle structure that was at hand. Now, however,     T  ​  V   TV    also has a second vector bundle structure, namely      T  ​  V    ⟶   T  ​  π     T  ​  M    TV\stackrel{{\scriptstyle T\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}TM   . We would like the vertical part of a tangent vector, after identifying      V  ​  V   ≅   V   ×  M   V    VV\cong V\times_{M}V   , to respect the linear structure on the fibres of      T  ​  V    ⟶   T  ​  π     T  ​  M    TV\stackrel{{\scriptstyle T\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}TM    as well. When this is the case, we call     P  V   P_{V}    an  affine connection . 
 
 
 Note that, in the light of equation   1.2  , the affinity of the connection is equivalent to the linearity over the ring      C  ∞   ​   (  M  )    C^{\infty}(M)    of the Christoffel forms in the second argument:       Γ  α   ​   (  X  ,   f  ⋅  v   )    =    f  ⋅   Γ  α    ​   (  X  ,  v  )     \Gamma^{\alpha}(X,f\cdot v)=f\cdot\Gamma^{\alpha}(X,v)   . This means that we can view     X  ↦    Γ  α   ​   (  X  ,  −  )     X\mapsto\Gamma^{\alpha}(X,-)    as an     End  ⁡   (   𝕜  k   )    \mathrm{End}(\Bbbk^{k})   -valued    1  1   -form      Γ  α   ∈    Ω  1   ​   (   U  α   ,   End  ⁡   (   𝕜  k   )    )     \Gamma^{\alpha}\in\Omega^{1}(U_{\alpha},\mathrm{End}(\Bbbk^{k}))   . 
 
 
 This means that the equation that defines parallel transport will be a linear one, whose solution, obviously, depends on the initial values in a linear way. This means that the parallel transport along a curve    c  c    over time    t  t    defines a (fibrewise linear) map      V   c  ⁡   (  0  )      ⟶    T  c   ​   (  −  ,  t  )      V   c  ⁡   (  t  )      V_{c(0)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle T_{c}(-,t)}}{{\longrightarrow}}V_{c(t)}   . We will denote this map by     T   c  ,  t    T_{c,t}   . (Compare this with the    G  G   -equivariance of the parallel transport on a    G  G   -principal bundle with an equivariant connection.) 
 
 
  1.4.1  Covariant Derivatives 

 
 There is hardly any mathematical device that is more recurrent in the theory of general relativity than the so-called covariant derivative. It arises wherever an ordinary derivative would appear in non-relativistic physics. We will see that this derivative is closely related to the notions we discussed in the previous paragraphs: connections, curvature, horizontal lifts, Christoffel forms and parallel transport. It is defined as follows. 
 
 
 Let us take a vector field     M   ⟶  X    T  ​  M    M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle X}}{{\longrightarrow}}TM    and a section     M   ⟶  σ   V   M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma}}{{\longrightarrow}}V   . We can canonically identify      V  ​   V  v    =    T  v   ​   V   π  ⁡   (  v  )      ≅   V   π  ⁡   (  v  )      VV_{v}=T_{v}V_{\pi(v)}\cong V_{\pi(v)}    for every     v  ∈  V   v\in V   . On the level of vector bundles over    M  M    this becomes an isomorphism               V   ×  M   V   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\times_{M}V}               V  ​  V   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces VV}                 (   u  m   ,   v  m   )   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(u_{m},v_{m})}                   d   d  ​  t    |    t  =  0    ​   (    u  m   +   t  ​   v  m     )    .   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}(u_{m}+tv_{m}).}                        Φ  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}                       
 This means that the effect of the connection     P  V   P_{V}    is entirely captured in the map     K  :=    π  2   ∘   Φ   −  1    ∘   P  V     K:=\pi_{2}\circ\Phi^{-1}\circ P_{V}   , known as the  connector  of the connection. For an affine connection,    K  K    is a map of vector bundles (i.e. respects the linear structure on the fibres) in the following two senses:               T  ​  V   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TV}              V  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}               T  ​  V   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TV}              V  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}                V  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}              M  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}               T  ​  M   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}               M  .   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M.}                        K  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}                       π  V   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{V}}                      π  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}                      K  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}                       T  ​  π   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi}                      π  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}                      π  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}                       π  M   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{M}}           
 The first linearity follows from the fact that     P  V   P_{V}    is a vector-bundle projection over    V  V   ; the second is precisely the affinity condition. 
 
 
 The  covariant derivative         ∇  X   σ   ​   (  m  )    :=     K  ∘  T   ​  σ   ∘   X  ⁡   (  m  )      \nabla_{X}\sigma(m):=K\circ T\sigma\circ X(m)    of    σ  \sigma    along    X  X    is then an element of      V   π  ⁡   (   σ  ⁡   (  m  )    )     =   V  m    V_{\pi(\sigma(m))}=V_{m}   .      ∇  X   σ   \nabla_{X}\sigma    is simply a section       ∇  X   σ   ∈   Γ  ⁡   (  V  )     \nabla_{X}\sigma\in\Gamma(V)   . This means that     ∇  X   \nabla_{X}    is an operator      Γ  ⁡   (  V  )     ⟶   ∇  X     Γ  ⁡   (  V  )     \Gamma(V)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nabla_{X}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Gamma(V)   . In particular, we can iterate it, to obtain higher covariant derivatives. Moreover, it has the following properties. 
 
 
   Claim 1.4.1   .  
 
  Let      X  ,  Y   ∈   𝒳  ⁡   (  M  )     X,Y\in\mathcal{X}(M)   ,      σ  ,  τ   ∈   Γ  ⁡   (  V  )     \sigma,\tau\in\Gamma(V)   ,     f  ∈    C  ∞   ​   (  M  )     f\in C^{\infty}(M)    and     m  ∈  M   m\in M   . Then the covariant derivative has the following properties:  
 
  1.  
 
       ∇  X   σ   ​   (  m  )    \nabla_{X}\sigma(m)     only depends on the value of     X  X     at     m  m     and on that of     σ  \sigma     on a curve      ]  −  ϵ  ,  ϵ  [   ⟶  c   M   ]-\epsilon,\epsilon[\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\longrightarrow}}M     with       c  ⁡   (  0  )    =  m   c(0)=m     and        c  ′   ​   (  0  )    =   X  ​   (  m  )     c^{\prime}(0)=X(m)    . 
  
  
  2.  
 
       ∇    f  ⋅  X   +  Y    σ   =    f  ⋅    ∇  X   σ    +    ∇  Y   σ     \nabla_{f\cdot X+Y}\sigma=f\cdot\nabla_{X}\sigma+\nabla_{Y}\sigma   
  
  
  3.
