       ∇  X    (   σ  +  τ   )    =     ∇  X   σ   +    ∇  X   τ     \nabla_{X}(\sigma+\tau)=\nabla_{X}\sigma+\nabla_{X}\tau   
  
  
  4.  
 
       ∇  X    (   f  ⋅  σ   )    =     X  ⁡   (  f  )    ⋅  σ   +   f  ⋅    ∇  X   σ      \nabla_{X}(f\cdot\sigma)=X(f)\cdot\sigma+f\cdot\nabla_{X}\sigma    .  
  
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
  Property   1.   follows directly from the definition        ∇  X   σ   ​   (  m  )    =   K  ⁡   (    T  m   ​  σ  ​   (   X  ⁡   (  m  )    )    )    :=    K  ∘     d   d  ​  t    |    t  =  0     ​   (   σ  ∘  c   )     \nabla_{X}\sigma(m)=K(T_{m}\sigma(X(m))):=K\circ\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}(\sigma\circ c)   . Property   2.   comes from the fact that    K  K    is a     π  V   \pi_{V}   -   π  \pi   -fibrewise linear map, while property   3.   comes from the     T  ​  π   T\pi   -   π  \pi   -fibrewise linearity of    K  K    (see diagrams above).  
 
 
  For   4.  , choose a local trivialisation     (  U  ,  ϕ  )   (U,\phi)    of    V  V    around    m  m    and write      σ  e   =    π  2   ∘  ϕ  ∘  σ    \sigma_{e}=\pi_{2}\circ\phi\circ\sigma   . In these coordinates,  
 

  
  
        T  ⁡   (   f  ​  σ   )    ​   (  X  )    =   T  ​   ϕ   −  1    ​   (  X  ,     X  ⁡   (  f  )    ​   σ  e    +   f  ​  T  ​   σ  e   ​   (  X  )     )     .   T(f\sigma)(X)=T\phi^{-1}\bigl(X,X(f)\sigma_{e}+f\,T\sigma_{e}(X)\bigr).    
    
 
  The local expression of the connector is  
 

  
  
        π  2   ∘  ϕ  ∘  K  ∘   T  ​   ϕ   −  1    ​   (  X  ,  η  )     =   η  +    Γ  α   ​   (  X  ,  v  )      ,   \pi_{2}\circ\phi\circ K\circ T\phi^{-1}(X,\eta)=\eta+\Gamma^{\alpha}(X,v),    
    
 
  for a tangent vector based at      (  m  ,  v  )   ∈   U  ×   𝕜  k     (m,v)\in U\times\Bbbk^{k}   . Since the connection is affine,       Γ  α   ​   (  X  ,   f  ​  v   )    =   f  ​   Γ  α   ​   (  X  ,  v  )     \Gamma^{\alpha}(X,fv)=f\Gamma^{\alpha}(X,v)   . Therefore  
 

  
  
      π  2   ∘   ϕ  ⁡   (    ∇  X    (   f  ​  σ   )    )     \displaystyle\pi_{2}\circ\phi(\nabla_{X}(f\sigma))    
       =     X  ⁡   (  f  )    ​   σ  e    +   f  ​  T  ​   σ  e   ​   (  X  )    +    Γ  α   ​   (  X  ,   f  ​   σ  e    )      \displaystyle=X(f)\sigma_{e}+f\,T\sigma_{e}(X)+\Gamma^{\alpha}(X,f\sigma_{e})    
    
  
  
  
        =     X  ⁡   (  f  )    ​   σ  e    +   f  ⁡   (    T  ​   σ  e   ​   (  X  )    +    Γ  α   ​   (  X  ,   σ  e   )     )      ,   \displaystyle=X(f)\sigma_{e}+f\bigl(T\sigma_{e}(X)+\Gamma^{\alpha}(X,\sigma_{e})\bigr),    
    
 
  which is the coordinate expression for       ∇  X    (   f  ​  σ   )    =     X  ⁡   (  f  )    ​  σ   +   f  ​    ∇  X   σ      \nabla_{X}(f\sigma)=X(f)\sigma+f\nabla_{X}\sigma   .
∎  
 
 
 
 One can also define a map       𝒳  ⁡   (  M  )    ×   Γ  ⁡   (  V  )      ⟶  ∇    Γ  ⁡   (  V  )     \mathcal{X}(M)\times\Gamma(V)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nabla}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Gamma(V)    satisfying these properties to be a covariant derivative. From this one can construct a connection  17    17 
                 17 
                
                
                
              Corollary   1.4.2   gives a formula for the Christoffel forms in terms of the covariant derivative. Equation   1.2   then gives a formula for what connection     P  V   P_{V}    should be.   . This is easily (see for example  [ 5 ] ) seen to be an equivalent characterisation of an affine connection. 
 
 
   Corollary 1.4.2   .  
 
  Let     (   U  α   ,   ϕ  α   )   (U_{\alpha},\phi_{\alpha})    be a local trivialisation for    V  V    and write     Γ  α   \Gamma^{\alpha}    for the associated Christoffel    1  1   -form. Then, if we write     (   e  i   )   (e_{i})    for the local frame corresponding to the trivialisation and      σ  e   =   (   σ  i   )    \sigma_{e}=(\sigma^{i})    for the components of a section    σ  \sigma    with respect to this frame,  
 

  
  
        (    ∇  X   σ   )   e   =    T  ​   σ  e   ​   (  X  )    +    Γ  α   ​   (  X  )   ​   σ  e      .   (\nabla_{X}\sigma)_{e}=T\sigma_{e}(X)+\Gamma^{\alpha}(X)\sigma_{e}.    
    
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
  We first deal with a simple case. Denote by      Γ  α   ​    (  X  )   i  j    \Gamma^{\alpha}(X)_{i}^{j}    the matrix coefficients of      Γ  α   ​   (  X  )    \Gamma^{\alpha}(X)    with respect to the frame     (   e  i   )   (e_{i})    and write      e  ^   i   \hat{e}_{i}    for the    i  i   -th basis vector of     𝕜  k   \Bbbk^{k}   . Furthermore write      T  ​   𝕜  k    ≅    𝕜  k   ×   𝕜  k     ⟶   π      ∙  ×  𝕜     k        𝕜  k    T\Bbbk^{k}\cong\Bbbk^{k}\times\Bbbk^{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{\bullet\times\Bbbk^{k}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Bbbk^{k}    for the projection      (  x  ,  y  )   ↦  y   (x,y)\mapsto y   , where the second     𝕜  k   \Bbbk^{k}    denotes the tangent space of the first. Then
   
 

  
  
      (    ∇  X    e  i    )   e   \displaystyle(\nabla_{X}e_{i})_{e}    
     =   π   ∙  ×  𝕜     k     ∘  T   ϕ  α   ∘   P  V    (  T   e  i    (  X  )   )    \displaystyle=\pi_{\bullet\times\Bbbk^{k}}\circ T\phi_{\alpha}\circ P_{V}(Te_{i}(X))    
    
  
  
  
     =   π   ∙  ×  𝕜     k     ∘  T   ϕ  α   ∘   P  V    (  T   ϕ  α   −  1     (  X  ,   0    e  i   ^    )   )    \displaystyle=\pi_{\bullet\times\Bbbk^{k}}\circ T\phi_{\alpha}\circ P_{V}(T\phi_{\alpha}^{-1}(X,0_{\hat{e_{i}}}))    
    
  
  
  
        =    Γ  α   ​   (  X  )   ​    e  i   ^     .   \displaystyle=\Gamma^{\alpha}(X)\hat{e_{i}}.    
    
 
  Hence       ∇  X    e  i    =    ∑  j     Γ  α   ​    (  X  )   i  j   ​   e  j      \nabla_{X}e_{i}=\sum_{j}\Gamma^{\alpha}(X)^{j}_{i}e_{j}   .  
 
 
  This implies that for a general section     σ  ∈   Γ  ⁡   (  V  )     \sigma\in\Gamma(V)   ,  
 

  
  
      ∇  X   σ   \displaystyle\nabla_{X}\sigma    
     =  ∇     X    ∑       i  =  1       k   σ     i   e     i    \displaystyle=\nabla_{X}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sigma^{i}e_{i}    
    
  
  
  
       =      ∑   i  =  1   k     T  ​   σ  i   ​   (  X  )   ​   e  i     +     ∑   i  =  1   k      σ  i   ​    ∇  X    e  i        \displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}T\sigma^{i}(X)e_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sigma^{i}\nabla_{X}e_{i}    
    
  
  
  
       =      ∑   i  =  1   k     T  ​   σ  i   ​   (  X  )   ​   e  i     +     ∑    i  ,  j   =  1   k      σ  i   ​   Γ  α   ​    (  X  )   i  j   ​   e  j       \displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}T\sigma^{i}(X)e_{i}+\sum_{i,j=1}^{k}\sigma^{i}\Gamma^{\alpha}(X)^{j}_{i}e_{j}    
    
  
  
  
        =     ∑   i  =  1   k      (    T  ​   σ  i   ​   (  X  )    +    (    Γ  α   ​   (  X  )   ​   σ  e    )   i    )   ​   e  i      .   \displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}(T\sigma^{i}(X)+(\Gamma^{\alpha}(X)\sigma_{e})^{i})e_{i}.    
    
 
  ∎  
 
 
 
 This result shows that       ∇  X   σ   ​   (  m  )    \nabla_{X}\sigma(m)    depends only on the value of    X  X    at    m  m    and that of    σ  \sigma    on an arbitrarily small path     ]  −  ϵ  ,  ϵ  [   ⟶  c   M   ]-\epsilon,\epsilon[\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\longrightarrow}}M   , with      c  ⁡   (  0  )    =  m   c(0)=m    and       c  ′   ​   (  0  )    =   X  ​   (  m  )     c^{\prime}(0)=X(m)   . 
 
 
   Claim 1.4.3   .  
 
  Let     ]  −  ϵ  ,  ϵ  [   ⟶  c   M   ]-\epsilon,\epsilon[\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    be a     C  1   C^{1}   -curve with      c  ⁡   (  0  )    =  a   c(0)=a    and       c  ′   ​   (  0  )    =   X  ​   (  a  )     c^{\prime}(0)=X(a)   . Then, in the canonical identification       T   σ  ⁡   (  a  )     ​   V  a    ≅   V  a    T_{\sigma(a)}V_{a}\cong V_{a}   ,        ∇  X   σ   ​   (  a  )    =      d   d  ​  t    |    t  =  0    ​   T   c  ,  t    −  1    ​   (   σ  ⁡   (   c  ⁡   (  t  )    )    )     \nabla_{X}\sigma(a)=\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}T_{c,t}^{-1}(\sigma(c(t)))   .  
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
  This is a simple computation.  
 

  
  
       ∇  X   σ   ​   (  a  )    \displaystyle\nabla_{X}\sigma(a)    
       =   K  ⁡   (    T  a   ​  σ  ​   (   X  ⁡   (  a  )    )    )     \displaystyle=K(T_{a}\sigma(X(a)))    
    
  
  
  
       =      K  ​    d   d  ​  t      |    t  =  0    ​  σ  ​   (   c  ⁡   (  t  )    )     \displaystyle=K\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}\sigma(c(t))    
    
  
  
  
       =        K  ​    d   d  ​  t      |    t  =  0    ​   T   c  ,  t     ∘   T   c  ,  t    −  1     ​   (   σ  ⁡   (   c  ⁡   (  t  )    )    )     \displaystyle=K\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}T_{c,t}\circ T_{c,t}^{-1}(\sigma(c(t)))    
    
  
  
  
       =       K  ​    d   d  ​  t      |    t  =  0    ​   T   c  ,  t    ​   (   σ  ⁡   (   c  ⁡   (  0  )    )    )    +      K  ​   T   c  ,  0    ​    d   d  ​  t      |    t  =  0    ​   T   c  ,  t    −  1    ​   (   σ  ⁡   (   c  ⁡   (  t  )    )    )      \displaystyle=K\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}T_{c,t}(\sigma(c(0)))+KT_{c,0}\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}T_{c,t}^{-1}(\sigma(c(t)))    
    
  
  
  
        =       K  ​    d   d  ​  t      |    t  =  0    ​   T   c  ,  t    ​   (   σ  ⁡   (  a  )    )    +      K  ​    d   d  ​  t      |    t  =  0    ​   T   c  ,  t    −  1    ​   (   σ  ⁡   (   c  ⁡   (  t  )    )    )      .   \displaystyle=K\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}T_{c,t}(\sigma(a))+K\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}T_{c,t}^{-1}(\sigma(c(t))).    
    
 
  Now, the first term vanishes, since        d   d  ​  t    |    t  =  0    ​   T   c  ,  t    ​   (   σ  ⁡   (  a  )    )    \frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}T_{c,t}(\sigma(a))    is horizontal, by definition of parallel transport. Moreover, since       T   c  ,  t    −  1    ​   (   σ  ⁡   (   c  ⁡   (  t  )    )    )    ∈   V  a    T_{c,t}^{-1}(\sigma(c(t)))\in V_{a}    for all    t  t   ,        d   d  ​  t    |    t  =  0    ​   T   c  ,  t    −  1    ​   (   σ  ⁡   (   c  ⁡   (  t  )    )    )    \frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}T_{c,t}^{-1}(\sigma(c(t)))    is a vertical vector. The conclusion follows.
∎  
 
 
 
 Finally, I would like to introduce one more notation that is ubiquitous in physics literature. Let     ]  −  ϵ  ,  ϵ  [   ⟶  c   M   ]-\epsilon,\epsilon[\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    denote a smooth curve. Then, we can consider the pullback connection      c  ∗   ​   P  V    c^{*}P_{V}    if     P  V   P_{V}    denotes a given affine connection on     V   ⟶  π   M   V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}M   . It is conventional to write      D  /  d   ​  t   D/dt    for its covariant derivative. Explicitly,        D  ​  s   /  d   ​  t   =   K  ∘   s  ′     Ds/dt=K\circ s^{\prime}   , if     ]  −  ϵ  ,  ϵ  [   ⟶  s   V   ]-\epsilon,\epsilon[\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\longrightarrow}}V    is a smooth map such that      π  ∘  s   =  c   \pi\circ s=c    (i.e.    s  s    is a section of the pullback bundle      c  ∗   ​  π   c^{*}\pi   ). 
 
 
 
  1.4.2  Curvature of Affine Connections 

 
 The definition of curvature of corollary   1.2.6   takes a particularly easy form if we are dealing with an affine connection. Pointwise,    R  R    corresponds to maps 
 

  
  
         T   π  ⁡   (  v  )     ​  M   ∧    T   π  ⁡   (  v  )     ​  M     ⟶   R  v     V  ​   V  v    =    T  v   ​   V   π  ⁡   (  v  )      ≅   V   π  ⁡   (  v  )      ,   T_{\pi(v)}M\wedge T_{\pi(v)}M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R_{v}}}{{\longrightarrow}}VV_{v}=T_{v}V_{\pi(v)}\cong V_{\pi(v)},    
    
 
 depending smoothly on the basepoint     v  ∈  V   v\in V   . In terms of the connector, this becomes       R  v   ​   (  X  ,  Y  )    =   −   K  ⁡   (   [    X  hor   ,   Y  hor    ]   )      R_{v}(X,Y)=-K([X_{\mathrm{hor}},Y_{\mathrm{hor}}])   . In this form, the curvature is closely related to the second covariant derivative. 
 
 
   Claim 1.4.4   .  
 
  Suppose    V  V    is a vector bundle that is equipped with an affine connection. Let     m  ∈  M   m\in M   ,     σ  ∈   Γ  ⁡   (  V  )     \sigma\in\Gamma(V)    and      X  ,  Y   ∈   𝒳  ⁡   (  M  )     X,Y\in\mathcal{X}(M)   . Then the following formula holds for the curvature:  
 

  
  
        R   σ  ⁡   (  m  )     ​   (  X  ,  Y  )    =      ∇  X    ∇  Y    ​  σ  ​   (  m  )    −     ∇  Y    ∇  X    ​  σ  ​   (  m  )    −     ∇   [  X  ,  Y  ]    σ   ​   (  m  )      .   R_{\sigma(m)}(X,Y)=\nabla_{X}\nabla_{Y}\sigma(m)-\nabla_{Y}\nabla_{X}\sigma(m)-\nabla_{[X,Y]}\sigma(m).    
  
  (1.3)    
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
  We prove the formula in a local frame, using the formula from corollary   1.4.2  . With respect to a local frame     (   e  i   )   (e_{i})    with Christoffel    1  1   -form     Γ  α   \Gamma^{\alpha}   , we have  
 

  
  
      (     ∇  X    ∇  Y    ​  σ   )   e   \displaystyle(\nabla_{X}\nabla_{Y}\sigma)_{e}    
       =     ℒ  X   ​   (    ℒ  Y   ​   (   σ  e   )    )    +    ℒ  X   ​   (    Γ  α   ​   (  Y  )    )   ​   σ  e    +    Γ  α   ​   (  Y  )   ​   ℒ  X   ​   (   σ  e   )      \displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{X}(\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\sigma_{e}))+\mathcal{L}_{X}(\Gamma^{\alpha}(Y))\sigma_{e}+\Gamma^{\alpha}(Y)\mathcal{L}_{X}(\sigma_{e})    
    
  
  
  
       +    Γ  α   ​   (  X  )   ​   ℒ  Y   ​   (   σ  e   )     +    Γ  α   ​   (  X  )   ​   Γ  α   ​   (  Y  )   ​   σ  e     .   \displaystyle+\Gamma^{\alpha}(X)\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\sigma_{e})+\Gamma^{\alpha}(X)\Gamma^{\alpha}(Y)\sigma_{e}.    
    
 
  and since        ℒ  X   ​   (    ℒ  Y   ​   (   σ  e   )    )    −    ℒ  Y   ​   (    ℒ  X   ​   (   σ  e   )    )     =    ℒ   [  X  ,  Y  ]    ​   (   σ  e   )     \mathcal{L}_{X}(\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\sigma_{e}))-\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\mathcal{L}_{X}(\sigma_{e}))=\mathcal{L}_{[X,Y]}(\sigma_{e})   , we find that the right-hand side of equation   1.3   equals  
 

  
  
  
       (       ℒ  X   ​   (    Γ  α   ​   (  Y  )    )    −    ℒ  Y   ​   (    Γ  α   ​   (  X  )    )     +    Γ  α   ​   (  X  )   ​   Γ  α   ​   (  Y  )     −    Γ  α   ​   (  Y  )   ​   Γ  α   ​   (  X  )    −    Γ  α   ​   (   [   X  ,  Y   ]   )     )   ​   σ  e    .   \displaystyle\Big(\mathcal{L}_{X}(\Gamma^{\alpha}(Y))-\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\Gamma^{\alpha}(X))+\Gamma^{\alpha}(X)\Gamma^{\alpha}(Y)-\Gamma^{\alpha}(Y)\Gamma^{\alpha}(X)-\Gamma^{\alpha}([X,Y])\Big)\sigma_{e}.    
    
 
  On the other hand, in local coordinates, for     v  ∈   𝕜  k    v\in\Bbbk^{k}   ,  
 

  
  
      (   −    R  v   ​   (  X  ,  Y  )     )   e   \displaystyle(-R_{v}(X,Y))_{e}    
       =     π  2   ∘   ψ  α    ​   (   K  ⁡   (    [    X  hor   ,   Y  hor    ]   ​   (  v  )    )    )     \displaystyle=\pi_{2}\circ\psi_{\alpha}\bigl(K([X_{\mathrm{hor}},Y_{\mathrm{hor}}](v))\bigr)    
    
  
  
  
     =   π   ∙  ×  𝕜     k      (  T   ψ  α    (   [   X  hor   ,   Y  hor   ]    (  v  )   −    [  X  ,  Y  ]   hor    (  v  )   )   )    \displaystyle=\pi_{\bullet\times\Bbbk^{k}}(T\psi_{\alpha}([X_{\mathrm{hor}},Y_{\mathrm{hor}}](v)-[X,Y]_{\mathrm{hor}}(v)))    
    
  
  
  
     =   π   ∙  ×  𝕜     k      (   [  T   ψ  α    (   X  hor   )   ,  T   ψ  α    (   Y  hor   )   ]    (  v  )   −  T   ψ  α    (    [  X  ,  Y  ]   hor    (  v  )   )   )    \displaystyle=\pi_{\bullet\times\Bbbk^{k}}([T\psi_{\alpha}(X_{\mathrm{hor}}),T\psi_{\alpha}(Y_{\mathrm{hor}})](v)-T\psi_{\alpha}([X,Y]_{\mathrm{hor}}(v)))    
    
  
  
  
     =   π   ∙  ×  𝕜     k      (   T  v    (  T   ψ  α    (   Y  hor   )   )    (  T   ψ  α    (   X  hor    (  v  )   )   )   −    \displaystyle=\pi_{\bullet\times\Bbbk^{k}}(T_{v}(T\psi_{\alpha}(Y_{\mathrm{hor}}))(T\psi_{\alpha}(X_{\mathrm{hor}}(v)))-    
    
  
  
  
       (     T  v   ​   (   T  ​   ψ  α   ​   (   X  hor   )    )   ​   (   T  ​   ψ  α   ​   (    Y  hor   ​   (  v  )    )    )    −   T  ​   ψ  α   ​   (     [   X  ,  Y   ]   hor   ​   (  v  )    )     )   ,  (*)      \displaystyle(T_{v}(T\psi_{\alpha}(X_{\mathrm{hor}}))(T\psi_{\alpha}(Y_{\mathrm{hor}}(v)))-T\psi_{\alpha}([X,Y]_{\mathrm{hor}}(v))),\quad\textnormal{(*)}\quad    
    
 
  while  
 

  
  
     T  ​   ψ  α   ​   (   X  hor   )   ​   (  U  ,  W  )    \displaystyle T\psi_{\alpha}(X_{\mathrm{hor}})(U,W)    
        =   (   T  ​  X  ​   (  U  )    ,    −    U  ⁡   (    Γ  α   ​   (  X  )    )    ​  v    −    Γ  α   ​   (  X  )   ​  W    )    ,   \displaystyle=(TX(U)\;,\;-U(\Gamma^{\alpha}(X))v-\Gamma^{\alpha}(X)W),    
    
 
  which implies that  
 

  
  
      T  v   ​   (   T  ​   ψ  α   ​   (   Y  hor   )    )   ​   (   T  ​   ψ  α   ​   (   X  hor   )    )    \displaystyle T_{v}(T\psi_{\alpha}(Y_{\mathrm{hor}}))(T\psi_{\alpha}(X_{\mathrm{hor}}))    
        =   (   T  ​  Y  ​   (  X  )    ,    −    X  ⁡   (    Γ  α   ​   (  Y  )    )    ​  v    +    Γ  α   ​   (  Y  )   ​   Γ  α   ​   (  X  )   ​  v    )    .   \displaystyle=(TY(X)\;,\;-X(\Gamma^{\alpha}(Y))v+\Gamma^{\alpha}(Y)\Gamma^{\alpha}(X)v).    
    
 
  Moreover,  
 

  
  
      π   ∙  ×  𝕜     k      (  T   ψ  α     [  X  ,  Y  ]   hor    (  v  )   )   =  −   Γ  α    (   [  X  ,  Y  ]   )   v  .   \pi_{\bullet\times\Bbbk^{k}}(T\psi_{\alpha}[X,Y]_{\mathrm{hor}}(v))=-\Gamma^{\alpha}([X,Y])v.    
    
 
  We therefore find from     (  ∗  )   (*)    that  
 

  
  
      (    R  v   ​   (  X  ,  Y  )    )   e   \displaystyle(R_{v}(X,Y))_{e}    
       =     (     ℒ  X   ​   (    Γ  α   ​   (  Y  )    )    −    ℒ  Y   ​   (    Γ  α   ​   (  X  )    )     )   ​  v   +   (     Γ  α   ​   (  X  )   ​   Γ  α   ​   (  Y  )    −    Γ  α   ​   (  Y  )   ​   Γ  α   ​   (  X  )     CLOSE     \displaystyle=(\mathcal{L}_{X}(\Gamma^{\alpha}(Y))-\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\Gamma^{\alpha}(X)))v+(\Gamma^{\alpha}(X)\Gamma^{\alpha}(Y)-\Gamma^{\alpha}(Y)\Gamma^{\alpha}(X)    
    
  
  
  
       OPEN   −    Γ  α   ​   (   [   X  ,  Y   ]   )     )   ​  v   .   \displaystyle-\Gamma^{\alpha}([X,Y]))v.    
    
 
  The equality follows.
∎  
 
 
 
 Using this formula, we can interpret curvature as a measure of the extent to which     X  ↦   ∇  X    X\mapsto\nabla_{X}    fails to be a Lie algebra homomorphism  18    18 
                 18 
                
                
                
              Here, we understand this to be a map from the Lie algebra of vector fields on    M  M    to the commutator Lie algebra of the associative algebra of operators      Γ  ⁡   (  V  )     ⟶      Γ  ⁡   (  V  )     \Gamma(V)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Gamma(V)    under composition.   .
Moreover, it shows us that     R  v   R_{v}    depends linearly on    v  v   , so we can equivalently view    R  R    as a morphism of vector bundles over    M  M   : 
 

  
  
        T  ​  M   ∧   T  ​  M     ⟶  R    End  ⁡   (  V  )     .   TM\wedge TM\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{End}(V).    
    
 
 This fact can equivalently be expressed as     R  ∈    Ω  2   ​   (  M  ,    End  ⁡   (  V  )     ⟶     M   )     R\in\Omega^{2}(M,\mathrm{End}(V)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}M)   . 
 
 
 In the preceding proof, we have found another useful expression for the curvature. If we agree to write      d  ​   Γ  α   ​   (  X  ,  Y  )    :=     ℒ  X   ​   (    Γ  α   ​   (  Y  )    )    −    ℒ  Y   ​   (    Γ  α   ​   (  X  )    )    −    Γ  α   ​   (   [   X  ,  Y   ]   )      d\Gamma^{\alpha}(X,Y):=\mathcal{L}_{X}(\Gamma^{\alpha}(Y))-\mathcal{L}_{Y}(\Gamma^{\alpha}(X))-\Gamma^{\alpha}([X,Y])    and       Γ  α   ∧    Γ  α   ​   (  X  ,  Y  )     :=     Γ  α   ​   (  X  )   ​   Γ  α   ​   (  Y  )    −    Γ  α   ​   (  Y  )   ​   Γ  α   ​   (  X  )      \Gamma^{\alpha}\wedge\Gamma^{\alpha}(X,Y):=\Gamma^{\alpha}(X)\Gamma^{\alpha}(Y)-\Gamma^{\alpha}(Y)\Gamma^{\alpha}(X)   , then the following holds. 
 
 
   Corollary 1.4.5   .  
 
  In a local frame     (   e  i   )   (e_{i})   , the curvature can be expressed as follows:  
 

  
  
       R  ​    (  X  ,  Y  )   e    =   (     d  ​   Γ  α   ​   (  X  ,  Y  )    +   Γ  α    ∧    Γ  α   ​   (  X  ,  Y  )     )    .   R(X,Y)_{e}=\Big(d\Gamma^{\alpha}(X,Y)+\Gamma^{\alpha}\wedge\Gamma^{\alpha}(X,Y)\Big).    
    
 
 
 
 
 This result closely resembles that of theorem   1.3.6   for principal connections. Remember, though, that this result only holds locally, as     Γ  α   \Gamma^{\alpha}    is only defined in a local frame. Nevertheless, the resemblance is not a coincidence. As we will see, it is a consequence of the fact that an affine connection is induced from a principal connection on the frame bundle. 
 
 
 
  1.4.3  Equivalent Notions of a Connection 

 
 In section   1.1.6  , we have seen that there is an equivalence of categories between     G  ​  L  ​   (   𝕜  k   )    GL(\Bbbk^{k})   -principal bundles and    k  k   -dimensional vector bundles over a manifold    M  M   , in the form of the associated vector bundle construction and the frame bundle construction. The question is whether one can, given a suitable connection on a vector bundle, construct one on the frame bundle and vice versa. The answer turns out to be affirmative. 
 
 
   Claim 1.4.6   .  
 
  Suppose     V   ⟶  π   M   V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    is a    k  k   -dimensional vector bundle. Then, there is a canonical one-to-one correspondence between affine connections on    V  V    and principal connections on the frame bundle      F  λ   ​   (  V  )    F^{\lambda}(V)   , where one direction of the correspondence is precisely the induced connection (if we use the natural identification       F  λ   ​   (  V  )   ​   [  λ  ]    ≅  V   F^{\lambda}(V)[\lambda]\cong V   ).  
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
  Suppose we are given an affine connection     P  V   P_{V}    on    V  V   . We take      F   T  ​  λ     (   P  V   )   :=   P  V   ∘  −   F^{T\lambda}(P_{V}):=P_{V}\circ-    as a connection on      F  λ   ​   (  V  )    F^{\lambda}(V)   . (This notation is of course suggestive, as it resembles that of the frame bundle functor     F   T  ​  λ    F^{T\lambda}    for the effective action     T  ​  λ   T\lambda   . However,     P  V   P_{V}    is not invertible on the fibre, so technically     F   T  ​  λ    F^{T\lambda}    is not defined on it.) Clearly, this yields an equivariant connection precisely since     P  V   P_{V}    is affine. Then, if we take the induced connection on the associated bundle, we obtain  
 

  
  
      (    F   T  ​  λ    ​   (   P  V   )    )   ​   [  λ  ]   ​   (   [    (  f  ,  w  )   ,   (  u  ,  v  )    ]   )    \displaystyle(F^{T\lambda}(P_{V}))[\lambda]([(f,w),(u,v)])    
       =   [     F   T  ​  λ    ​   (   P  V   )   ​   (  f  ,  w  )    ,   (  u  ,  v  )    ]    \displaystyle=[F^{T\lambda}(P_{V})(f,w),(u,v)]    
    
  
  
  
        =   [     P  V   ∘   (  f  ,  w  )    ,   (  u  ,  v  )    ]    .   \displaystyle=[P_{V}\circ(f,w),(u,v)].    
    
 
  We conclude that         (    F   T  ​  λ    ​   (   P  V   )    )   ​   [  λ  ]    ∘  T   ​  q  ​   (   (  f  ,  w  )   ,  −  )    =   T  ​  q  ​   (    P  V   ∘   (  f  ,  w  )    ,  −  )     (F^{T\lambda}(P_{V}))[\lambda]\circ Tq((f,w),-)=Tq(P_{V}\circ(f,w),-)   , i.e.     P  V   P_{V}    and      (    F   T  ​  λ    ​   (   P  V   )    )   ​   [  λ  ]    (F^{T\lambda}(P_{V}))[\lambda]    are related by the derivative of the natural isomorphism               V  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}                 F  λ   ​   (  V  )   ​   [  λ  ]    .   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\lambda}(V)[\lambda].}                     
  (Compare this with equation     (  ∗  )   (*)    on page   1.1.5  .) We conclude that     F   T  ​  λ    F^{T\lambda}    is injective.  
 
 
  Conversely, suppose that     P  V  ′   P_{V}^{\prime}    is an equivariant connection on      F  λ   ​   (  V  )    F^{\lambda}(V)   . Then, by     G  ​  L  ​   (   𝕜  k   )    GL(\Bbbk^{k})   -equivariance of     P  V  ′   P_{V}^{\prime}   , the induced connection on the associated vector bundle is affine. Suppose that it corresponds to a connection     P  V   P_{V}    on    V  V    through the identification       F  λ   ​   (  V  )   ​   [  λ  ]    ≅  V   F^{\lambda}(V)[\lambda]\cong V   . This means that  
 

  
  
       T  ​  q  ​   (    P  V  ′   ​   (  f  ,  w  )    ,  −  )    =      P  V  ′   ​   [  λ  ]    ∘  T   ​  q  ​   (   (  f  ,  w  )   ,  −  )     =  !    T  ​  q  ​   (    P  V   ∘   (  f  ,  w  )    ,  −  )     .   Tq(P_{V}^{\prime}(f,w),-)=P_{V}^{\prime}[\lambda]\circ Tq((f,w),-)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}Tq(P_{V}\circ(f,w),-).    
    
 
  This implies that      (   P  V  ′    (  f  ,  w  )   ⋅  g  ,   g   −  1    ⋅  −  )   =   (   P  V   ∘   (  f  ,  w  )   ,  −  )    (P_{V}^{\prime}(f,w)\cdot g,g^{-1}\cdot-)=(P_{V}\circ(f,w),-)   , for some     g  ∈   T  ​  G  ​  L  ​   (   𝕜  k   )     g\in TGL(\Bbbk^{k})   . Since this equality holds for every second component,    g  g    acts trivially on     T  ​   𝕜  k    T\Bbbk^{k}   . By faithfulness of the tangent representation     T  ​  λ   T\lambda   ,     g  =   (   id   𝕜  k    ,  0  )    g=(\mathrm{id}_{\Bbbk^{k}},0)   . Therefore       P  V  ′   ​   (  f  ,  w  )    =    P  V   ∘   (  f  ,  w  )    =    F   T  ​  λ    ​   (   P  V   )   ​   (  f  ,  w  )     P_{V}^{\prime}(f,w)=P_{V}\circ(f,w)=F^{T\lambda}(P_{V})(f,w)   , so     F   T  ​  λ    F^{T\lambda}    is surjective.
∎  
 
 
 
   Remark 1.4.7   .  
 
 Perhaps this result would be better placed in section   1.3.3  , in a more general form. Certainly, if one were to formulate an appropriate notion of a ‘    (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -connection’ in a fibre bundle with structure group     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   , one would expect that the same construction would work to establish a one-to-one correspondence between such connections on the     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -fibre bundle and principal connections on the corresponding generalised frame bundle. (I would opt to define such connections as ones whose vertical projections are maps of     (   T  ​  G   ,   T  ​  λ   )   (TG,T\lambda)   -fibre bundles over     T  ​  M   TM   ). One could even go a step further and define a category of     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -fibre bundles with     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -connections, in which a natural definition of arrows would be maps of     (  G  ,  λ  )   (G,\lambda)   -fibre bundles such that the two connections involved would be related by pullback along such a fibre bundle, and try to extend the equivalence of categories of theorem   1.1.3   to one between such categories. However, such a result would probably have few applications and would be established purely for aesthetic purposes. Therefore, it is omitted. 
 
 
 
 We see that we can interpret the theory of vector bundles with affine connections as a special case of that of principal bundles with equivariant connections. In particular, we have the following. 
 
 
   Corollary 1.4.8   .  
 
  Affine connections are complete.  
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
  In the light of claim   1.4.6  , this follows immediately from the fact that the induced parallel transport on the associated bundle is    q  q   -related to that on the original principal bundle, which we already know to be defined along all curves by theorem   1.3.12  .  
 
 
  Of course, we could also write out the differential equation defining parallel transport in local coordinates, which would be linear in the case of an affine connection. The global existence theorem for linear differential equations would do the rest.
∎  
 
 
 
 To complete the equivalence between affine connections on a vector bundle and equivariant connections on its frame bundle, we state the following result, due to Kolář et al.  [ 19 ] , relating the two derivatives. We have seen in remark   1.3.9   that for a principal    G  G   -bundle     P   ⟶   π  ~    M   P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{\pi}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    and a vector space    W  W    with a linear    G  G   -action    λ  \lambda    we have an identification       Ω  hor  k   ​    (  P  ,  W  )   G      ⟶  ≃    Ψ  λ      Ω  k   ​   (  M  ,   P  ⁡   [  λ  ]    )     \Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{k}(P,W)^{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Psi_{\lambda}}}{{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}}\Omega^{k}(M,P[\lambda])   . In particular, we can identify an equivariant function     P   ⟶  Φ   W   P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Phi}}{{\longrightarrow}}W    with a section    σ  \sigma    of the associated bundle     P  ⁡   [  λ  ]    P[\lambda]   . In the presence of a connection, we can take the exterior covariant derivative along the horizontal lift of a vector field     X  ∈   𝒳  ⁡   (  M  )     X\in\mathcal{X}(M)    on one side to obtain another equivariant function     P   ⟶    d  ω   ​  Φ  ​   (   X  hor   )     W   P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d^{\omega}\Phi(X_{\mathrm{hor}})}}{{\longrightarrow}}W    and on the other we can take the covariant derivative (of the induced connection) along the vector field to obtain another section:       ∇  X   σ   ∈   Γ  ⁡   (   P  ⁡   [  λ  ]    )     \nabla_{X}\sigma\in\Gamma(P[\lambda])   . One wonders if the two are related. Indeed, they turn out to coincide in the identification     Ψ  λ   \Psi_{\lambda}   . 
 
 
 If we extend    ∇  \nabla    from       Ω  0   ​   (  M  ,   P  ⁡   [  λ  ]    )    =   Γ  ⁡   (   P  ⁡   [  λ  ]    )     \Omega^{0}(M,P[\lambda])=\Gamma(P[\lambda])    to      Ω  k   ​   (  M  ,   P  ​   [  λ  ]    )    \Omega^{k}(M,P[\lambda])   , by setting, for     α  ∈    Ω  k   ​   (  M  ,   P  ⁡   [  λ  ]    )     \alpha\in\Omega^{k}(M,P[\lambda])    and      X  0   ,  …  ,    X  k   ∈   𝒳  ⁡   (  M  )      X_{0},\ldots,X_{k}\in\mathcal{X}(M)   : 
 

  
  
       (    d  ∇   ​  α   )   ​   (   X  0   ,  …  ,   X  k   )    :=    ∑   i  =  0   k      (   −  1   )   i   ​    ∇   X  i     (   α  ⁡   (   X  0   ,  …  ,    X  i   ^   ,   …  ​   X  k    )    )       (d_{\nabla}\alpha)(X_{0},\ldots,X_{k}):=\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{i}\nabla_{X_{i}}(\alpha(X_{0},\ldots,\hat{X_{i}},\ldots X_{k}))    
    
 
 

  
  
     +   ∑   0  ≤  i  <  j  ≤  k      (  −  1  )    i  +  j    α   (   [   X  i   ,   X  j   ]   ,   X  0   ,  …  ,    X  ^   i   ,  …  ,    X  ^   j   ,  …   X  k   )    +\sum_{0\leq i<j\leq k}(-1)^{i+j}\alpha([X_{i},X_{j}],X_{0},\ldots,\hat{X}_{i},\ldots,\hat{X}_{j},\ldots X_{k})    
    
 
 - this is just the definition of the ordinary differential    d  d    with     ∇   X  i    \nabla_{X_{i}}    replacing     ℒ   X  i    \mathcal{L}_{X_{i}}    - then we have the following. 
 
 
   Claim 1.4.9   .  
 
     d  ∇   d_{\nabla}     acts on     P  ⁡   [  λ  ]    P[\lambda]   -valued differential forms on    M  M    as     d  ω   d^{\omega}    does on equivariant horizontal ones on    P  P   , in the sense that                 Ω  hor  k   ​    (  P  ,  W  )   G    {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{k}(P,W)^{G}}                Ω  k   ​   (  M  ,   P  ​   [  λ  ]    )    {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{k}(M,P[\lambda])}                  Ω  hor   k  +  1    ​    (  P  ,  W  )   G    {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{k+1}(P,W)^{G}}                 Ω   k  +  1    ​   (  M  ,   P  ​   [  λ  ]    )    .   {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{k+1}(M,P[\lambda]).}                         Ψ  λ   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{\lambda}}              ≃  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}                       d  ω   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\omega}}                    ↺  {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circlearrowleft}                       d  ∇   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{\nabla}}                       Ψ  λ   \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{\lambda}}              ≃  \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}           
 
 
 
 The proof, which can be found in  [ 19 ] , proceeds as one would expect. One first checks the identity on     Ω  0   \Omega^{0}   . (This is a short computation.) Next, one extends it to elements of      Ω  k   ​   (  M  ,   P  ​   [  λ  ]    )    \Omega^{k}(M,P[\lambda])    that can be decomposed as     σ  ⊗  η   \sigma\otimes\eta   , with     σ  ∈    Ω  0   ​   (  M  ,   P  ⁡   [  λ  ]    )     \sigma\in\Omega^{0}(M,P[\lambda])    and     η  ∈    Ω  k   ​   (  M  )     \eta\in\Omega^{k}(M)   . Finally, one obtains the general statement by working locally in a frame of     P  ⁡   [  λ  ]    P[\lambda]   : every vector-valued form is a finite sum of such terms, and the two sides are local and    𝕜  \Bbbk   -linear. 
 
 
 
  1.4.4  Categorical Constructions on Affine Connections 

 
 It turns out to be possible to transfer a connection to quite a few categorical constructions we can build out of a vector bundle. We can, for instance, give a sensible definition of a  dual connection  on the dual bundle of a vector bundle with a connection. The same applies for the tensor product. 
 
 
 Suppose     V   ⟶  π   M   V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    is a     𝕜  k   \Bbbk^{k}   -vector bundle that is equipped with an affine connection     P  V   P_{V}   . We have seen in claim   1.4.6   that this canonically induces a connection      F   T  ​  λ    ​   (   P  V   )    F^{T\lambda}(P_{V})    on the frame bundle      F  λ   ​   (  V  )    F^{\lambda}(V)   . Note that we have a canonical left action     λ  ∗   \lambda^{*}    of     G  ​  L  ​   (   𝕜  k   )    GL(\Bbbk^{k})    on     𝕜     k      ∗    \Bbbk^{k}{}^{*}    by (inverse) precomposition     G  L   (   𝕜  k   )   ∋  g  ↦  −  ∘   g   −  1    ∈  G  L   (   𝕜  k    )      ∗     GL(\Bbbk^{k})\ni g\mapsto-\circ g^{-1}\in GL(\Bbbk^{k}{}^{*})   . We are in the familiar setting in which we construct an associated vector bundle. Note that this is just the dual vector bundle      V  ∗    ⟶   π  ∗    M   V^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi^{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M   . This gives us a canonical way to transfer the connection on    V  V    to     V  ∗   V^{*}   :      F   T  ​  λ    ​   (   P  V   )    F^{T\lambda}(P_{V})    induces an affine connection on the associated bundle      V  ∗   →  M   V^{*}\to M   :       F   T  ​  λ    ​   (   P  V   )   ​   [   λ  ∗   ]    F^{T\lambda}(P_{V})[\lambda^{*}]   . It is easily verified, using the characterisation of the covariant derivative given in claim   1.4.3   in combination with the Leibniz rule, that this yields the following relation between the covariant derivatives: 
 

  
  
        ⟨     ∇  X  ∗   ξ   ,  σ   ⟩   +   ⟨   ξ  ,    ∇  X   σ    ⟩    =   X  ⁡   (   ⟨   ξ  ,  σ   ⟩   )     ,   \langle\>\nabla^{*}_{X}\xi\,,\sigma\>\rangle+\langle\>\xi\,,\nabla_{X}\sigma\>\rangle=X(\langle\>\xi\,,\sigma\>\rangle),    
    
 
 where     X  ∈   𝒳  ⁡   (  M  )     X\in\mathcal{X}(M)   ,     σ  ∈   Γ  ⁡   (  π  )     \sigma\in\Gamma(\pi)    and     ξ  ∈   Γ  ⁡   (   π  ∗   )     \xi\in\Gamma(\pi^{*})   . 
 
 
 In the case of a complex vector bundle, there is the concept of the complex conjugate bundle. This is easily seen to be an associated bundle as well. Indeed, let     V   ⟶  π   M   V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    be a vector bundle and describe it as an associated bundle of     P   ⟶   π  ~    M   P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{\pi}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    corresponding to the canonical action    λ  \lambda    of     G  ​  L  ​   (   ℂ  k   )    GL(\mathbb{C}\;^{k})    on     ℂ  k   \mathbb{C}\;^{k}   . Then we have an action     λ  ¯   \bar{\lambda}    of     G  ​  L  ​   (   ℂ  k   )    GL(\mathbb{C}\;^{k})    on      ℂ  k   ¯   \overline{\mathbb{C}\;^{k}}   , by       λ  ¯   ​   (  g  ,   v  ¯   )    :=    λ  ⁡   (  g  ,  v  )    ¯    \bar{\lambda}(g,\bar{v}):=\overline{\lambda(g,v)}   . This induces a connection on the complex conjugate bundle      V  ¯    ⟶   π  ¯    M   \bar{V}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bar{\pi}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M   . The covariant derivative     ∇  ¯   \bar{\nabla}    of this  complex conjugate connection  acts on sections     ψ  ∈   Γ  ⁡   (   π  ¯   )     \psi\in\Gamma(\bar{\pi})    as one would expect: 
 

  
  
         ∇  ¯   X   ​  ψ   =     ∇  X    ψ  ¯    ¯    ,   \bar{\nabla}_{X}\psi=\overline{\nabla_{X}\bar{\psi}},    
    
 
 where    ∇  \nabla    is the covariant derivative of the original connection on    π  \pi    (of which we understand     ψ  ¯   \bar{\psi}    to be a section). This construction is important in physics when one encounters spinors. 
 
 
 Similarly, if      V  ′    ⟶   π  ′    M   V^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi^{\prime}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    is a second vector bundle, of dimension, say,    l  l    over    𝕜  \Bbbk   , on which an affine connection     P  V  ′   P_{V}^{\prime}    is defined, we have a canonical notion of a  tensor product connection  on      V  ⊗   V  ′     ⟶   π  ⊗   π  ′     M   V\otimes V^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi\otimes\pi^{\prime}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M   . Again, we note that we obtain principal connections      F   T  ​  λ    ​   (   P  V   )    F^{T\lambda}(P_{V})    and      F   T  ​   λ  ′     ​   (   P  V  ′   )    F^{T\lambda^{\prime}}(P_{V}^{\prime})    on the frame bundles. This defines a principal connection       F   T  ​  λ    ​   (   P  V   )     ×  M     F   T  ​   λ  ′     ​   (   P  V  ′   )     F^{T\lambda}(P_{V})\times_{M}F^{T\lambda^{\prime}}(P_{V}^{\prime})    on the spliced principal       G  ​  L  ​   (   𝕜  k   )    ×  G   ​  L  ​   (   𝕜  l   )    GL(\Bbbk^{k})\times GL(\Bbbk^{l})   -bundle        F  λ   ​   (  V  )     ×  M     F   λ  ′    ​   (   V  ′   )     →  M   F^{\lambda}(V)\times_{M}F^{\lambda^{\prime}}(V^{\prime})\to M   . Again, we can form an associated vector bundle by the tensor product action     λ  ⊗   λ  ′    \lambda\otimes\lambda^{\prime}    of       G  ​  L  ​   (   𝕜  k   )    ×  G   ​  L  ​   (   𝕜  l   )    GL(\Bbbk^{k})\times GL(\Bbbk^{l})    on      𝕜  k   ⊗   𝕜  l    \Bbbk^{k}\otimes\Bbbk^{l}   . This vector bundle is, of course, just the tensor product bundle      V  ⊗   V  ′    →  M   V\otimes V^{\prime}\to M   . Since we have realised this bundle as an associated bundle, we obtain an induced connection      (     F   T  ​  λ    ​   (   P  V   )     ×  M     F   T  ​   λ  ′     ​   (   P  V  ′   )     )   ​   [   λ  ⊗   λ  ′    ]    (F^{T\lambda}(P_{V})\times_{M}F^{T\lambda^{\prime}}(P_{V}^{\prime}))[\lambda\otimes\lambda^{\prime}]    on it. In terms of the covariant derivative, this means that, 
 

  
  
      ∇  X        ⊗    (  σ  ⊗   σ  ′   )   =   (   ∇  X   σ  )   ⊗   σ  ′   +  σ  ⊗   (   ∇  X  ′    σ  ′   )   ,   {}^{\otimes}\nabla_{X}(\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\prime})=(\nabla_{X}\sigma)\otimes\sigma^{\prime}+\sigma\otimes(\nabla^{\prime}_{X}\sigma^{\prime}),    
    
 
 if     X  ∈   𝒳  ⁡   (  M  )     X\in\mathcal{X}(M)   ,     σ  ∈   Γ  ⁡   (  π  )     \sigma\in\Gamma(\pi)    and      σ  ′   ∈   Γ  ⁡   (   π  ′   )     \sigma^{\prime}\in\Gamma(\pi^{\prime})    and if we denote the covariant derivative on     V  ⊗   V  ′    V\otimes V^{\prime}    by        ⊗   ∇   {}^{\otimes}\nabla   . Again, this identity is almost immediate if one uses the relation of claim   1.4.3   between the covariant derivative and parallel transport. Note that physicists use these induced connections all the time when dealing with tensor bundles, for example in the context of general relativity. 
 
 
 An analogous construction for which we can also define an induced connection is the direct sum (categorical product). We have already seen how to define the product connection for general fibre bundles. In this context, it will be no different. We can also understand the direct sum bundle to be an associated bundle and construct the connection as we did for the tensor product (using the direct sum action). The result on the covariant derivative is as follows: 
 

  
  
      ∇  X        ⊕    (  σ  ⊕   σ  ′   )   =   ∇  X   σ  ⊕   ∇  X  ′    σ  ′    {}^{\oplus}\nabla_{X}(\sigma\oplus\sigma^{\prime})=\nabla_{X}\sigma\oplus\nabla^{\prime}_{X}\sigma^{\prime}    
    
 
 and we extend by linearity. 
 
 
 Finally, the construction works equally for    Hom  \mathrm{Hom}   -bundles. Obviously, these can also be seen as associated vector bundles, since we have the canonical isomorphism      Hom  ⁡   (  V  ,   V  ′   )    ≅    V  ∗   ⊗   V  ′     \mathrm{Hom}(V,V^{\prime})\cong V^{*}\otimes V^{\prime}   . 
 
 
 
 
  1.5  Manifolds with Connections 

 
 An important context in which one encounters connections is through a (pseudo-)metric on the tangent bundle of a manifold    M  M   . This induces the so-called Levi-Civita connection on     T  ​  M   TM   . This subject, however, will be dealt with in section   1.6  . 
 
 
 The more general class of arbitrary affine connections on a tangent bundle is dealt with first, in the hope that the reader realises which aspects are special to the case of Riemannian geometry and which structure derives from a more general principle. If a manifold is equipped with an affine connection on its tangent bundle, one sometimes simply says a connection is defined on the manifold. These manifolds form a broader class than the (pseudo)Riemannian manifolds. They have important applications. 
 
 
 For instance, they are used in the Newton-Cartan theory of gravity, which is a beautiful theory of Newtonian gravity, using a geometry very similar to that of the general theory of relativity. In this theory, the particle moves through Newtonian spacetime along geodesics that are determined by a non-metric connection. In this way, one can use the language of manifolds with connections to describe Newtonian as well as Einsteinian gravity and one can give a particularly clear demonstration that the latter reduces to the former in the limit     c  →  ∞   c\to\infty   . A useful reference on this subject is a text by Cartan himself,  [ 8 ] .
 
 A first remark, although it is not of very fundamental importance, is the following. Let us adopt the notation       T   (  k  ,  l  )    ​  M   :=     ⨂  M  k    T  ​  M    ⊗    ⨂  l     T  ∗   ​  M      T^{(k,l)}M:=\bigotimes_{M}^{k}TM\otimes\bigotimes^{l}T^{*}M   . As we have seen, the affine connection on     T  ​  M   TM    induces one and therefore also a corresponding covariant derivative on these tensor bundles. We have seen that       ∇  X   σ   ​   (  m  )    \nabla_{X}\sigma(m)    only depends on the value of    X  X    at    m  m    (and on the value of    σ  \sigma    along a compatible curve). Therefore, if     S  ∈   Γ  ⁡   (    T   (  k  ,  l  )    ​  M   )     S\in\Gamma(T^{(k,l)}M)   , then we can interpret     ∇  S   \nabla S    as an element of      Γ  ⁡   (      T  ∗   ​  M   ⊗   T   (  k  ,  l  )     ​  M   )    ≅   Γ  ⁡   (    T   (  k  ,   l  +  1   )    ​  M   )     \Gamma(T^{*}M\otimes T^{(k,l)}M)\cong\Gamma(T^{(k,l+1)}M)   , where 
 

  
  
        (   ∇  S   )   ​   (  X  ,   α  1   ,  …  ,   α  k   ,   Y  1   ,  …  ,   Y  l   )    =    (    ∇  X   S   )   ​   (   α  1   ,  …  ,   α  k   ,   Y  1   ,  …  ,   Y  l   )     .   (\nabla S)(X;\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{l})=(\nabla_{X}S)(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{l}).    
    
 
 This is a notation that physicists use a lot in general relativity. 
 
 
  1.5.1  Geodesics 

 
 The most important new concept that we encounter when specialising from vector bundles with affine connections to manifolds with affine connections is that of  geodesics . The reader might know the concept of a geodesic from Riemannian geometry, where a geodesic might be defined as a curve that locally minimises the path length (or, equivalently, the energy integral) between two points that lie on it. Of course, this definition cannot be formulated in the context of an arbitrary manifold with an affine connection. However, a geodesic can equivalently be defined as a curve whose tangent vectors are related by parallel transport along the curve itself. To be precise, a curve     ]  −  ϵ  ,  ϵ  [   ⟶  c   M   ]-\epsilon,\epsilon[\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    is called a(n) (affinely parametrised) geodesic if 
 

  
  
        D  ​   c  ′     d  ​  t    =  0   .   \frac{Dc^{\prime}}{dt}=0.    
    
 
 This definition makes equal sense when the connection is not a Levi-Civita connection of some metric. 
 
 
 Equivalently, for a given affine connection on     T  ​  M   TM   , one can define the  geodesic spray  to be the unique horizontal vector field    W  W    on     T  ​  M   TM    such that      T  ​  π  ​   (   W  ⁡   (  v  )    )    =  v   T\pi(W(v))=v    for all     v  ∈   T  ​  M    v\in TM   . A geodesic is then precisely the projection along    π  \pi    of an integral curve of    W  W   . More generally, we call a vector field    W  W    on     T  ​  M   TM    a  spray , if       T  ​   π  M    ∘  W   =   id   T  ​  M     T\pi_{M}\circ W=\mathrm{id}_{TM}    and, in a chart induced by the double tangent bundle functor,      W  ⁡   (  x  ,  y  )    =   (  x  ,  y  ,  y  ,    A  x   ​   (  y  )    )    W(x,y)=(x,y;y,A_{x}(y))   , where     y  ↦    A  x   ​   (  y  )     y\mapsto A_{x}(y)    is quadratic. In this terminology, the geodesic spray of a connection is the unique horizontal spray. 
 
 
 
  1.5.2  Torsion 

 
 We have seen that an affine connection on the tangent bundle uniquely determines a geodesic spray. One can ask whether the converse is also true. The answer turns out to be: almost, once we fix a tensor field known as the torsion. 
 
 
 Given a manifold with an affine connection     (  M  ,   P  V   )   (M,P_{V})    we define the  torsion      T  ∈   Γ  ⁡   (    T   (  1  ,  2  )    ​  M   )     T\in\Gamma(T^{(1,2)}M)    of the connection to be the     (  1  ,  2  )   (1,2)   -tensor 
 

  
  
       T  ⁡   (  X  ,  Y  )    :=     ∇  X   Y   −    ∇  Y   X   −   [   X  ,  Y   ]     .   T(X,Y):=\nabla_{X}Y-\nabla_{Y}X-[X,Y].    
    
 
 The following theorem gives an interpretation of the torsion tensor as the freedom in the choice of a connection, after we have specified a certain geodesic spray.  [ 2 ]  
 
 
   Theorem 1.5.1     (Ambrose-Palais-Singer) .  
 
  Let    M  M    be a smooth manifold with a spray    W  W   . Then, for each type     (  1  ,  2  )   (1,2)   -tensor field    T  T    that is anti-symmetric in the last pair of arguments, there exists a unique affine connection on      T  ​  M    ⟶     M   TM\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    such that    W  W    is its geodesic spray and such that    T  T    is its torsion.  
 
 
 
 In particular, for every affine connection on    M  M   , we can find a unique torsion-free one that has the same geodesic spray. If we were to divide the affine connections on a manifold    M  M    into equivalence classes of those that have the same geodesic spray, we could take this to be a canonical representative of each class. In computations, torsion-free connections are often easiest to work with; therefore, for example in the general theory of relativity, one uses this freedom to simplify calculations. 
 
 
 In light of this theorem, moreover, we see that it was no coincidence that Cartan was able to formulate Newtonian gravity using the geometry of an affine connection  19    19 
                 19 
                
                
                
              Indeed, after adjoining the absolute time coordinate in Newton-Cartan spacetime, the Newtonian gravitational force of one point source gives rise to a spray whose spatial part is      A   (  t  ,  x  )     (   t  ˙   ,   x  ˙   )   =  −  γ  x    t  ˙   2   /    ‖  x  ‖   3    A_{(t,x)}(\dot{t},\dot{x})=-\gamma x\dot{t}^{2}/\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{3}   , where    γ  \gamma    is some scale constant for the gravitational interaction. Similarly, arbitrary Newtonian gravitational fields give spatial acceleration     −    ∇  Φ   ​   (  t  ,  x  )   ​    t  ˙   2     -\nabla\Phi(t,x)\dot{t}^{2}   . In particular, the acceleration is quadratic in the full spacetime velocity.   . However, when Cartan formulated his Newton-Cartan theory in 1923, this result would not be proved for forty years. 
 
 
 
 
  1.6  (Pseudo)Riemannian Manifolds and the Levi-Civita Connection 

 
 Suppose    M  M    is a (pseudo-)Riemannian manifold, that is a manifold equipped with a symmetric, non-degenerate tensor     g  ∈   Γ  ⁡   (      T  ∗   ​  M   ⊗   T  ∗    ​  M   )     g\in\Gamma(T^{*}M\otimes T^{*}M)   . Then, there is a canonical choice for a connection on    M  M    (an affine connection on the tangent bundle). I will not give the standard derivation  20    20 
               20 
              
              
              
            This would involve demanding that the metric have covariant derivative zero and showing that this defines a unique connection for a specified torsion tensor (the canonical choice being torsion zero). My derivation is somewhat less standard, but yields the same connection.    of this connection. The one I give might be somewhat more involved, but is more in line with the rest of this thesis. In particular, it will be more natural from a physicist’s point of view, since it starts from an action principle. 
 
 
 The idea is the following. From the metric, we can define a so-called  energy integral  along each path     ]  −  ϵ  ,  ϵ  [   ⟶  c   M   ]-\epsilon,\epsilon[\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\longrightarrow}}M   :       S  g   ​   (  c  )    :=    1  2   ​    ∫   −  ϵ   ϵ     g  ⁡   (    c  ′   ​   (  t  )    ,    c  ′   ​   (  t  )    )    ​   𝑑  t       S_{g}(c):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{-\epsilon}^{\epsilon}g(c^{\prime}(t),c^{\prime}(t))dt   . We will define the so-called  Levi-Civita connection  associated with    g  g    in such a way that its geodesics are precisely the curves for which the energy integral is stationary with respect to all compactly supported variations in the curve. (Note that this yields precisely the straight lines in the case of     M  =   ℝ   p  ,  q     M=\mathbb{R}^{p,q}    with the standard metric.)

   Notation 1.6.2   .  
 
 Note that this claim implies that the energy integral of a curve is stationary with respect to all variations with compact support if and only if 
 

  
  
       g   c  ⁡   (  t  )     ​   (    c  ′′   ​   (  t  )    ,  Z  )    =     1  2   ​   T   c  ⁡   (  t  )     ​  g  ​   (  Z  )   ​   (    c  ′   ​   (  t  )    ,    c  ′   ​   (  t  )    )    −    T   c  ⁡   (  t  )     ​  g  ​   (    c  ′   ​   (  t  )    )   ​   (    c  ′   ​   (  t  )    ,  Z  )      g_{c(t)}(c^{\prime\prime}(t),Z)=\frac{1}{2}T_{c(t)}g(Z)(c^{\prime}(t),c^{\prime}(t))-T_{c(t)}g(c^{\prime}(t))(c^{\prime}(t),Z)
