In general, however, the term        T  ​   R   τ  ⁡   (  p  )     ​   (    T  p   ​   τ   −  1     )    ⋅  τ   ​    (  p  )    −  1     ⋅   ψ  ⁡   (  p  )     TR_{\tau(p)}(T_{p}\tau^{-1})\cdot\tau(p)^{-1}\cdot\psi(p)    does not vanish and therefore gauge invariance is not satisfied.
∎  
 
 
 
 Up to this point I have tried to argue why principal bundles arise in physics. However, no real answer has been given to the question why physicists would be interested in principal connections. The connection in the Kaluza-Klein theory of electromagnetism arose as a technical curiosity rather than as an inevitable consequence of some fundamental demand on our physical theories. However, principal connections arise in a very natural way in gauge theory. In fact, we are forced to introduce them when we want our theory to be gauge-invariant! 
 
 
 Remember that we write      𝒞  ⁡   (  P  )    ⊂    Ω  1   ​   (  P  ,  𝔤  )     \mathscr{C}(P)\subset\Omega^{1}(P,\mathfrak{g})    for the space of principal connections. Note that the right action of     Gau  ​  P   \mathrm{Gau}\,P    on      Ω  1   ​   (  P  ,  𝔤  )    \Omega^{1}(P,\mathfrak{g})    by pullback restricts to     𝒞  ⁡   (  P  )    \mathscr{C}(P)   . 
 
 
   Claim 2.6.3   .  
 
  Let     f  ∈   Gau  ​  P    f\in\mathrm{Gau}\,P    and     ω  ∈   𝒞  ⁡   (  P  )     \omega\in\mathscr{C}(P)    then       f  ∗   ​  ω   ∈   𝒞  ⁡   (  P  )     f^{*}\omega\in\mathscr{C}(P)   .  
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
  Let     Z  ∈  𝔤   Z\in\mathfrak{g}    and     f  ∈   Gau  ​  P    f\in\mathrm{Gau}\,P   . Then       (    f  ∗   ​  ω   )   ​   (    Z  P   ​   (  p  )    )    =   ω  ⁡   (   T  ​  f  ​   Z  P   ​   (  p  )    )    =   ω  ⁡   (      d   d  ​  t    |    t  =  0    ​  f  ​   (   p  ⋅   exp  ⁡   (   t  ​  Z   )     )    )    =   ω  ⁡   (       d   d  ​  t    |    t  =  0    ​  f  ​   (  p  )    ⋅   exp  ⁡   (   t  ​  Z   )     )    =   ω  ⁡   (    Z  P   ​   (   f  ⁡   (  p  )    )    )    =  Z   (f^{*}\omega)(Z_{P}(p))=\omega(TfZ_{P}(p))=\omega(\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}f(p\cdot\exp(tZ)))=\omega(\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}f(p)\cdot\exp(tZ))=\omega(Z_{P}(f(p)))=Z   , so      f  ∗   ​  ω   f^{*}\omega    reproduces vertical vectors.  
 
 
  Moreover, since    f  f    is a map of principal bundles,       ρ  g  ∗   ​   f  ∗   ​  ω   =    f  ∗   ​   ρ  g  ∗   ​  ω   =    f  ∗   ​   Ad   g   −  1     ​  ω   =    Ad   g   −  1     ​   f  ∗   ​  ω    \rho_{g}^{*}f^{*}\omega=f^{*}\rho_{g}^{*}\omega=f^{*}\mathrm{Ad}_{g^{-1}}\omega=\mathrm{Ad}_{g^{-1}}f^{*}\omega   , i.e.      f  ∗   ​  ω   f^{*}\omega    is equivariant in the sense of claim   1.3.4  .
∎  
 
 
 
 Moreover,     Gau  ​  P   \mathrm{Gau}\,P    acts on      C  ∞   ​    (  P  ,  W  )   G    C^{\infty}(P,W)^{G}    on the right by       f  ∗   ​  ψ  ​   (  p  )    :=   ψ  ∘   f  ⁡   (  p  )      f^{*}\psi(p):=\psi\circ f(p)   . 
 
 
   Theorem 2.6.4   .  
 
  Let     L  i   L_{i}    be a    G  G   -invariant Lagrangian. If we define the  interaction action density         C  ∞   ​    (  P  ,  W  )   G    ×   𝒞  ⁡   (  P  )      ⟶   ℒ  i      C  ∞   ​   (  M  )     C^{\infty}(P,W)^{G}\times\mathscr{C}(P)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathscr{L}_{i}}}{{\longrightarrow}}C^{\infty}(M)    by       ℒ  i   ​   (  ψ  ,  ω  )   ​   (  m  )    :=    L  i   ​   (  p  ,   ψ  ⁡   (  p  )    ,    d  ω   ​  ψ  ​   (  p  )    )     \mathscr{L}_{i}(\psi,\omega)(m):=L_{i}(p,\psi(p),d^{\omega}\psi(p))   , for     p  ∈     π  ~    −  1    ​   (  m  )     p\in\tilde{\pi}^{-1}(m)   , then     ℒ  i   \mathscr{L}_{i}    is gauge-invariant in the sense that       ℒ  i   ​   (  ψ  ,  ω  )    =    ℒ  i   ​   (    f  ∗   ​  ψ   ,    f  ∗   ​  ω   )     \mathscr{L}_{i}(\psi,\omega)=\mathscr{L}_{i}(f^{*}\psi,f^{*}\omega)   , for all     f  ∈   Gau  ​  P    f\in\mathrm{Gau}\,P   .  
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
  We begin by checking that     ℒ  i   \mathscr{L}_{i}    is well-defined in this way. Note that by equivariance of       d  ω   ​  ψ   ∈    Ω  hor  1   ​    (  P  ,  W  )   G     d^{\omega}\psi\in\Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{1}(P,W)^{G}   , we have       d  ω   ​  ψ  ​   (   p  ⋅  g   )    =       g   −  1    ⋅   d  ω    ​  ψ  ​   (  p  )    ∘  T   ​   ρ   g   −  1       d^{\omega}\psi(p\cdot g)=g^{-1}\cdot d^{\omega}\psi(p)\circ T\rho_{g^{-1}}   . Therefore  
 

  
  
      L  i   ​   (   p  ⋅  g   ,   ψ  ⁡   (   p  ⋅  g   )    ,    d  ω   ​  ψ  ​   (   p  ⋅  g   )    )    \displaystyle L_{i}(p\cdot g,\psi(p\cdot g),d^{\omega}\psi(p\cdot g))    
       =    L  i   ​   (   p  ⋅  g   ,    g   −  1    ⋅   ψ  ⁡   (  p  )     ,       g   −  1    ⋅   d  ω    ​  ψ  ​   (  p  )    ∘  T   ​   ρ   g   −  1      )     \displaystyle=L_{i}(p\cdot g,g^{-1}\cdot\psi(p),g^{-1}\cdot d^{\omega}\psi(p)\circ T\rho_{g^{-1}})    
    
  
  
  
        =    L  i   ​   (  p  ,   ψ  ⁡   (  p  )    ,    d  ω   ​  ψ  ​   (  p  )    )     ,   \displaystyle=L_{i}(p,\psi(p),d^{\omega}\psi(p)),    
    
 
  where the last identity holds by definition of the interaction Lagrangian.  
 
 
  Now, we verify gauge invariance.  
 

  
  
      ℒ  i   ​   (    f  ∗   ​  ψ   ,    f  ∗   ​  ω   )   ​   (  m  )    \displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{i}(f^{*}\psi,f^{*}\omega)(m)    
       =    L  i   ​   (  p  ,    (    f  ∗   ​  ψ   )   ​   (  p  )    ,    d    f  ∗   ​  ω    ​   f  ∗   ​  ψ  ​   (  p  )    )     \displaystyle=L_{i}(p,(f^{*}\psi)(p),d^{f^{*}\omega}f^{*}\psi(p))    
    
  
  
  
       =    L  i   ​   (  p  ,    (    f  ∗   ​  ψ   )   ​   (  p  )    ,    f  ∗   ​   (    d  ω   ​  ψ   )   ​   (  p  )    )     \displaystyle=L_{i}(p,(f^{*}\psi)(p),f^{*}(d^{\omega}\psi)(p))    
    
  
  
  
       =    L  i   ​   (  p  ,    τ  ​    (  p  )    −  1     ⋅   ψ  ⁡   (  p  )     ,     τ  ​    (  p  )    −  1     ⋅   d  ω    ​  ψ  ​   (  p  )    )     \displaystyle=L_{i}(p,\tau(p)^{-1}\cdot\psi(p),\tau(p)^{-1}\cdot d^{\omega}\psi(p))    
    
  
  
  
     =   L  i    (  p  ,  ψ   (  p  )   ,   d  ω   ψ   (  p  )   )    (by   G  -invariance)      \displaystyle=L_{i}(p,\psi(p),d^{\omega}\psi(p))\quad\textnormal{(by $G$-invariance)}\quad    
    
  
  
  
        =    ℒ  i   ​   (  ψ  ,  ω  )   ​   (  m  )     .   \displaystyle=\mathscr{L}_{i}(\psi,\omega)(m).    
    
 
  ∎  
 
 
 
 We see that the use of principal connections was forced on physicists if they wanted their theory to be gauge-invariant. Now, it turns out that these principal connections have a physical interpretation: that of gauge potentials for the interactions between particles. They are generalisations of the electromagnetic potential. 
 
 
 Now, we know from the theory of electromagnetism that the fields can exhibit non-trivial behaviour, even in the absence of matter. This means that we have to add a term to the action density that depends only on the gauge potential, if we want to formulate an action principle from which this behaviour can be derived. Let us write this term, called the  self-action density  of the fields, as     ℒ  s   \mathscr{L}_{s}   . This term will be a map      𝒞  ​   (  P  )     ⟶   ℒ  s      C  ∞   ​   (  M  )     \mathscr{C}(P)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathscr{L}_{s}}}{{\longrightarrow}}C^{\infty}(M)   , but we will interpret it as a map        C  ∞   ​    (  P  ,  W  )   G    ×   𝒞  ⁡   (  P  )      ⟶   ℒ  s      C  ∞   ​   (  M  )     C^{\infty}(P,W)^{G}\times\mathscr{C}(P)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathscr{L}_{s}}}{{\longrightarrow}}C^{\infty}(M)   . We will write     ℒ  :=    ℒ  i   +   ℒ  s     \mathscr{L}:=\mathscr{L}_{i}+\mathscr{L}_{s}    for the total action density. 
 
 
 Again,     ℒ  s   \mathscr{L}_{s}    will only depend on the potential and its covariant derivative (i.e. the curvature) at a point. This can be made explicit by demanding that it comes from a  self Lagrangian for the fields        J  1   ​  𝒞  ​   (  P  )     ⟶   L  s    ℝ   J^{1}\mathscr{C}(P)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L_{s}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{R}   , in a similar way as     ℒ  i   \mathscr{L}_{i}    did. (Here,      J  1   ​  𝒞  ​   (  P  )    J^{1}\mathscr{C}(P)    denotes the bundle, over    P  P   , of    1  1   -jets of germs of local principal connection forms; it is not the jet bundle of the global affine space     𝒞  ⁡   (  P  )    \mathscr{C}(P)   .) 
 
 
 We have a natural action of     Gau  ​  P   \mathrm{Gau}\,P    on      J  1   ​  𝒞  ​   (  P  )    J^{1}\mathscr{C}(P)   . Indeed, we take     f  ∈   Gau  ​  P    f\in\mathrm{Gau}\,P    and     τ  =   (  p  ,   ω  ⁡   (  p  )    ,   d  ​  ω  ​   (  p  )    )   ∈    J  1   ​  𝒞  ​   (  P  )     \tau=(p,\omega(p),d\omega(p))\in J^{1}\mathscr{C}(P)    and set      f  ⋅  τ   :=   (    f   −  1    ​   (  p  )    ,    (    f  ∗   ​  ω   )   ​   (    f   −  1    ​   (  p  )    )    ,    d  ⁡   (    f  ∗   ​  ω   )    ​   (    f   −  1    ​   (  p  )    )    )    f\cdot\tau:=(f^{-1}(p),(f^{*}\omega)(f^{-1}(p)),d(f^{*}\omega)(f^{-1}(p)))   . Moreover,    G  G    acts on      J  1   ​  𝒞  ​   (  P  )    J^{1}\mathscr{C}(P)    by       (  p  ,   ω  ⁡   (  p  )    ,   d  ​  ω  ​   (  p  )    )   ⋅  g   :=   (   p  ⋅  g   ,   ω  ⁡   (   p  ⋅  g   )    ,   d  ​  ω  ​   (   p  ⋅  g   )    )    (p,\omega(p),d\omega(p))\cdot g:=(p\cdot g,\omega(p\cdot g),d\omega(p\cdot g))   . 
 
 
 A  self Lagrangian for the fields  is then defined to be a map       J  1   ​  𝒞  ​   (  P  )     ⟶   L  s    ℝ   J^{1}\mathscr{C}(P)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L_{s}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{R}    such that       L  s   ​   (   τ  ⋅  g   )    =    L  s   ​   (  τ  )     L_{s}(\tau\cdot g)=L_{s}(\tau)   , for all     τ  ∈    J  1   ​  𝒞  ​   (  P  )     \tau\in J^{1}\mathscr{C}(P)    and     g  ∈  G   g\in G   . We now define     ℒ  s   \mathscr{L}_{s}    by       ℒ  s   ​   (    π  ~   ​   (  p  )    )    :=    L  s   ​   (   τ  p   )     \mathscr{L}_{s}(\tilde{\pi}(p)):=L_{s}(\tau_{p})   , where      τ  p   =   (  p  ,   ω  ⁡   (  p  )    ,   d  ​  ω  ​   (  p  )    )   ∈    (    J  1   ​  𝒞  ​   (  P  )    )   p    \tau_{p}=(p,\omega(p),d\omega(p))\in(J^{1}\mathscr{C}(P))_{p}   . This is well-defined since the principal right action is transitive on the fibre and       L  s   ​   (   τ  p   )    =    L  s   ​   (    τ  p   ⋅  g   )    =    L  s   ​   (   τ   p  ⋅  g    )     L_{s}(\tau_{p})=L_{s}(\tau_{p}\cdot g)=L_{s}(\tau_{p\cdot g})   . Moreover, we have the following. 
 
 
   Claim 2.6.5   .  
 
     ℒ  s   \mathscr{L}_{s}     is gauge-invariant if and only if     L  s   L_{s}    is.  
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
  The statement follows from comparison of the following two equalities.
       ℒ  s   ​   (  ω  )   ​   (    π  ~   ​   (  p  )    )    =    L  s   ​   (  p  ,   ω  ⁡   (  p  )    ,   d  ​  ω  ​   (  p  )    )     \mathscr{L}_{s}(\omega)(\tilde{\pi}(p))=L_{s}(p,\omega(p),d\omega(p))    and  
 

  
  
      ℒ  s   ​   (    f  ∗   ​  ω   )   ​   (    π  ~   ​   (  p  )    )    \displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{s}(f^{*}\omega)(\tilde{\pi}(p))    
       =    ℒ  s   ​   (    f  ∗   ​  ω   )   ​   (    π  ~   ​   (    f   −  1    ​   (  p  )    )    )     \displaystyle=\mathscr{L}_{s}(f^{*}\omega)(\tilde{\pi}(f^{-1}(p)))    
    
  
  
  
       =    L  s   ​   (    f   −  1    ​   (  p  )    ,    (    f  ∗   ​  ω   )   ​   (    f   −  1    ​   (  p  )    )    ,    d  ⁡   (    f  ∗   ​  ω   )    ​   (    f   −  1    ​   (  p  )    )    )     \displaystyle=L_{s}(f^{-1}(p),(f^{*}\omega)(f^{-1}(p)),d(f^{*}\omega)(f^{-1}(p)))    
    
  
  
  
        =    L  s   ​   (   f  ⋅   (  p  ,   ω  ⁡   (  p  )    ,   d  ​  ω  ​   (  p  )    )    )     ,   \displaystyle=L_{s}(f\cdot(p,\omega(p),d\omega(p))),    
    
 
  for     f  ∈   Gau  ​  P    f\in\mathrm{Gau}\,P   .
∎  
 
 
 
 Note that the curvature      𝒞  ⁡   (  P  )     ⟶  Ω     Ω  hor  2   ​    (  P  ,  𝔤  )   G     \mathscr{C}(P)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Omega}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{2}(P,\mathfrak{g})^{G}    depends locally on the connection in the sense that it is derived from a function      germ  ​  𝒞  ​   (  P  )     ⟶   Ω  ~     germ  ​   Ω  2   ​   (  P  ,  𝔤  )     \mathrm{germ}\,\mathscr{C}(P)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{\Omega}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{germ}\,\Omega^{2}(P,\mathfrak{g})   . This map even factors through a map       J  1   ​  𝒞  ​   (  P  )     ⟶   Ω  ^      J  0   ​   (    Ω  hor  2   ​    (  P  ,  𝔤  )   G    )     J^{1}\mathscr{C}(P)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{\Omega}}}{{\longrightarrow}}J^{0}(\Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{2}(P,\mathfrak{g})^{G})   . (Here,      J  0   ​   Ω  hor  2   ​    (  P  ,  𝔤  )   G    J^{0}\Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{2}(P,\mathfrak{g})^{G}    denotes the zeroth jet bundle of the bundle of germs of sections of      Ω  hor  2   ​    (  P  ,  𝔤  )   G    \Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{2}(P,\mathfrak{g})^{G}   .) Indeed,       Ω  ^   ​   (  τ  )    =    d  ​  ω  ​   (  p  )    +    1  2   ​   [    ω  ⁡   (  p  )    ,   ω  ⁡   (  p  )     ]     =    Ω  ω   ​   (  p  )     \hat{\Omega}(\tau)=d\omega(p)+\frac{1}{2}[\omega(p),\omega(p)]=\Omega^{\omega}(p)   , for     ω  ∈   𝒞  ⁡   (  P  )     \omega\in\mathscr{C}(P)    and     τ  =   (  p  ,   ω  ⁡   (  p  )    ,   d  ​  ω  ​   (  p  )    )    \tau=(p,\omega(p),d\omega(p))   . 
 
 
 This observation is important to understand the following theorem, which is due to Utiyama and gives a characterisation of the gauge-invariant self Lagrangians. 
 
 
   Theorem 2.6.6     (Utiyama’s theorem) .  
 
  A self Lagrangian     L  s   L_{s}   , or equivalently its self-action density     ℒ  s   \mathscr{L}_{s}   , is gauge-invariant if and only if it is of the form      L  s   =   K  ∘   Ω  ^     L_{s}=K\circ\hat{\Omega}    for some    Ad  \mathrm{Ad}   -invariant       J  0   ​   (    Ω  hor  2   ​    (  P  ,  𝔤  )   G    )     ⟶  K   ℝ   J^{0}(\Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{2}(P,\mathfrak{g})^{G})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle K}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{R}   .  
 
 
 
 A simple proof can be found in  [ 6 ] . What does this theorem tell us? It says that a self Lagrangian is gauge-invariant precisely if its value at a point only depends on the value of the curvature at that point and also depends on it in a    Ad  \mathrm{Ad}   -invariant way. Obviously, this result shows that the possibilities for gauge-invariant self Lagrangians are very limited. 
 
 
 
  2.6.4  Actions and the Euler-Lagrange Equations 

 
 We have been talking about action principles for a while, but up to this point we have not been very formal about them. With the machinery of the preceding paragraphs we are in a position to make them mathematically sound. 
 
 
 We will define a function        C  ∞   ​    (  P  ,  W  )   G    ×   𝒞  ⁡   (  P  )      ⟶   S  U    ℝ   C^{\infty}(P,W)^{G}\times\mathscr{C}(P)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S^{U}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{R}    for each open     U  ⊂  M   U\subset M    that has compact closure. These functions will be called the  action functionals . We will show that the action     S  U   S^{U}    is stationary for some      (  ψ  ,  ω  )   ∈     C  ∞   ​    (  P  ,  W  )   G    ×   𝒞  ⁡   (  P  )      (\psi,\omega)\in C^{\infty}(P,W)^{G}\times\mathscr{C}(P)    if and only if     (  ψ  ,  ω  )   (\psi,\omega)    satisfies a set of partial differential equations on       π  ~    −  1    ​   (  U  )    \tilde{\pi}^{-1}(U)   , called the  Euler-Lagrange equations  for this action. 
 
 
 Let us fill in the details. We define       S  U   ​   (  ψ  ,  ω  )    :=    ∫  U     ℒ  ⁡   (  ψ  ,  ω  )    ​  μ     S^{U}(\psi,\omega):=\int_{U}\mathscr{L}(\psi,\omega)\mu   , where    μ  \mu    is the volume form on    M  M    that we obtain from the spacetime metric  26    26 
                 26 
                
                
                
              Note that    μ  \mu    does not need to exist globally. Indeed, not all spacetimes are orientable. However, every spacetime is locally orientable. We can therefore find a neighbourhood    U  U    for each point     m  ∈  M   m\in M   , such that we can choose a Lorentzian volume form on    U  U   . Then we can define the action over    U  U    and derive the Euler-Lagrange equation, which we find not to depend on the choice of orientation. However, if this technicality bothers you, assume that    M  M    has been given a global orientation! (This can be done for many important spacetimes.  [ 32 ] )   . Now, we say that     S  U   S^{U}    is  stationary  at     (  ψ  ,  ω  )   (\psi,\omega)   , if for all      (  σ  ,  τ  )   ∈      C  ∞   ​    (  P  ,  W  )   G    ×   Ω  hor  1    ​    (  P  ,  𝔤  )   G     (\sigma,\tau)\in C^{\infty}(P,W)^{G}\times\Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{1}(P,\mathfrak{g})^{G}    with projected support contained in    U  U   , 
 

  
  
          d   d  ​  t    |    t  =  0    ​   S  U   ​   (   ψ  +   t  ​  σ    ,   ω  +   t  ​  τ    )    =  0   .   \frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}S^{U}(\psi+t\sigma,\omega+t\tau)=0.    
    
 
 We want to show that this is equivalent to     (  ψ  ,  ω  )   (\psi,\omega)    satisfying the Euler-Lagrange equations. 
 
 
 To write down the Euler-Lagrange equation for the particle field, we need some notation. I will use the convention of  [ 6 ] , although I do not think it is standard. Let      (  p  ,  w  ,  θ  )   ∈    J  1   ​   (  P  ,  W  )     (p,w,\theta)\in J^{1}(P,W)    and let     L  i   L_{i}    be an interaction Lagrangian. Note that      w  ′   ↦      d   d  ​  t    |    t  =  0    ​  L  ​   (  p  ,   w  +   t  ​   w  ′     ,  θ  )     w^{\prime}\mapsto\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}L(p,w+tw^{\prime},\theta)    is a linear map     W   ⟶     ℝ   W\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{R}   . By non-degeneracy of the inner product     k  W   k_{W}    on    W  W   , this defines a unique element        ∇  2    L  i    ​   (  p  ,  w  ,  θ  )    ∈  W   \nabla_{2}L_{i}(p,w,\theta)\in W   , such that       k  W   ​   (     ∇  2    L  i    ​   (  p  ,  w  ,  θ  )    ,   w  ′   )    =      d   d  ​  t    |    t  =  0    ​  L  ​   (  p  ,   w  +   t  ​   w  ′     ,  θ  )     k_{W}(\nabla_{2}L_{i}(p,w,\theta),w^{\prime})=\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}L(p,w+tw^{\prime},\theta)   , for all      w  ′   ∈  W   w^{\prime}\in W   . Similarly, note that      θ  ′   ↦      d   d  ​  t    |    t  =  0    ​   L  i   ​   (  p  ,  w  ,   θ  +   t  ​   θ  ′     )     \theta^{\prime}\mapsto\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}L_{i}(p,w,\theta+t\theta^{\prime})    is a linear map       H  ​   P  p  ∗    ⊗  W    ⟶     ℝ   HP_{p}^{*}\otimes W\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{R}   . By non-degeneracy of the inner product      (    h  ¯   ​   k  W    )   p   (\bar{h}k_{W})_{p}    on      H  ​   P  p  ∗    ⊗  W   HP_{p}^{*}\otimes W    (the space of    W  W   -valued    1  1   -forms, vanishing on vertical vectors), we obtain a unique        ∇  3    L  i    ​   (  p  ,  w  ,  θ  )    ∈    H  ​   P  p  ∗    ⊗  W    \nabla_{3}L_{i}(p,w,\theta)\in HP_{p}^{*}\otimes W   , such that     OPEN     h  ¯   ​   k  W   ​   (     ∇  3    L  i    ​   (  p  ,  w  ,  θ  )    ,   θ  ′   )    =      d   d  ​  t    |    t  =  0    ​   L  i   ​   (  p  ,  w  ,   θ  +   t  ​   θ  ′     )     )   \bar{h}k_{W}(\nabla_{3}L_{i}(p,w,\theta),\theta^{\prime})=\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}L_{i}(p,w,\theta+t\theta^{\prime}))   , for all      θ  ′   ∈    H  ​   P  p  ∗    ⊗  W    \theta^{\prime}\in HP_{p}^{*}\otimes W   . Finally, we write         ∂   L  i     ∂  ψ    ​   (  p  )    :=     ∇  2    L  i    ​   (  p  ,   ψ  ⁡   (  p  )    ,    d  ω   ​  ψ  ​   (  p  )    )     \frac{\partial L_{i}}{\partial\psi}(p):=\nabla_{2}L_{i}(p,\psi(p),d^{\omega}\psi(p))    and        ∂   L  i     ∂   (    d  ω   ​  ψ   )     ​   (  p  )    :=     ∇  3    L  i    ​   (  p  ,   ψ  ⁡   (  p  )    ,    d  ω   ​  ψ  ​   (  p  )    )     \frac{\partial L_{i}}{\partial(d^{\omega}\psi)}(p):=\nabla_{3}L_{i}(p,\psi(p),d^{\omega}\psi(p))   . Obviously, these define a function in      C  ∞   ​   (  P  ,  W  )    C^{\infty}(P,W)    and an element of      Ω  1   ​   (  P  ,  W  )    \Omega^{1}(P,W)   , vanishing on vertical vectors, respectively. However, the definitions even turn out to be    G  G   -equivariant. 
 
 
   Claim 2.6.7   .  
 
  With these definitions,       ∂   L  i     ∂  ψ    ∈    C  ∞   ​    (  P  ,  W  )   G    =    Ω  hor  0   ​    (  P  ,  W  )   G     \frac{\partial L_{i}}{\partial\psi}\in C^{\infty}(P,W)^{G}=\Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{0}(P,W)^{G}    and       ∂   L  i     ∂   (    d  ω   ​  ψ   )     ∈    Ω  hor  1   ​    (  P  ,  W  )   G     \frac{\partial L_{i}}{\partial(d^{\omega}\psi)}\in\Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{1}(P,W)^{G}   .  
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
  We verify equivariance of      ∂   L  i     ∂  ψ    \frac{\partial L_{i}}{\partial\psi}   . The computation for      ∂   L  i     ∂   (    d  ω   ​  ψ   )     \frac{\partial L_{i}}{\partial(d^{\omega}\psi)}    is almost identical and it is left to the reader.  
 
 
  Let      w  ′   ∈  W   w^{\prime}\in W   . Then  
 

  
  
      k  W   ​   (      ∂   L  i     ∂  ψ     ​   (   p  ⋅  g   )    ,   w  ′   )    \displaystyle k_{W}(\frac{\partial L_{i}}{\partial\psi}(p\cdot g),w^{\prime})    
       =       d   d  ​  t     |    t  =  0    ​   L  i   ​   (   p  ⋅  g   ,    ψ  ⁡   (   p  ⋅  g   )    +   t  ​   w  ′     ,    d  ω   ​  ψ  ​   (   p  ⋅  g   )    )     \displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}L_{i}(p\cdot g,\psi(p\cdot g)+tw^{\prime},d^{\omega}\psi(p\cdot g))    
    
  
  
  
       =       d   d  ​  t     |    t  =  0    ​   L  i   ​   (   p  ⋅  g   ,     g   −  1    ⋅   ψ  ⁡   (  p  )     +   t  ​   w  ′     ,       g   −  1    ⋅   d  ω    ​  ψ  ​   (  p  )    ∘  T   ​   ρ   g   −  1      )     \displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}L_{i}(p\cdot g,g^{-1}\cdot\psi(p)+tw^{\prime},g^{-1}\cdot d^{\omega}\psi(p)\circ T\rho_{g^{-1}})    
    
  
  
  
     =    d   d  ​  t      |   t  =  0     L  i    (  p  ,  ψ   (  p  )   +  t  g  ⋅   w  ′   ,   d  ω   ψ   (  p  )   )    (by definition of    L  i   )      \displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}L_{i}(p,\psi(p)+tg\cdot w^{\prime},d^{\omega}\psi(p))\quad\textnormal{(by definition of $L_{i}$)}\quad    
    
  
  
  
       =    k  W   ​   (      ∂   L  i     ∂  ψ     ​   (  p  )    ,   g  ⋅   w  ′    )     \displaystyle=k_{W}(\frac{\partial L_{i}}{\partial\psi}(p),g\cdot w^{\prime})    
    
  
  
  
         =    k  W   ​   (     g   −  1    ⋅     ∂   L  i     ∂  ψ      ​   (  p  )    ,   w  ′   )     .     (since    k  W    is an invariant inner product)       \displaystyle=k_{W}(g^{-1}\cdot\frac{\partial L_{i}}{\partial\psi}(p),w^{\prime}).\quad\textnormal{(since $k_{W}$ is an invariant inner product)}\quad    
    
 
  Non-degeneracy of     k  W   k_{W}    does the rest.
∎  
 
 
 
 With this notation, the Euler-Lagrange equation for the particle field takes the following simple form. 
 
 
   Theorem 2.6.8     (Euler-Lagrange equation for the particle field) .  
 
  The action is stationary with respect to the particle field,         d   d  ​  t    |    t  =  0    ​   S  U   ​   (   ψ  +   t  ​  σ    ,  ω  )    =  0   \frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}S^{U}(\psi+t\sigma,\omega)=0   , for all open     U  ⊂  M   U\subset M    with compact closure and     σ  ∈    C  ∞   ​    (  P  ,  W  )   G     \sigma\in C^{\infty}(P,W)^{G}    with projected support in    U  U   , if and only if    ψ  \psi    satisfies the Euler-Lagrange equation  
 

  
  
         δ  ω   ​   (    ∂   L  i     ∂   (    d  ω   ​  ψ   )     )    +    ∂   L  i     ∂  ψ     =  0   .   \delta^{\omega}\left(\frac{\partial L_{i}}{\partial(d^{\omega}\psi)}\right)+\frac{\partial L_{i}}{\partial\psi}=0.    
    
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
  We compute  
 

  
  
       ∫  U         d   d  ​  t     |    t  =  0    ​   ℒ  i   ​   (   ψ  +   t  ​  σ    ,  ω  )   ​   (    π  ~   ​   (  p  )    )   ​  μ    \displaystyle\int_{U}\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}\mathscr{L}_{i}(\psi+t\sigma,\omega)(\tilde{\pi}(p))\mu    
       =     ∫  U         d   d  ​  t     |    t  =  0    ​   L  i   ​   (  p  ,    ψ  ⁡   (  p  )    +   t  ​  σ  ​   (  p  )     ,     d  ω   ​  ψ  ​   (  p  )    +   t  ​   d  ω   ​  σ  ​   (  p  )     )   ​  μ     \displaystyle=\int_{U}\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}L_{i}(p,\psi(p)+t\sigma(p),d^{\omega}\psi(p)+td^{\omega}\sigma(p))\mu    
    
  
  
  
       =     ∫  U         d   d  ​  t     |    t  =  0    ​   L  i   ​   (  p  ,    ψ  ⁡   (  p  )    +   t  ​  σ  ​   (  p  )     ,    d  ω   ​  ψ  ​   (  p  )    )   ​  μ     \displaystyle=\int_{U}\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}L_{i}(p,\psi(p)+t\sigma(p),d^{\omega}\psi(p))\mu    
    
  
  
  
     +    ∫  U      d   d  ​  t      |   t  =  0     L  i    (  p  ,  ψ   (  p  )   ,   d  ω   ψ   (  p  )   +  t   d  ω   σ   (  p  )   )   μ   \displaystyle+\int_{U}\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}L_{i}(p,\psi(p),d^{\omega}\psi(p)+td^{\omega}\sigma(p))\mu    
    
  
  
  
       =      ∫  U      k  W   ​   (      ∂   L  i     ∂  ψ     ​   (  p  )    ,   σ  ⁡   (  p  )    )   ​  μ    +     ∫  U      h  ¯   ​   k  W   ​   (      ∂   L  i     ∂   (    d  ω   ​  ψ   )      ​   (  p  )    ,    d  ω   ​  σ  ​   (  p  )    )   ​  μ      \displaystyle=\int_{U}k_{W}(\frac{\partial L_{i}}{\partial\psi}(p),\sigma(p))\mu+\int_{U}\bar{h}k_{W}(\frac{\partial L_{i}}{\partial(d^{\omega}\psi)}(p),d^{\omega}\sigma(p))\mu    
    
  
  
  
       =      ∫  U      k  W   ​   (      ∂   L  i     ∂  ψ     ​   (  p  )    ,   σ  ⁡   (  p  )    )   ​  μ    +     ∫  U      h  ¯   ​   k  W   ​   (    δ  ω   ​     ∂   L  i     ∂   (    d  ω   ​  ψ   )      ​   (  p  )    ,   σ  ⁡   (  p  )    )   ​  μ      \displaystyle=\int_{U}k_{W}(\frac{\partial L_{i}}{\partial\psi}(p),\sigma(p))\mu+\int_{U}\bar{h}k_{W}(\delta^{\omega}\frac{\partial L_{i}}{\partial(d^{\omega}\psi)}(p),\sigma(p))\mu    
    
  
  
  
        =     ∫  U      k  W   ​   (     δ  ω   ​     ∂   L  i     ∂   (    d  ω   ​  ψ   )      ​   (  p  )    +      ∂   L  i     ∂  ψ     ​   (  p  )     ,   σ  ⁡   (  p  )    )   ​  μ     ,   \displaystyle=\int_{U}k_{W}(\delta^{\omega}\frac{\partial L_{i}}{\partial(d^{\omega}\psi)}(p)+\frac{\partial L_{i}}{\partial\psi}(p),\sigma(p))\mu,    
    
 
  where, in the last equality we use that       h  ¯   ​   k  W    =   k  W    \bar{h}k_{W}=k_{W}    on    0  0   -forms and in the equality before we use claim   1.3.10  . Thus, we find that         d   d  ​  t    |    t  =  0    ​   S  U   ​   (   ψ  +   t  ​  σ    ,  ω  )    =  0   \frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}S^{U}(\psi+t\sigma,\omega)=0    iff       ∫  U     k  W   ​   (     δ  ω   ​    ∂   L  i     ∂   (    d  ω   ​  ψ   )     ​   (  p  )    +     ∂   L  i     ∂  ψ    ​   (  p  )     ,   σ  ⁡   (  p  )    )   ​  μ    =  0   \int_{U}k_{W}(\delta^{\omega}\frac{\partial L_{i}}{\partial(d^{\omega}\psi)}(p)+\frac{\partial L_{i}}{\partial\psi}(p),\sigma(p))\mu=0   . Now, the demand that this holds for all open     U  ⊂  M   U\subset M    with compact closure is equivalent to the demand that the integrand be zero (since the integrand is continuous). The non-degeneracy of     k  W   k_{W}    does the rest.
∎  
 
 
 
 We want to demand that the action be stationary with respect to the fields as well and derive a similar equation. To do this, we have to introduce the concept of a  current , which will play an analogous role to that of the electric current in electromagnetism. Note that my treatment, and therefore the terminology as well, is non-standard. 
 
 
 Let us note that       T  ω   ​  𝒞  ​   (  P  )    =    Ω  hor  1   ​    (  P  ,  𝔤  )   G     T_{\omega}\mathscr{C}(P)=\Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{1}(P,\mathfrak{g})^{G}   . The map       T  ω   ​  𝒞  ​   (  P  )     ⟶       C  ∞   ​   (  M  )     T_{\omega}\mathscr{C}(P)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}C^{\infty}(M)    given by     τ  ↦      d   d  ​  t    |    t  =  0    ​   ℒ  i   ​   (  ψ  ,   ω  +   t  ​  τ    )     \tau\mapsto\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}\mathscr{L}_{i}(\psi,\omega+t\tau)    is linear in    τ  \tau   . This means that, pointwise and by non-degeneracy of      h  ¯   ​   k  𝔤    \bar{h}k_{\mathfrak{g}}   , we have a unique      J  ⁡   (  ψ  ,  ω  )    ∈    Ω  hor  1   ​    (  P  ,  𝔤  )   G     J(\psi,\omega)\in\Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{1}(P,\mathfrak{g})^{G}    such that         d   d  ​  t    |    t  =  0    ​   ℒ  i   ​   (  ψ  ,   ω  +   t  ​  τ    )    =    h  ¯   ​   k  𝔤   ​   (   J  ⁡   (  ψ  ,  ω  )    ,  τ  )     \frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}\mathscr{L}_{i}(\psi,\omega+t\tau)=\bar{h}k_{\mathfrak{g}}(J(\psi,\omega),\tau)    for all     τ  ∈    T  ω   ​  𝒞  ​   (  P  )     \tau\in T_{\omega}\mathscr{C}(P)   . We shall call this     J  ⁡   (  ψ  ,  ω  )    J(\psi,\omega)    the  interaction current   27    27 
                 27 
                
                
                
              This quantity is usually called simply  the  current in the literature.   . Since     ℒ  s   \mathscr{L}_{s}    may depend on the first jet of    ω  \omega   , its variation need not be pointwise algebraic in    τ  \tau   . We therefore define (following the terminology in  [ 1 ] ) a  self current  of the gauge field as the unique      𝒥  ⁡   (  ω  )    ∈    Ω  hor  1   ​    (  P  ,  𝔤  )   G     \mathscr{J}(\omega)\in\Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{1}(P,\mathfrak{g})^{G}    such that 
 

  
  
      −   d   d  ​  t     |      ∫  U       t  =  0      ⁡   ℒ  s    ​   (   ω  +   t  ​  τ    )   ​  μ   =    ∫  U     h  ¯   ​   k  𝔤   ​   (   𝒥  ⁡   (  ω  )    ,  τ  )   ​  μ      -\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}\int_{U}\mathscr{L}_{s}(\omega+t\tau)\mu=\int_{U}\bar{h}k_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathscr{J}(\omega),\tau)\mu    
    
 
 for all open     U  ⊂  M   U\subset M    with compact closure and all     τ  ∈    T  ω   ​  𝒞  ​   (  P  )     \tau\in T_{\omega}\mathscr{C}(P)    with projected support in    U  U   , whenever such a form exists. These definitions enable us to formulate the second Euler-Lagrange equation. 
 
 
   Theorem 2.6.9     (Euler-Lagrange equation for the gauge field) .  
 
  The action is stationary at     (  ψ  ,  ω  )   (\psi,\omega)    with respect to the gauge field,         d   d  ​  t    |    t  =  0    ​   S  U   ​   (  ψ  ,   ω  +   t  ​  τ    )    =  0   \frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}S^{U}(\psi,\omega+t\tau)=0   , for all open     U  ⊂  M   U\subset M    with compact closure and     τ  ∈    T  ω   ​  𝒞  ​   (  P  )     \tau\in T_{\omega}\mathscr{C}(P)    with projected support in    U  U   , if and only if    ω  \omega    satisfies the Euler-Lagrange equation  
 

  
  
       𝒥  ⁡   (  ω  )    =   J  ⁡   (  ψ  ,  ω  )     .   \mathscr{J}(\omega)=J(\psi,\omega).    
    
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
  Using the definitions of the interaction and self currents, we compute  
 

  
  
         d   d  ​  t     |    t  =  0    ​   S  U   ​   (  ψ  ,   ω  +   t  ​  τ    )    \displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}S^{U}(\psi,\omega+t\tau)    
        =        d   d  ​  t     |    t  =  0    ​     ∫  U      ℒ  i   ​   (  ψ  ,   ω  +   t  ​  τ    )   ​  μ     +    d   d  ​  t       |      ∫  U       t  =  0       ⁡   ℒ  s    ​   (   ω  +   t  ​  τ    )   ​  μ    \displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}\int_{U}\mathscr{L}_{i}(\psi,\omega+t\tau)\mu+\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}\int_{U}\mathscr{L}_{s}(\omega+t\tau)\mu    
    
  
  
  
        =     ∫  U      h  ¯   ​   k  𝔤   ​   (    J  ⁡   (  ψ  ,  ω  )    −   𝒥  ⁡   (  ω  )     ,  τ  )   ​  μ     .   \displaystyle=\int_{U}\bar{h}k_{\mathfrak{g}}(J(\psi,\omega)-\mathscr{J}(\omega),\tau)\mu.    
    
 
  Again, by continuity of the integrand, we see that      h  ¯   ​   k  𝔤   ​   (    J  ⁡   (  ψ  ,  ω  )    −   𝒥  ⁡   (  ω  )     ,  τ  )    \bar{h}k_{\mathfrak{g}}(J(\psi,\omega)-\mathscr{J}(\omega),\tau)    is zero if and only if         d   d  ​  t    |    t  =  0    ​   S  U   ​   (  ψ  ,   ω  +   t  ​  τ    )    =  0   \frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}S^{U}(\psi,\omega+t\tau)=0    for all    U  U   . Now, the non-degeneracy of      h  ¯   ​   k  𝔤    \bar{h}k_{\mathfrak{g}}    tells us that this holds for all    τ  \tau    precisely if       J  ⁡   (  ψ  ,  ω  )    −   𝒥  ⁡   (  ω  )     =  0   J(\psi,\omega)-\mathscr{J}(\omega)=0   .
∎  
 
 
 
 One may wonder why these quantities are called currents. For one thing, as we shall see, in the case of electromagnetism this equation reduces to the second (inhomogeneous) Maxwell equation and     J  ⁡   (  ψ  ,  ω  )    J(\psi,\omega)    will equal the electric current. Another reason for this nomenclature may be the following variant of Noether’s theorem  28    28 
                 28 
                
                
                
              Actually, Noether’s theorem can be proved in a much broader context. Indeed, we obtain a similar conserved quantity if the Lagrangian is invariant under an arbitrary    G  G   -action. See, for example,  [ 17 ]    . 
 
 
   Theorem 2.6.10     (Noether’s theorem) .  
 
  Let     L  i   L_{i}    be a    G  G   -invariant interaction Lagrangian and suppose that     (  ψ  ,  ω  )   (\psi,\omega)    satisfies the Euler-Lagrange equation for the particle field, then       δ  ω   ​   (   J  ⁡   (  ψ  ,  ω  )    )    =  0   \delta^{\omega}(J(\psi,\omega))=0   .  
 
 
 
 This is a kind of continuity equation for     J  ⁡   (  ψ  ,  ω  )    J(\psi,\omega)   . Apparently,     J  ⁡   (  ψ  ,  ω  )    J(\psi,\omega)    represents the current density of some quantity that is locally conserved in spacetime. The proof of this theorem requires some more definitions. Therefore, the interested reader is referred to  [ 6 ] . However, as we shall see, if we have a special form of the self-action, called the Yang-Mills action, we immediately obtain that       δ  ω   ​  𝒥  ​   (  ω  )    =  0   \delta^{\omega}\mathscr{J}(\omega)=0   , even if the interaction Lagrangian is not    G  G   -invariant. Application of the Euler-Lagrange equation for the gauge field again gives us conservation of the interaction current. We see that we obtain an interpretation of     J  ⁡   (  ψ  ,  ω  )    J(\psi,\omega)    as a current if either the interaction Lagrangian or the self Lagrangian has a particularly simple form.
 
 Summarising, we have found that      S  U   ​   (  ψ  ,  ω  )    S^{U}(\psi,\omega)    is stationary with respect to all compactly supported variations in     (  ψ  ,  ω  )   (\psi,\omega)    if and only if    ψ  \psi    and    ω  \omega    satisfy both Euler-Lagrange equations: 
 

  
  
          δ  ω   ​   (    ∂   L  i     ∂   (    d  ω   ​  ψ   )     )    +    ∂   L  i     ∂  ψ     =  0     and      𝒥  ⁡   (  ω  )    =   J  ⁡   (  ψ  ,  ω  )      .   \delta^{\omega}\left(\frac{\partial L_{i}}{\partial(d^{\omega}\psi)}\right)+\frac{\partial L_{i}}{\partial\psi}=0\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\mathscr{J}(\omega)=J(\psi,\omega).    
    
 
 (Specific cases of these two equations will be the Dirac equation and the second (inhomogeneous) Maxwell equation, respectively.) As in the case of Maxwell’s electromagnetism, we can hope that these two equations, together with the integrability condition provided by the Bianchi identity, 
 

  
  
        d  ω   ​   Ω  ω    =  0   ,   d^{\omega}\Omega^{\omega}=0,    
    
 
 will uniquely determine the evolution of the fields. (Of course, the first Maxwell equation is a specific example of this Bianchi identity.) 
 
 
 
  2.6.5  The Yang-Mills Action 

 
 We have seen that we can interpret Maxwell’s electromagnetism as a     U  ⁡   (  1  )    U(1)   -gauge theory. From this point of view, the electromagnetic tensor acquired the interpretation of (a multiple of) the curvature     Ω  ω   \Omega^{\omega}    of some connection    ω  \omega    (    F  =   i  ​  e  ​   e  α  ∗   ​   Ω  ω     F=ie\,e_{\alpha}^{*}\Omega^{\omega}    in a local gauge). We have seen that the Maxwell equations can be derived from an action density     −   1  2   tr      1  ,  3    tr      2  ,  4    F  ⊗  F   -\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}^{1,3}\mathrm{tr}^{2,4}F\otimes F   , which is proportional to     −    1  2   ​   h  ¯   ​   k   𝔲  ⁡   (  1  )     ​   (   Ω  ω   ,   Ω  ω   )     -\frac{1}{2}\bar{h}k_{\mathfrak{u}(1)}(\Omega^{\omega},\Omega^{\omega})    under the normalisation     F  =   i  ​  e  ​   e  α  ∗   ​   Ω  ω     F=ie\,e_{\alpha}^{*}\Omega^{\omega}   . This turns out to be a very general form of the self-action density for the gauge field. Indeed, it can be used to describe the weak, the unified electroweak, and the strong interactions as well  29    29 
                 29 
                
                
                
              In the case of the (electro)weak interaction, this requires some alterations, such as a Higgs mechanism (which can also be understood in this formalism for gauge theory).  [ 6 ]    , albeit on a different principal bundle. For instance, one uses an     S  ​  U  ​   (  2  )    SU(2)   -bundle for the weak interaction, a       U  ⁡   (  1  )    ×  S   ​  U  ​   (  2  )    U(1)\times SU(2)   -bundle for the electroweak interaction, and an     S  ​  U  ​   (  3  )    SU(3)   -bundle for the strong one. We therefore write out the Euler-Lagrange equation for the gauge field for this specific form of the self-action density. This leads to the famous  Yang-Mills equation . 
 
 
 For this derivation, we need the following result. 
 
 
   Claim 2.6.11   .  
 
  If     τ  ∈    Ω  hor  1   ​    (  P  ,  𝔤  )   G     \tau\in\Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{1}(P,\mathfrak{g})^{G}   , then       d  ω   ​  τ   =    d  ​  τ   +   [   ω  ,  τ   ]     d^{\omega}\tau=d\tau+[\omega,\tau]   .  
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
  Let      X  ,  Y   ∈   𝒳  ⁡   (  P  )     X,Y\in\mathcal{X}(P)   . We should verify that     (  ∗  )   (*)   :
       d  ω   ​  τ  ​   (  X  ,  Y  )    =   d  ​  τ  ​   (    P  H   ​  X   ,    P  H   ​  Y   )    =    d  ​  τ  ​   (  X  ,  Y  )    +    [   ω  ,  τ   ]   ​   (  X  ,  Y  )     =     d  ​  τ  ​   (  X  ,  Y  )    +   [    ω  ⁡   (  X  )    ,   τ  ⁡   (  Y  )     ]    −   [   ω  ⁡   (  Y  )    ,   τ  ⁡   (  X  )    ]     d^{\omega}\tau(X,Y)=d\tau(P_{H}X,P_{H}Y)=d\tau(X,Y)+[\omega,\tau](X,Y)=d\tau(X,Y)+[\omega(X),\tau(Y)]-[\omega(Y),\tau(X)]   . We use bilinearity of both sides to reduce the equality to the case in which    X  X    is either horizontal or vertical and the same for    Y  Y   .  
 
 
  If    X  X    and    Y  Y    are both horizontal, then the equality obviously holds, since both sides are equal to     d  ​  τ  ​   (  X  ,  Y  )    d\tau(X,Y)   .  
 
 
  Moreover, if both    X  X    and    Y  Y    are vertical,     (  ∗  )   (*)    becomes the condition that      d  ​  τ  ​   (  X  ,  Y  )    =  0   d\tau(X,Y)=0   . Now,      d  ​  τ  ​   (  X  ,  Y  )    =     ℒ  X   ​  τ  ​   (  Y  )    −    ℒ  Y   ​  τ  ​   (  X  )    −   τ  ⁡   (   [   X  ,  Y   ]   )     =   −   τ  ⁡   (   [   X  ,  Y   ]   )      d\tau(X,Y)=\mathcal{L}_{X}\tau(Y)-\mathcal{L}_{Y}\tau(X)-\tau([X,Y])=-\tau([X,Y])   . To see that this equals zero, note that the vertical bundle is integrable (claim   1.2.3  ), so     [  X  ,  Y  ]   [X,Y]    is a vertical vector field.  
 
 
  Finally, if    X  X    is vertical and    Y  Y    is horizontal, we should verify that     0  =    d  ​  τ  ​   (  X  ,  Y  )    +   [    ω  ⁡   (  X  )    ,   τ  ⁡   (  Y  )     ]     0=d\tau(X,Y)+[\omega(X),\tau(Y)]   . Now,      d  ​  τ  ​   (  X  ,  Y  )    =     ℒ  X   ​  τ  ​   (  Y  )    −    ℒ  Y   ​  τ  ​   (  X  )    −   τ  ⁡   (   [   X  ,  Y   ]   )     =     ℒ  X   ​  τ  ​   (  Y  )    −   τ  ⁡   (   [   X  ,  Y   ]   )      d\tau(X,Y)=\mathcal{L}_{X}\tau(Y)-\mathcal{L}_{Y}\tau(X)-\tau([X,Y])=\mathcal{L}_{X}\tau(Y)-\tau([X,Y])   , so     (  ∗  )   (*)    reduces to        ℒ  X   ​  τ  ​   (  Y  )    −   τ  ⁡   (   [   X  ,  Y   ]   )     =   −   [   ω  ⁡   (  X  )    ,   τ  ⁡   (  Y  )    ]     \mathcal{L}_{X}\tau(Y)-\tau([X,Y])=-[\omega(X),\tau(Y)]   . We verify this equality pointwise. Since both sides are tensorial, it is enough at    p  p    to take     X  =   Z  P    X=Z_{P}   , where     Z  ∈  𝔤   Z\in\mathfrak{g}    is chosen so that       Z  P   ​   (  p  )    =   X  ​   (  p  )     Z_{P}(p)=X(p)   . Then  
 

  
  
      ℒ  X   ​   (   τ  ​   (  Y  )    )   ​   (  p  )    \displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{X}(\tau(Y))(p)    
       =       d   d  ​  t     |    t  =  0    ​   τ    ρ    exp  ⁡  t   ​  Z    ​   (  p  )     ​   (   Y  ⁡   (    ρ    exp  ⁡  t   ​  Z    ​   (  p  )    )    )     \displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}\tau_{\rho_{\exp tZ}(p)}\bigl(Y(\rho_{\exp tZ}(p))\bigr)    
    
  
  
  
     =    d   d  ​  t      |   t  =  0     (   Ad   exp  ⁡   (   −   t  ​  Z    )     ∘   τ  p   )    (  T   ρ   exp  −   t  ​  Z     ∘  Y  ∘   ρ    exp  ⁡  t   ​  Z     (  p  )   )    (by equivariance of   τ  )      \displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}\left(\mathrm{Ad}_{\exp(-tZ)}\circ\tau_{p}\right)\left(T\rho_{\exp-tZ}\circ Y\circ\rho_{\exp tZ}(p)\right)\quad\textnormal{(by equivariance of $\tau$)}\quad    
    
  
  
  
       =     (        d   d  ​  t     |    t  =  0    ​   Ad   exp  ⁡   (   −   t  ​  Z    )      ∘   τ  p    )   ​   (   Y  ⁡   (  p  )    )    +    τ  p   ​   (        d   d  ​  t     |    t  =  0    ​  T  ​   ρ   exp  −   t  ​  Z      ∘  Y  ∘    ρ    exp  ⁡  t   ​  Z    ​   (  p  )     )      \displaystyle=\left(\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}\mathrm{Ad}_{\exp(-tZ)}\circ\tau_{p}\right)(Y(p))+\tau_{p}\left(\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}T\rho_{\exp-tZ}\circ Y\circ\rho_{\exp tZ}(p)\right)    
    
  
  
  
       =    −    ad  ⁡   (  Z  )    ​   (   τ  ⁡   (  Y  )    )   ​   (  p  )     +    τ  ⁡   (   [   X  ,  Y   ]   )    ​   (  p  )      \displaystyle=-\mathrm{ad}(Z)(\tau(Y))(p)+\tau([X,Y])(p)    
    
  
  
  
       =    −   [   Z  ,    τ  ⁡   (  Y  )    ​   (  p  )     ]    +    τ  ⁡   (   [   X  ,  Y   ]   )    ​   (  p  )      \displaystyle=-[Z,\tau(Y)(p)]+\tau([X,Y])(p)    
    
  
  
  
     =  −   [  ω   (  X  )    (  p  )   ,  τ   (  Y  )    (  p  )   ]   +  τ   (   [  X  ,  Y  ]   )    (  p  )    (by claim     1.3.2    )     .   \displaystyle=-[\omega(X)(p),\tau(Y)(p)]+\tau([X,Y])(p)\quad\textnormal{(by claim \ref{claim:repro})}\quad.    
    
 
  ∎  
 
 
 
 The Yang-Mills equation now easily follows. 
 
 
   Theorem 2.6.12     (The Yang-Mills equation) .  
 
  For the  Yang-Mills self-action density        ℒ  s   ​   (  ω  )    =   −    1  2   ​   h  ¯   ​   k  𝔤   ​   (   Ω  ω   ,   Ω  ω   )      \mathscr{L}_{s}(\omega)=-\frac{1}{2}\bar{h}k_{\mathfrak{g}}(\Omega^{\omega},\Omega^{\omega})   , the Euler-Lagrange equation for the gauge field takes the form  
 

  
  
        δ  ω   ​   Ω  ω    =   J  ⁡   (  ψ  ,  ω  )     .   \delta^{\omega}\Omega^{\omega}=J(\psi,\omega).    
    
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
  We assume that     U  ⊂  M   U\subset M    is open with compact closure and     τ  ∈    Ω  hor  1   ​    (  P  ,  𝔤  )   G     \tau\in\Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}^{1}(P,\mathfrak{g})^{G}    with projected support in    U  U   .  
 
 
  By theorem   1.3.6  ,      Ω   ω  +   t  ​  τ     =    d  ⁡   (   ω  +   t  ​  τ    )    +    1  2   ​   [    ω  +   t  ​  τ    ,   ω  +   t  ​  τ     ]     =    d  ​  ω   +    1  2   ​   [   ω  ,  ω   ]    +   t  ⁡   (    d  ​  τ   +    1  2   ​   [   τ  ,  ω   ]    +    1  2   ​   [   ω  ,  τ   ]     )    +   𝒪  ⁡   (   t  2   )     =    d  ​  ω   +    1  2   ​   [   ω  ,  ω   ]    +   t  ​   d  ω   ​  τ   +   𝒪  ⁡   (   t  2   )      \Omega^{\omega+t\tau}=d(\omega+t\tau)+\frac{1}{2}[\omega+t\tau,\omega+t\tau]=d\omega+\frac{1}{2}[\omega,\omega]+t(d\tau+\frac{1}{2}[\tau,\omega]+\frac{1}{2}[\omega,\tau])+\mathcal{O}(t^{2})=d\omega+\frac{1}{2}[\omega,\omega]+td^{\omega}\tau+\mathcal{O}(t^{2})   , where the last equality holds by claim   2.6.11  . We see that         d   d  ​  t    |    t  =  0    ​   Ω   ω  +   t  ​  τ      =    d  ω   ​  τ    \frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}\Omega^{\omega+t\tau}=d^{\omega}\tau   . Therefore      ∫  U    h  ¯    k  𝔤    (  𝒥   (  ω  )   ,  τ  )   μ  =  −   ∫  U    d   d  ​  t     |   t  =  0     ℒ  s    (  ω  +  t  τ  )   μ  =   ∫  U    d   d  ​  t     |   t  =  0     1  2    h  ¯    k  𝔤    (   Ω   ω  +   t  ​  τ     ,   Ω   ω  +   t  ​  τ     )   μ  =   ∫  U    h  ¯    k  𝔤    (   Ω  ω   ,   d  ω   τ  )   μ  =   ∫  U    h  ¯    k  𝔤    (   δ  ω    Ω  ω   ,  τ  )   μ   \int_{U}\bar{h}k_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathscr{J}(\omega),\tau)\mu=-\int_{U}\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}\mathscr{L}_{s}(\omega+t\tau)\mu=\int_{U}\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}\frac{1}{2}\bar{h}k_{\mathfrak{g}}(\Omega^{\omega+t\tau},\Omega^{\omega+t\tau})\mu=\int_{U}\bar{h}k_{\mathfrak{g}}(\Omega^{\omega},d^{\omega}\tau)\mu=\int_{U}\bar{h}k_{\mathfrak{g}}(\delta^{\omega}\Omega^{\omega},\tau)\mu   , where the last equality holds by claim   1.3.10  . Finally, the continuity of the integrand and non-degeneracy of      h  ¯   ​   k  𝔤    \bar{h}k_{\mathfrak{g}}    allow us to conclude that      𝒥  ⁡   (  ω  )    =    δ  ω   ​   Ω  ω     \mathscr{J}(\omega)=\delta^{\omega}\Omega^{\omega}   .
∎  
 
 
 
 We see that this equation reduces to the second (inhomogeneous) Maxwell equation in the case     G  =   U  ⁡   (  1  )     G=U(1)   . It is well known that electric charge conservation can be derived from this Maxwell equation. We obtain a similar result for the Yang-Mills equation. 
 
 
   Corollary 2.6.13     (Generalised charge conservation) .  
 
  If     (  ψ  ,  ω  )   (\psi,\omega)    satisfies the Yang-Mills equation, we have  
 

  
  
        δ  ω   ​  J  ​   (  ψ  ,  ω  )    =  0   .   \delta^{\omega}J(\psi,\omega)=0.    
    
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
  This is immediate:       δ  ω   ​  J  ​   (  ψ  ,  ω  )    =    δ  ω   ​   (    δ  ω   ​   (   Ω  ω   )    )    =   ±    ∗  ¯   ​   d  ω   ​   ∗  ¯   ​   ∗  ¯   ​   d  ω   ​   ∗  ¯   ​   Ω  ω     =   ±    ∗  ¯   ​   d  ω   ​   d  ω   ​   ∗  ¯   ​   Ω  ω     =   ±    ∗  ¯   ​   [    Ω  ω   ,    ∗  ¯   ​   Ω  ω     ]     =  0   \delta^{\omega}J(\psi,\omega)=\delta^{\omega}(\delta^{\omega}(\Omega^{\omega}))=\pm\bar{*}d^{\omega}\bar{*}\bar{*}d^{\omega}\bar{*}\Omega^{\omega}=\pm\bar{*}d^{\omega}d^{\omega}\bar{*}\Omega^{\omega}=\pm\bar{*}[\Omega^{\omega},\bar{*}\Omega^{\omega}]=0   . For the last equality, write      Ω  ω   =    ∑  a     E  a   ​   Ω  a      \Omega^{\omega}=\sum_{a}E_{a}\Omega^{a}    in a basis of    𝔤  \mathfrak{g}   ; then      Ω  a   ∧    ∗  ¯   ​   Ω  b     \Omega^{a}\wedge\bar{*}\Omega^{b}    is symmetric in     a  ,  b   a,b   , while     [   E  a   ,   E  b   ]   [E_{a},E_{b}]    is antisymmetric.
∎  
 
 
 
   Remark 2.6.14   .  
 
 The logical continuation of this thesis would be the case of a specific interaction Lagrangian, called the  Dirac interaction Lagrangian , that can be used to describe matter fields like electrons, nucleons (protons and neutrons), quarks and many other particles, which then obey the  Dirac equation . Its importance in physics can be compared to that of the Yang-Mills self Lagrangian for the gauge fields. However, there is no room for this here. The reader can find a treatment for the case of the electron and the nucleons with electroweak interaction in  [ 6 ] . Unfortunately, this work does not feature a more general treatment involving general spinor fields obeying a Dirac equation. In particular, an account of the strong interactions between quarks is missing. However, I have yet to find a better text on the subject. In particular, most other texts, even when they feature a theory of the strong interaction, do not take a geometric approach. Usually they are full of phenomenology and computations, proceeding immediately to the mathematically problematic procedure of second quantisation. An introduction to second quantisation can be found in  [ 12 ] . 
 
 
 
 
  2.6.6  The Einstein-Yang-Mills Self-Action Density 

 
 The bundle metric    h  h    can be seen to be of even more physical importance than we have seen so far by computing its curvature scalar. This lengthy (but straightforward) computation, which I shall omit (see  [ 6 ] ), gives the following theorem. 
 
 
   Theorem 2.6.15   .  
 
  Let us write     𝒮  h   \mathcal{S}_{h}    for the curvature scalar of    h  h    (which, by remark   2.5.1  , is constant along fibres of     π  ~   \tilde{\pi}    and therefore descends to a function on    M  M   ),     𝒮  g   \mathcal{S}_{g}    for that of    g  g    and     𝒮  k   \mathcal{S}_{k}    for that of    k  k    (which is constant, since, by invariance of    k  k   ,    G  G    acts transitively on itself by    k  k   -isometries by left multiplication). Then, we have an equality of smooth functions on    M  M   :  
 

  
  
       𝒮  h   =     𝒮  g   −    1  2   ​   (   g  ​   k  𝔤    )   ​   (   Ω  ω   ,   Ω  ω   )     +   𝒮  k     .   \mathcal{S}_{h}=\mathcal{S}_{g}-\frac{1}{2}(gk_{\mathfrak{g}})(\Omega^{\omega},\Omega^{\omega})+\mathcal{S}_{k}.    
    
 
 
 
 
 We see that this action density involves contributions from both a Yang-Mills action density and the Einstein-Hilbert action density. Up to the constant term     𝒮  k   \mathcal{S}_{k}    (which is zero for Abelian one-dimensional fibres, and otherwise contributes a cosmological-constant term when    g  g    is varied), this means that the corresponding action functional is stationary at values     (  g  ,  ω  )   (g,\omega)    of the spacetime metric and the principal    G  G   -connection, respectively, if and only if    g  g    satisfies the Einstein equation with the stress-energy tensor of the Yang-Mills field and    ω  \omega    satisfies the source-free Yang-Mills equation. 
 
 
 
 
  2.7  Gravity on the Frame Bundle? 

 
 We have seen that there exists a parallel between the equations of Einstein’s general relativity and those of Yang-Mills theories. Indeed, the field is determined by a field equation involving sources (the Einstein and Yang-Mills equations, respectively) and an integrability condition in the form of a Bianchi identity. This parallel is supported by theorems   2.5.2   and   2.6.15  , which interpret the Yang-Mills fields as pseudo-Riemannian metrics and indeed put source-free Yang-Mills theories in a framework that closely resembles that of general relativity. 
 
 
 Since there is a vast theory of the quantisation of Yang-Mills fields, one might want to do the opposite. Would it be possible to formulate the general theory of relativity as a gauge theory on a principal bundle? If so, this could help lead to a quantum theory of gravity. 
 
 
 Using the equivalence in corollary   1.1.12  , we have an obvious candidate for this principal bundle: the bundle of Lorentz frames (a principal     O  ⁡   (  1  ,  3  )    O(1,3)   -bundle  30    30 
               30 
              
              
              
            Perhaps it would be better to start with the Einstein-Cartan theory of gravity and construct the bundle of spin frames, which would be a     S  ​  L  ​   (  2  ,  ℂ  )    SL(2,\mathbb{C}\;)   -principal bundle.   ). Indeed, we can formulate general relativity on this bundle by interpreting tensors as equivariant maps from the bundle of Lorentz frames, using claim   1.1.14   (and noting that by section   1.4.4   each tensor bundle is associated with the bundle of Lorentz frames). Moreover, by claim   1.4.6   we can transfer the Levi-Civita connection to a unique principal connection on the bundle of Lorentz frames  31    31 
               31 
              
              
              
            In fact, this claim would let us transfer it to a unique principal connection on the bundle of all frames. However, it is easily seen that this restricts to a connection on the bundle of Lorentz frames, since the horizontal bundle is tangent to the bundle of Lorentz frames. (This property together with the demand that it has zero torsion even uniquely characterises the Levi-Civita connection.  [ 6 ] )   . Of course, one can write down the Einstein equation in this setting to obtain a principal bundle formulation of general relativity. 
 
 
 However, for unification purposes, this is not entirely satisfactory. Three of the four fundamental interactions in our current picture of nature can (modulo Higgs mechanisms in the case of the weak and electroweak interactions) be described by a Yang-Mills equation, but the equation for gravity has an entirely different form. Moreover, gravity appears in an entirely different way in the gauge theory than the other interactions. Indeed, it is not only described by the Levi-Civita connection (as a kind of gravitational potential) and its curvature (as a sort of field strength), but it is also present as the metric on the base space of the principal bundle, governing the geometry of spacetime. It is clear that the fundamental forces of nature are still far from being on an equal footing.

     Γ  ⁡   (   F   ⟶  π   M   )    \Gamma(F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}M)

      h  ¯   f   \bar{h}_{f}    
  
 
 
 In the case of a fibre bundle     F   ⟶  π   M   F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    with a connection, where    M  M    has a pseudo-Riemannian metric    g  g   , the inner product on     H  ​   F  f    HF_{f}    given by        h  ¯   f   ​   (  X  ,  Y  )    =    g   π  ⁡   (  f  )     ​   (    T  f   ​  π  ​   (  X  )    ,    T  f   ​  π  ​   (  Y  )    )     \bar{h}_{f}(X,Y)=g_{\pi(f)}(T_{f}\pi(X),T_{f}\pi(Y))    
   
 
      H  ​  F    ⟶     F   HF\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}F    
  
 
 
 Horizontal bundle of    F  F   , if     F   ⟶  π   M   F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}M    is a fibre bundle with a connection 
   
 
      h  ¯   ​   k  W    \bar{h}k_{W}    
  
 
 
 See page   1.3.2   
   
 
       J  k   ​   (  M  ,  N  )     ⟶     M   J^{k}(M,N)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}M

    K  K    
  
 
 
 Connector of some connection 
   
 
       J  k   ​  F    ⟶     M   J^{k}F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}M

     k  W   k_{W}    
  
 
 
 For a vector space    W  W    with a linear    G  G   -action, an invariant inner product 
   
 
    𝕜  \Bbbk    
  
 
 
 Denotes either    ℝ  \mathbb{R}    or    ℂ  \mathbb{C}\;    
   
 
     𝕜   p  ,  q    \Bbbk^{p,q}    
  
 
 
     𝕜  k   \Bbbk^{k}    with the standard     (  p  ,  q  )   (p,q)   -inner product 
   
 
    L  L    
  
 
 
 Left action of a Lie group on itself by left multiplication

     −   [  λ  ]    -[\lambda]

     ∂    L  i   /   ∂  ψ     {\partial L_{i}}/{\partial\psi}

      Ω  hor   ​    (  P  ,  W  )   G    \Omega_{\mathrm{hor}}(P,W)^{G}

     P  ​  B   PB    
  
 
 
 Category of principal bundles 
   
 
     P  ​   B  G    PB^{G}

     P  ​   B  M    PB_{M}

     P  H   P_{H}

     π      ∙  ×  𝕜     k      \pi_{\bullet\times\Bbbk^{k}}    
  
 
 
 Projection      T  ​   𝕜  k    ≅    𝕜  k   ×   𝕜  k     ⟶   π      ∙  ×  𝕜     k        𝕜  k    T\Bbbk^{k}\cong\Bbbk^{k}\times\Bbbk^{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{\bullet\times\Bbbk^{k}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Bbbk^{k}   ;      (  x  ,  y  )   ↦  y   (x,y)\mapsto y   , where the second     𝕜  k   \Bbbk^{k}    represents the tangent space 
   
 
     P  V   P_{V}

 Often denotes the principal right action on a principal fibre bundle.

     ρ  p   \rho^{p}

    Ric  \mathrm{Ric}

     Instructions for reporting errors 
     We are continuing to improve HTML versions of papers, and your feedback helps enhance accessibility and mobile
      support. To report errors in the HTML that will help us improve conversion and rendering, choose any of the
      methods listed below: 
     
       Click the "Report Issue"  ( 
             
           )  button, located in the page header. 
     
      Tip:  You can select the relevant text first, to include it in your report. 
     Our team has already identified  the following issues . We appreciate your time reviewing and reporting rendering errors we
      may not have found yet. Your efforts will help us improve the HTML versions for all readers, because disability
      should not be a barrier to accessing research. Thank you for your continued support in championing open access for
      all. 
     Have a free development cycle? Help support accessibility at arXiv! Our collaborators at LaTeXML maintain a  list of packages that need conversion , and welcome  developer contributions .
